\chapter{The Sherrington--Kirkpatrick Model at High Temperature: The Talagrand Theory}\label{chap:sg-talagrand}

This chapter develops Sections~1.1 through 1.9 of Michel Talagrand's \emph{Spin Glasses: A Challenge for Mathematicians} (Volume~I, Springer, 2010), with the scope qualifications below, and begins the higher-order cavity preparation of Section~1.10.
Section~1.6, including Proposition~1.6.8 (the cavity free-energy increment), is proved without any smallness assumption on the model parameters.
Of Section~1.7 Theorem~1.7.11 (the general Gaussian fluctuation principle) is formalized; Corollaries~1.7.13 and~1.7.15, Lemma~1.7.14, Lemma~1.7.5, Lemma~1.7.4, Lemma~1.7.6 and Theorem~1.7.1 are formalized as well (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:fluct}).
The mathematical narrative develops the high-temperature theory of the Sherrington--Kirkpatrick (SK) model:
\begin{enumerate}
\item The decoupled reference system, hypercube factorizations, exact first-moment calculations, and the first-moment upper bound on the free energy (Section~1.2).
\item Guerra's smart path Gaussian interpolation, the derivative formula with a negative square remainder, and Guerra's replica-symmetric (RS) upper bound minimized at the fixed-point equation $q = \mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta z \sqrt{q} + h)]$ (Section~1.3).
\item R.~Lata\l{}a's high-temperature overlap concentration: the general second-order replica derivative formula along Gaussian interpolations, the monotonicity of the compensated exponential functional, exponential overlap concentration $\nu(\exp s N (R_{1,2} - q)^2) \le (1 - 2s - 4\beta^2)^{-1/2}$ for $\beta^2 < 1/4$, and the Aizenman--Lebowitz--Ruelle (ALR) high-temperature free energy formula $|p_N(\beta, h) - \operatorname{RS}(q)| \le K/N$ at a solution $q$ of the self-consistency equation (Section~1.4).
\item Spin decorrelation, asymptotic product measures on finite spin marginals, and high-dimensional random projections (Sections~1.4--1.5).
\item The cavity method: splitting off the last spin, interpolating the new couplings against an independent Gaussian field, concentration of the overlap around $q$ with all its moments, and the free-energy increment $(N+1)p_{N+1}-Np_N$ (Section~1.6).
\item The Gaussian fluctuation principle: the Gaussian interpolation between the centered projections $N^{-1/2}\sum_i y_i\dot\sigma_i$ of the Gibbs state and independent Gaussians of variance $1-q$, the parity argument that makes the first $2k-1$ derivatives vanish at the start of the path, and the moment bounds that make the $2k$-th derivative $O(N^{-k})$ (Section~1.7).
\item Second moment computations (Section~1.8): the second-order expansion of the cavity path (Proposition~1.8.1), the rectangular combination at the decoupled endpoint (Lemma~1.8.4), and the asymptotic second moment of the rectangular overlap and the variance of the truncated spin correlation (Proposition~1.8.5, Corollary~1.8.6), with explicit $O(N^{-3/2})$ errors.
\item Beyond the Almeida--Thouless line (Section~1.9): Lemmas~1.9.3 and~1.9.4, Proposition~1.9.2 and Proposition~1.9.5 (nonzero field) are formalized for the SK model (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:beyond-at}); the coupled free energy and Proposition~1.9.7 (derivative separation) are formalized for the SK model with $h\ne0$, while Theorem~1.9.6 (non-differentiability of the limit) is formalized only conditionally on the existence of the limit as a hypothesis (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:thm-196}).
\item Higher overlap moments (Section~1.10): the product cavity expansions (1.298)--(1.299)
are proved for products of individual truncated centered overlaps; Lemma~1.10.2 and both
formulas of Corollary~1.10.3 hold for products of finite linear combinations
(Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:overlap-clt}). The joint $T$-basis mixed-moment theorem remains open.
\end{enumerate}

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Source tags.}
Each definition, lemma, theorem and remark below that has a counterpart in the book begins with a bold tag \textbf{[Talagrand, \dots]} giving the corresponding theorem, lemma, proposition or exercise number and the equation numbers of the book, written (1.$k$) for the displayed equations of Chapter~1 and (A.$k$) for the appendix. The tag says where the statement is found, not that the Lean statement is identical to it; deviations are listed in the remarks of each section. The section headings are this blueprint's own grouping: for example the Slepian lemma, the concentration inequality and the Guerra--Toninelli theorem (Proposition~1.3.3, Theorem~1.3.4 and Theorem~1.3.9 of the book) appear under the heading ``Section~1.1''. A tag reading ``no counterpart in the book'' marks an auxiliary Lean step.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Conventions and Notation.}
The spin configuration space is $\Sigma_N = \{-1, +1\}^N$, represented in Lean as functions $\sigma : \mathrm{Fin}\,N \to \mathbb{Z}^\times$ where $\mathbb{Z}^\times = \{-1, +1\}$.
The Hamiltonian with inverse temperature $\beta \in \mathbb{R}$ and external field $h \in \mathbb{R}$ is
\[
-H_N(\sigma) = \frac{\beta}{\sqrt{N}} \sum_{1 \le i < j \le N} g_{ij} \sigma_i \sigma_j + h \sum_{i \le N} \sigma_i,
\]
where the disorder $(g_{ij})_{1 \le i < j \le N}$ consists of $\binom{N}{2} = \frac{N(N-1)}{2}$ independent standard Gaussian variables $\mathcal{N}(0, 1)$ indexed by $\mathrm{FinPairs}\,N = \{p \in \mathrm{Fin}\,N \times \mathrm{Fin}\,N \mid p.1 < p.2\}$.
The partition function is $Z_N(\beta) = \sum_\sigma \exp(-H_N(\sigma))$, the expected free energy is $p_N(\beta) = N^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\log Z_N(\beta)]$, $\langle\cdot\rangle$ denotes the Gibbs average (for several replicas, the product of independent copies), $\nu(\cdot)=\mathbb{E}[\langle\cdot\rangle]$ its average over the disorder (Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:nu-notation}), and the overlap of two configurations is $R_{1,2} = R(\sigma^1, \sigma^2) = N^{-1}\sum_{i \le N} \sigma_i^1 \sigma_i^2$.

\section{The SK Hamiltonian and Foundations (Talagrand Section~1.1)}\label{sec:sg-tal:foundations}

In Section~1.1, the SK Hamiltonian is defined on the discrete hypercube $\Sigma_N$.
Its covariance structure, Gaussian comparison properties, concentration of measure, and the thermodynamic limit are foundational.

\begin{remark}[Differences between the Lean statements and the book in Section~1.1]\label{rem:sg-tal:dev-11}
The theorem statements below are those of the Lean declarations. They differ from the setting of the text as follows.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Slepian's lemma} is proved for abstract finite Gaussian families $(X_a,Y_a)_{a\in\alpha}$ on a probability space, with $X$ and $Y$ uncorrelated, not only for fields on $\Sigma_N$.
  Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:slepian} is the book's general Proposition~1.3.3 for a $C^2$ test function with exponentially bounded value, gradient and Hessian instead of a $C^\infty$ function of moderate growth (Eq.~(A.18)). The free-energy comparison, the instance $F=-\log\sum_ae^{x_a+h_a}$ with the bias $h$ as a separate argument, is Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:slepian-lse}. The Lean statements also ask that the two vectors be independent and jointly Gaussian (without loss of generality, since only the two covariance matrices matter). See Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:slepian-compare}.
\item \emph{Concentration} is proved for the mixed $p$-spin Hamiltonian with the constant $4\sum_p\beta_p^2$ in the exponent. The SK model is a special case, but the SK specialization is not stated as a separate Lean theorem.
\item \emph{The thermodynamic limit} is proved for the full-matrix Hamiltonian $N^{-1/2}\sigma\cdot(G\sigma)$ without external field, not for the pair disorder with a field $h$ used in Sections~1.2--1.4. The two disorders have different covariances ($NR^2$ versus $\frac N2(R^2-\frac1N)$), and no Lean statement relates the corresponding free energies; the high-temperature limit in Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:alr} is therefore derived directly and does not use this theorem.
\item \emph{Covariance.} The SK pair field has covariance $\frac{N\beta^2}{2}(R^2-\frac1N)$ (Lean: \texttt{integral\_sk\_pair\_field\_mul\_eq\_overlap}).
\end{itemize}
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Disorder pairs and Hamiltonian]\label{def:sg-tal:hamiltonian}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.FinPairs, SpinGlasses.card_finPairs, SpinGlasses.cast_card_finPairs}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.12)}
For $N \in \mathbb{N}$, the type of disordered pairs is $\mathrm{FinPairs}\,N = \{p : \mathrm{Fin}\,N \times \mathrm{Fin}\,N \mid p.1 < p.2\}$.
Its cardinality is $|\mathrm{FinPairs}\,N| = \binom{N}{2} = \frac{N(N-1)}{2}$.
For disorder $g : \mathrm{FinPairs}\,N \to \mathbb{R}$ and configuration $\sigma : \mathrm{Fin}\,N \to \mathbb{Z}^\times$, the Hamiltonian is
\[
-H_N(g, \sigma) = \frac{\beta}{\sqrt{N}} \sum_{p \in \mathrm{FinPairs}\,N} g_p \sigma_{p.1} \sigma_{p.2} + h \sum_{i \le N} \sigma_i.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Covariance of the SK Hamiltonian]\label{thm:sg-tal:covariance}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_sk_pair_field_mul_eq_overlap}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.4), (1.6); with $\beta$, Eq.~(1.63)}
Let $N\ge1$ and $\beta\in\mathbb R$. For any two configurations $\sigma^1, \sigma^2 \in \Sigma_N$, the Hamiltonian field has covariance
\[
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{N}} \sum_{i < j} g_{ij} \sigma_i^1 \sigma_j^1\right)\left(\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{N}} \sum_{i < j} g_{ij} \sigma_i^2 \sigma_j^2\right)\right] = \frac{N \beta^2}{2}\left(R(\sigma^1, \sigma^2)^2 - \frac{1}{N}\right).
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
By independence of the $g_{ij}$, the expectation is $\frac{\beta^2}{N} \sum_{i < j} \sigma_i^1 \sigma_j^1 \sigma_i^2 \sigma_j^2$.
Using $\sum_{i < j} x_i x_j = \frac{1}{2}((\sum_i x_i)^2 - \sum_i x_i^2)$ with $x_i = \sigma_i^1 \sigma_i^2$ (so $x_i^2 = 1$ and $\sum x_i = N R_{1,2}$),
the sum equals $\frac{1}{2}(N^2 R_{1,2}^2 - N) = \frac{N^2}{2}(R_{1,2}^2 - 1/N)$.
Multiplying by $\beta^2 / N$ gives $\frac{N\beta^2}{2}(R_{1,2}^2 - 1/N)$.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKReplicaSymmetricBound.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Derivative of a Gaussian expectation along the trigonometric path]\label{lem:sg-tal:path-derivative}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.fderiv_apply_eq_sum_mul, SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_comp_trig}
  \leanok
\talsource{Lemma~1.3.1, Eq.~(1.40), with the trigonometric path in place of (1.38)}
Let $\kappa$ be a finite set, $(\Omega,P)$ a probability space and $X,Y:\kappa\to\Omega\to\mathbb R$ random variables such that the combined family $(X_l,Y_l)_{l\in\kappa}$ (indexed by $\kappa\sqcup\kappa$)
is a centered Gaussian vector with $\mathbb{E}[X_lY_k]=0$ for all $l,k$; write $A_{lk}=\mathbb{E}[X_lX_k]$ and $B_{lk}=\mathbb{E}[Y_lY_k]$. Let $F\in C^2(\mathbb R^\kappa)$ have \emph{exponential growth of order $\le2$}: there are constants
$C_0,c_0,c_1,c_2$ and $D_l$, $D_{kl}$ with
\[
|F(y)|\le C_0e^{c_0\|y\|_1},\qquad |\partial_lF(y)|\le D_le^{c_1\|y\|_1},\qquad |\partial_k\partial_lF(y)|\le D_{kl}e^{c_2\|y\|_1}\qquad(y\in\mathbb R^\kappa),
\]
where $\|y\|_1=\sum_l|y_l|$. Put $Z_s=\cos s\,X+\sin s\,Y$. Then $s\mapsto\mathbb{E}F(Z_s)$ is differentiable on $\mathbb R$, with
\[
\frac{d}{ds}\mathbb{E}F(Z_s)\Big|_{s=t}=\sin t\cos t\sum_{l,k\in\kappa}(B_{lk}-A_{lk})\,\mathbb{E}\,\partial_k\partial_lF(Z_t).
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:sg:steinvec, lem:sg:expgrowth}
The path $Z_s$ replaces the book's $\sqrt t\,u+\sqrt{1-t}\,v$ of Eq.~(1.38) by $\cos s\,X+\sin s\,Y$: it has the same endpoints in the roles $v=X$ (at $s=0$) and $u=Y$ (at $s=\pi/2$), the covariance of $Z_s$ is $\cos^2s\,A+\sin^2s\,B$, and it is smooth on all of $\mathbb R$, so that no singular derivative has to be handled at the endpoints.
\emph{Step 1: differentiate under the expectation.} Pointwise, $\frac{d}{ds}F(Z_s)=\sum_lz_s(l)\,\partial_lF(Z_s)$ with $z_s(l)=-\sin s\,X_l+\cos s\,Y_l$ (chain rule, Eq.~(1.41)). The $\ell^1$ norm of $Z_s$ is at most that of $(X,Y)$, since $|\cos|,|\sin|\le1$,
and $|z_s(l)|\le\|(X,Y)\|_1\le e^{\|(X,Y)\|_1}$. Hence $|\sum_lz_s(l)\partial_lF(Z_s)|\le(\sum_lD_l)\,e^{(|c_1|+1)\|(X,Y)\|_1}$, a bound that does not depend on $s$ and is integrable, because a Gaussian vector has exponential moments of every order (\texttt{integrable\_comp\_of\_exp\_growth}). Dominated differentiation under a Gaussian expectation (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:expgrowth}(ii), explained in Remark~\ref{rem:sg:dominated-differentiation}; \texttt{hasDerivAt\_integral\_comp\_of\_exp\_growth}) gives $\frac{d}{ds}\mathbb{E}F(Z_s)=\sum_l\mathbb{E}[z_s(l)\,\partial_lF(Z_s)]$.
\emph{Step 2: Gaussian integration by parts.} The pair $(z_s,Z_s)$ is a centered jointly Gaussian family (a linear image of $(X,Y)$), and, because $\mathbb{E}[X_lY_k]=0$,
\[
\mathbb{E}[z_s(l)Z_s(k)]=-\sin s\cos s\,A_{lk}+\cos s\sin s\,B_{lk}=\sin s\cos s\,(B_{lk}-A_{lk}).
\]
Gaussian integration by parts (Eq.~(A.17), Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:steinvec}) applied to the function $\partial_lF$ gives $\mathbb{E}[z_s(l)\,\partial_lF(Z_s)]=\sum_k\mathbb{E}[z_s(l)Z_s(k)]\,\mathbb{E}\,\partial_k\partial_lF(Z_s)$.
Summing over $l$ gives the formula. The growth bounds on $\partial_lF$ and $\partial_k\partial_lF$ are what the integration by parts needs, to see that all expectations exist.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/GaussianCovarianceInterpolation.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Slepian's lemma]\label{thm:sg-tal:slepian}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_comp_le_of_covariance_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:sg-tal:path-derivative}
\talsource{Proposition~1.3.3 (Slepian's lemma)}
Let $\kappa$ be a finite set, $(\Omega,P)$ a probability space and $X,Y:\kappa\to\Omega\to\mathbb R$ random variables such that $(X_l,Y_l)_{l\in\kappa}$ is a centered Gaussian vector with
$\mathbb{E}[X_lY_k]=0$ for all $l,k$ (that is, $X$ and $Y$ are independent centered Gaussian vectors). Assume
\begin{itemize}
\item $\mathbb{E}[X_l^2]=\mathbb{E}[Y_l^2]$ for all $l$, and $\mathbb{E}[X_lX_k]\le\mathbb{E}[Y_lY_k]$ for all $l\ne k$;
\item $F\in C^2(\mathbb R^\kappa)$ has exponential growth of order $\le2$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:path-derivative}) and $\partial_k\partial_lF\ge0$ for all $l\ne k$.
\end{itemize}
Then $\mathbb{E}F(X)\le\mathbb{E}F(Y)$. In the notation of the book, $u=Y$, $v=X$: if $\mathbb{E}u_i^2=\mathbb{E}v_i^2$, $\mathbb{E}u_iu_j\ge\mathbb{E}v_iv_j$ for $i\ne j$ and $\partial_i\partial_jF\ge0$ for $i\ne j$, then $\mathbb{E}F(u)\ge\mathbb{E}F(v)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\emph{Idea.} We do not compare $\mathbb{E}F(Y)$ and $\mathbb{E}F(X)$ directly. Instead we move continuously from the vector $X$ to the vector $Y$ along a path of Gaussian vectors, and show that the expectation of $F$ never decreases along the path. The covariance matrix of the vector at time $s$ is a mixture of $A$ and $B$, and the assumptions on $A$, $B$ and on the mixed second derivatives of $F$ are exactly what makes $\mathbb{E}F$ increase.

\emph{Notation.} Write $A_{lk}=\mathbb{E}[X_lX_k]$ and $B_{lk}=\mathbb{E}[Y_lY_k]$ (deterministic $\kappa\times\kappa$ matrices), and, for $s\in\mathbb R$,
\[
Z_s=\cos s\,X+\sin s\,Y,\qquad z_s=\frac{d}{ds}Z_s=-\sin s\,X+\cos s\,Y,\qquad \varphi(s)=\mathbb{E}F(Z_s).
\]

\textbf{Step 1: the two sides of the claim are the endpoints of the path.}
Since $\cos0=1$, $\sin0=0$, $\cos\frac\pi2=0$ and $\sin\frac\pi2=1$ we have $Z_0=X$ and $Z_{\pi/2}=Y$, hence
\[
\varphi(0)=\mathbb{E}F(X),\qquad \varphi(\tfrac\pi2)=\mathbb{E}F(Y).
\]
The inequality to be proved is therefore $\varphi(0)\le\varphi(\frac\pi2)$. The trigonometric path is used instead of the book's $\sqrt t\,u+\sqrt{1-t}\,v$ because it is smooth on all of $\mathbb R$ (no singular derivative at the endpoints), and it keeps the covariance of $Z_s$ equal to $\cos^2s\,A+\sin^2s\,B$ (Step 2, using $\mathbb{E}[XY]=0$), which interpolates between $A$ and $B$.

\textbf{Step 2: the vector is Gaussian along the whole path, and the covariances needed are computed.}
Because $(X,Y)$ is jointly Gaussian (Remark~\ref{rem:sg:hxy}), each of $Z_s$ and $z_s$ is a linear image of $(X,Y)$, so the pair $(z_s,Z_s)$ is again a centered jointly Gaussian family (\texttt{hasGaussianLaw\_sumElim\_lincomb}; centered by \texttt{integral\_lincomb\_eq\_zero}).
Expanding the product and using $\mathbb{E}[X_lY_k]=0$ for all $l,k$, so that every mixed term vanishes (\texttt{integral\_lincomb\_mul\_lincomb}), we get the two covariances
\[
\mathbb{E}[Z_s(l)Z_s(k)]=\cos^2s\,A_{lk}+\sin^2s\,B_{lk},
\qquad
\mathbb{E}[z_s(l)\,Z_s(k)]=-\sin s\cos s\,A_{lk}+\cos s\sin s\,B_{lk}=\sin s\cos s\,(B_{lk}-A_{lk}).
\]
The cross covariance $\mathbb{E}[z_s(l)Z_s(k)]$ is the quantity that appears in Gaussian integration by parts, and it is proportional to $B-A$.

\textbf{Step 3: $\varphi$ is differentiable, with derivative $\sum_l\mathbb{E}[z_s(l)\,\partial_lF(Z_s)]$.}
Pointwise in $\omega$, the chain rule gives $\frac{d}{ds}F(Z_s)=\sum_lz_s(l)\,\partial_lF(Z_s)$ (Eq.~(1.41) of the book). To differentiate under the expectation we need an integrable bound that is uniform in $s$.
Since $|\cos s|,|\sin s|\le1$, we have $\|Z_s\|_1\le\|(X,Y)\|_1$ and $|z_s(l)|\le\|(X,Y)\|_1\le e^{\|(X,Y)\|_1}$. The growth assumption $|\partial_lF(y)|\le D_le^{c_1\|y\|_1}$ then gives
\[
\Big|\sum_lz_s(l)\,\partial_lF(Z_s)\Big|\le\Big(\sum_lD_l\Big)e^{(|c_1|+1)\|(X,Y)\|_1},
\]
and the right side does not depend on $s$ and is integrable, because a Gaussian vector has exponential moments of every order (\texttt{integrable\_comp\_of\_exp\_growth}). The same growth assumption on $F$ shows that $F(Z_s)$ is integrable. By dominated differentiation under a Gaussian expectation (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:expgrowth}(ii) and Remark~\ref{rem:sg:dominated-differentiation}; \texttt{hasDerivAt\_integral\_comp\_of\_exp\_growth}),
\[
\varphi'(s)=\sum_l\mathbb{E}\big[z_s(l)\,\partial_lF(Z_s)\big]\qquad\text{for every }s\in\mathbb R.
\]
In particular $\varphi$ is differentiable, hence continuous, on all of $\mathbb R$.

\textbf{Step 4: Gaussian integration by parts evaluates the derivative.}
Gaussian integration by parts (Eq.~(A.17) of the book, Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:steinvec}) says: if $(W,V)$ is a centered jointly Gaussian family, $G$ is differentiable with exponentially bounded value and partial derivatives, then
$\mathbb{E}[W\,G(V)]=\sum_k\mathbb{E}[WV_k]\,\mathbb{E}\,\partial_kG(V)$. We apply it with $W=z_s(l)$, $V=Z_s$ and $G=\partial_lF$, whose value and first partial derivatives $\partial_k\partial_lF$ are exponentially bounded by assumption. With the cross covariance from Step 2,
\[
\mathbb{E}\big[z_s(l)\,\partial_lF(Z_s)\big]=\sin s\cos s\sum_k(B_{lk}-A_{lk})\,\mathbb{E}\,\partial_k\partial_lF(Z_s),
\]
and summing over $l$,
\[
\varphi'(s)=\sin s\cos s\sum_{l,k}(B_{lk}-A_{lk})\,\mathbb{E}\,\partial_k\partial_lF(Z_s).
\]
This is Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:path-derivative} (\texttt{hasDerivAt\_integral\_comp\_trig}), the analogue of Eq.~(1.40) of the book for the trigonometric path.
Note that $\mathbb{E}[z_s(l)\,\partial_lF(Z_s)]$ would be hard to bound directly, since $z_s$ is correlated with $Z_s$ and hence with $\partial_lF(Z_s)$; integration by parts replaces it by an expression involving only the deterministic covariances $A$ and $B$ and the second derivatives of $F$.

\textbf{Step 5: the derivative is nonnegative on $[0,\pi/2]$.} Use the assumptions term by term.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Equal variances} $A_{ll}=B_{ll}$: the diagonal terms $l=k$ of the double sum vanish, since they carry the factor $B_{ll}-A_{ll}=0$. This is why no sign condition on the pure second derivatives $\partial_l^2F$ is needed.
\item \emph{Covariance order} $A_{lk}\le B_{lk}$ for $l\ne k$ and \emph{mixed derivatives} $\partial_k\partial_lF\ge0$ for $l\ne k$: each off-diagonal term is the product $(B_{lk}-A_{lk})\cdot\mathbb{E}\,\partial_k\partial_lF(Z_s)$ of two nonnegative factors, because the expectation of a pointwise nonnegative function is nonnegative.
\item \emph{The path factor:} for $s\in[0,\pi/2]$ both $\sin s\ge0$ and $\cos s\ge0$, so $\sin s\cos s\ge0$.
\end{itemize}
Hence, for $s\in[0,\pi/2]$,
\[
\varphi'(s)=\sin s\cos s\sum_{l\ne k}(B_{lk}-A_{lk})\,\mathbb{E}\,\partial_k\partial_lF(Z_s)\;\ge\;0.
\]
(Outside $[0,\pi/2]$ the sign of $\sin s\cos s$ can be negative, which is why the argument is restricted to this interval; only the endpoints $0$ and $\pi/2$ are needed.)

\textbf{Step 6: a nonnegative derivative gives monotonicity, hence the inequality.}
The function $\varphi$ is continuous on $[0,\pi/2]$ and differentiable on the interior with $\varphi'\ge0$ (Steps 3--5). By the mean value theorem, for any $0\le s<t\le\pi/2$ there is $\xi\in(s,t)$ with
\[
\varphi(t)-\varphi(s)=\varphi'(\xi)\,(t-s)\ge0,
\]
because $\varphi'(\xi)\ge0$ and $t-s>0$. So $\varphi$ is non-decreasing on $[0,\pi/2]$ (\texttt{monotoneOn\_of\_deriv\_nonneg}). Taking $s=0$ and $t=\pi/2$ gives $\varphi(0)\le\varphi(\frac\pi2)$, which by Step 1 is
\[
\mathbb{E}F(X)\le\mathbb{E}F(Y).
\]
In words: if the mixed second derivatives of $F$ are nonnegative, then $F$ is ``supermodular'', it rewards coordinates that move together, so a vector whose coordinates are more strongly correlated (larger $B$ against $A$, same variances) has the larger expectation of $F$.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/GaussianCovarianceInterpolation.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Gaussian comparison for the free energy]\label{thm:sg-tal:slepian-lse}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_log_sum_exp_le_of_covariance_le, SpinGlasses.sum_exp_add_pos,
        SpinGlasses.fderiv_log_sum_exp_single, SpinGlasses.fderiv_fderiv_log_sum_exp_single,
        SpinGlasses.hasFDerivAt_sum_exp_add,
        SpinGlasses.sum_abs_cos_mul_add_sin_mul_le,
        SpinGlasses.abs_ite_one_sub_le_one,
        Real.add_le_add_one_mul_exp}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:sg-tal:slepian}
\talsource{Proposition~1.3.3 (Slepian's lemma) for the function $F=-\log\sum_ae^{x_a+h_a}$; no counterpart in the book as a separate statement}
Let $\alpha$ be a finite nonempty set, $(\Omega,P)$ a probability space, and $X,Y:\alpha\to\Omega\to\mathbb R$ such that $(X_a,Y_a)_{a\in\alpha}$ is a centered Gaussian vector with $\mathbb{E}[X_aY_b]=0$ for all $a,b$, $\mathbb{E}[X_a^2] = \mathbb{E}[Y_a^2]$ and $\mathbb{E}[X_aX_b] \le \mathbb{E}[Y_aY_b]$ for all $a,b$.
Then for any bias $h:\alpha\to\mathbb R$,
\[
\mathbb{E}\Big[\log \sum_a \exp(Y_a + h_a)\Big] \le \mathbb{E}\Big[\log \sum_a \exp(X_a + h_a)\Big].
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\emph{Idea.} The statement is Slepian's lemma (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:slepian}) for one particular test function, the negative free energy. All the work is in checking the hypotheses on the test function: smoothness, growth, and the sign of the mixed second derivatives.
Put $Z(x)=\sum_be^{x_b+h_b}>0$, $F(x)=-\log Z(x)$ and let $w_a(x)=e^{x_a+h_a}/Z(x)$ be the Gibbs weights; they lie in $[0,1]$ (\texttt{gibbsWeight\_mem\_Icc}). We check the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:slepian}.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Smoothness.} $x\mapsto Z(x)$ is $C^\infty$ and positive, and the logarithm is $C^\infty$ on $(0,\infty)$, so $F\in C^\infty\subseteq C^2$.
\item \emph{Gradient.} By the chain rule, $\partial_a\log Z=\partial_aZ/Z=e^{x_a+h_a}/Z=w_a$, that is, $\partial_aF=-w_a$ (\texttt{fderiv\_log\_sum\_exp\_single}). Hence $|\partial_aF|=w_a\le1$.
\item \emph{Hessian.} Write $w_l=e^{x_l+h_l}\,Z^{-1}$ and differentiate in the direction $k$ by the product rule: $\partial_ke^{x_l+h_l}=\mathbf 1[l=k]\,e^{x_l+h_l}$ and $\partial_kZ^{-1}=-Z^{-2}e^{x_k+h_k}$. Therefore
  \[
  \partial_k\partial_l\log Z=\partial_kw_l=\mathbf 1[l=k]\,w_l-w_lw_k=w_l\big(\mathbf 1[l=k]-w_k\big),
  \]
  that is, the Hessian of $\log Z$ is $\mathrm{diag}(w)-ww^{\top}$ (\texttt{fderiv\_fderiv\_log\_sum\_exp\_single}), and $\partial_k\partial_lF=w_lw_k-\mathbf 1[l=k]\,w_l$. Its absolute value is at most $1$, because $w_l\in[0,1]$ and $|\mathbf 1[l=k]-w_k|\le1$.
  \emph{The sign condition:} for $l\ne k$ the indicator vanishes and $\partial_k\partial_lF=w_lw_k\ge0$. The pure second derivatives $\partial_l^2F=w_l^2-w_l\le0$ have no sign condition imposed on them, and this is fine, because the diagonal terms drop out of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:slepian}.
  Note that the mixed derivatives of $\log Z$ itself are $-w_lw_k\le0$, the \emph{opposite} sign to the book's hypothesis. This is why the comparison is for $F=-\log Z$, and why the field with \emph{larger} correlations has the \emph{smaller} expected free energy.
\item \emph{Growth.} By the Lipschitz estimate for the free energy, $|\log\sum_ae^{x_a+h_a}|\le|\log\sum_ae^{h_a}|+\|x\|_1$ (\texttt{SpinGlasses.abs\_log\_sum\_exp\_le}, proved in the course of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:expmoment}), and $\|x\|_1\le e^{\|x\|_1}$ and $1\le e^{\|x\|_1}$, we get $|F(x)|\le(|\log\sum_ae^{h_a}|+1)\,e^{\|x\|_1}$. The gradient and Hessian are bounded by $1=1\cdot e^{0\cdot\|x\|_1}$. So $F$ has exponential growth of order $\le2$.
\end{itemize}
The covariance hypotheses are those of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:slepian}: equal variances on the diagonal, and $\mathbb{E}[X_aX_b]\le\mathbb{E}[Y_aY_b]$ for all $a,b$, which contains the condition for $a\ne b$. Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:slepian} gives $\mathbb{E}F(X)\le\mathbb{E}F(Y)$, that is, $-\mathbb{E}\log Z(X)\le-\mathbb{E}\log Z(Y)$, which is
$\mathbb{E}\log\sum_ae^{Y_a+h_a}\le\mathbb{E}\log\sum_ae^{X_a+h_a}$.

\emph{Sanity check.} Let $\alpha=\{1,2\}$, $X_1,X_2$ independent standard Gaussians and $Y_1=Y_2=Z$ standard Gaussian, with $Z$ independent of $X$. The hypotheses hold ($A=I$, $B=\begin{psmallmatrix}1&1\\1&1\end{psmallmatrix}$, so $B-A\ge0$ entrywise with zero diagonal). The left side is $\mathbb{E}[Z]+\log(e^{h_1}+e^{h_2})=\log(e^{h_1}+e^{h_2})$ and the right side
is $\mathbb{E}\log(e^{X_1+h_1}+e^{X_2+h_2})$, which is at least $\log(e^{h_1}+e^{h_2})$ by Jensen's inequality for the convex function $x\mapsto\log\sum_ae^{x_a+h_a}$ and $\mathbb{E}X=0$. This agrees with the theorem.
This is the form of Slepian's lemma used in the Guerra--Toninelli theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:gt}).
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/GaussianCovarianceInterpolation.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Comparison with the book's Proposition~1.3.3]\label{rem:sg-tal:slepian-compare}
The book states Slepian's lemma for a general test function: if $F:\mathbb R^M\to\mathbb R$ is infinitely differentiable with all partial derivatives of moderate growth (Eq.~(A.18): $F(x)e^{-a|x|^2}\to0$ for every $a>0$) and $\partial_i\partial_jF\ge0$ for all $i\ne j$, and $u,v$ are
centered jointly Gaussian vectors with $\mathbb{E}u_i^2=\mathbb{E}v_i^2$ for all $i$ and $\mathbb{E}u_iu_j\ge\mathbb{E}v_iv_j$ for all $i\ne j$, then $\mathbb{E}F(u)\ge\mathbb{E}F(v)$.
Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:slepian} is this statement with the following differences.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Regularity and growth.} The book asks $F\in C^\infty$ with all derivatives of moderate growth. Lean asks $F\in C^2$ (only derivatives up to order $2$ occur in the proof) with $F$ and its first and second partial derivatives bounded by $Ce^{c\|y\|_1}$. This is a restriction: functions that grow like $e^{a|y|^2}$ with small $a>0$ have moderate growth in the book's sense but are excluded. Polynomials, bounded functions and everything of exponential type are included. The restriction exists only because the Lean integration by parts is proved under exponential growth. The log-sum-exp function of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:slepian-lse} satisfies it.
\item \emph{Independence.} Lean assumes $(X,Y)$ jointly Gaussian and centered with $\mathbb{E}[X_aY_b]=0$, that is, $X$ and $Y$ independent Gaussian vectors. This is not a restriction, since the conclusion depends only on the two covariance matrices,
  and any two centered Gaussian laws can be realised as independent vectors on a product space; the book makes the same reduction before Eq.~(1.38), noting that one may assume $u$ and $v$ independent ``since we are comparing a function of the law of $u$ with a function of the law of $v$''. In the proof the independence is what makes the covariance of the interpolating vector $\cos^2s\,A+\sin^2s\,B$. The Lean theorem takes such a realisation as given.
\item \emph{Roles.} The Lean names are $v=X$ and $u=Y$, and the conclusion is written $\mathbb{E}F(X)\le\mathbb{E}F(Y)$. The covariance condition is stated for $l\ne k$ only (the diagonal is the equal-variance hypothesis).
\item \emph{The path.} The proof uses the path $\cos s\,X+\sin s\,Y$ in place of $\sqrt t\,u+\sqrt{1-t}\,v$, with the same endpoints; see Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:path-derivative}.
\end{itemize}
The free-energy statement of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:slepian-lse} is the instance $F=-\log\sum_ae^{x_a+h_a}$: the mixed derivatives of $\log\sum e^{x}$ are $\le0$, so the book's lemma applies to its negative and the field with larger correlations has the smaller expected free energy.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Concentration of the free energy]\label{thm:sg-tal:concentration}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.mixedPSpin_free_energy_concentration}
  \leanok
\talsource{Theorem~1.3.4, Eq.~(1.47); Proposition~1.3.5, Eq.~(1.53); the SK instance is Eq.~(1.54)}
Lean states this for the mixed $p$-spin Hamiltonian; the full-matrix SK model is the case $\beta_2=\beta$, $\beta_p=0$ otherwise, with $\sum_p\beta_p^2=\beta^2$. Let $\iota$ be a finite nonempty set of size $N$, $(\beta_p)_{p\ge0}$ real with $\beta_0=0$ and $\sum_p|\beta_p|<\infty$, and
\[
H(g,\sigma)=\sum_{p\ge0}\beta_p\,N^{-(p-1)/2}\sum_{i_1,\dots,i_p}g^{(p)}_{i_1\dots i_p}\sigma_{i_1}\cdots\sigma_{i_p}
\]
with independent standard Gaussian disorder $g$. For $F_N=N^{-1}\log\sum_\sigma e^{H(g,\sigma)}$ and every $x\ge0$,
\[
\mathbb{P}\left(\left|F_N - \mathbb{E}[F_N]\right| \ge x\right) \le 2 \exp\left(-\frac{N x^2}{4\sum_p\beta_p^2}\right).
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The free energy $F_N$ is a Lipschitz function of the Gaussian disorder (the variance of $H$ at every hypercube point is $N\sum_p\beta_p^2$), so it is sub-Gaussian with variance proxy $2\sum_p\beta_p^2/N$ by the Gaussian concentration inequality for log-sum-exp; both tails give the displayed bound.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/Concentration.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The thermodynamic limit: Guerra--Toninelli theorem]\label{thm:sg-tal:gt}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_log_sum_exp_add_le, SpinGlasses.sk_tendsto_free_energy}
  \leanok
\talsource{Theorem~1.3.9, Eqs.~(1.77)--(1.78)}
Lean proves this for the full-matrix SK Hamiltonian without external field, $H_N(G,\sigma)=N^{-1/2}\,\sigma\cdot(G\sigma)$ with $G$ an $N\times N$ matrix of independent standard Gaussians, and $F_N(\beta)=N^{-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\log\sum_\sigma\exp(\beta H_N(G,\sigma))\big]$.
\emph{Superadditivity (abstract form).} If $\xi$ is convex on $[-1,1]$ and $H_N$ are centered Gaussian fields on $\Sigma_N$ with $\mathbb{E}[H_N(\sigma)H_N(\tau)]=N\xi(R(\sigma,\tau))$, then for $N,M\ge1$
\[
\mathbb{E}\Big[\log\sum_\sigma e^{H_N(\sigma)}\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\log\sum_\sigma e^{H_M(\sigma)}\Big]\le \mathbb{E}\Big[\log\sum_\sigma e^{H_{N+M}(\sigma)}\Big].
\]
\emph{Limit.} For every $\beta\in\mathbb R$,
\[
F_N(\beta)\longrightarrow \sup_{N\ge1}F_N(\beta)\qquad(N\to\infty).
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
We first prove the abstract superadditivity statement.  Work on the product probability space carrying three independent disorders of sizes $N+M$, $N$, and $M$.  Index the two fields by pairs $a=(\rho,\tau)\in\Sigma_N\times\Sigma_M$, and set
\[
 X_a=H_{N+M}(\rho\frown\tau),\qquad
 Y_a=H_N(\rho)+H_M(\tau).
\]
The three fields are jointly Gaussian on this product space; $X$ and $Y$ are centered and have zero cross-covariance.  For $b=(\rho',\tau')$, write
\[
 r=R_N(\rho,\rho'),\qquad s=R_M(\tau,\tau').
\]
The overlap of concatenated configurations is
\[
 R_{N+M}(\rho\frown\tau,\rho'\frown\tau')=
 \frac{Nr+Ms}{N+M}.
\]
Consequently the covariance identities are
\[
 \operatorname{Cov}(X_a,X_b)=(N+M)\xi\!\left(\frac{Nr+Ms}{N+M}\right),
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Cov}(Y_a,Y_b)=N\xi(r)+M\xi(s).
\]
Here $r,s\in[-1,1]$, so convexity of $\xi$ gives $\operatorname{Cov}(X_a,X_b)\leq\operatorname{Cov}(Y_a,Y_b)$.  On the diagonal $r=s=1$, hence the two variances agree.  The Gaussian comparison theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:comparison}), with zero bias, now yields
\[
 \mathbb E\log\sum_{\rho,\tau}e^{H_N(\rho)+H_M(\tau)}
 \leq
 \mathbb E\log\sum_{\rho,\tau}e^{H_{N+M}(\rho\frown\tau)}.
\]
The left partition function factorizes as $Z_NZ_M$, while concatenation is a bijection
$\Sigma_N\times\Sigma_M\simeq\Sigma_{N+M}$ on the right.  Since the log-partition functions are integrable Gaussian finite sums, integrating with respect to the product measure gives
\[
 u_N+u_M\leq u_{N+M},\qquad u_K:=\mathbb E\log Z_K.
\]

It remains to justify the boundedness hypothesis in Fekete's lemma, rather than assuming it.  Jensen's inequality and the Gaussian moment-generating formula give, for every $K\geq1$,
\[
 u_K\leq \log\mathbb E Z_K
 =\log\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_K}\exp\!\left(\frac{\operatorname{Var}(H_K(\sigma))}{2}\right)
 =K\left(\log2+\frac{\xi(1)}2\right),
\]
because $|\Sigma_K|=2^K$ and $\operatorname{Var}(H_K(\sigma))=K\xi(1)$.  Thus $u_K/K$ is bounded above.  With $u_0=0$, Fekete's lemma (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:fekete}) proves
\[
 \frac{u_K}{K}\longrightarrow\sup_{K\geq1}\frac{u_K}{K}.
\]
Finally, for the full-matrix SK Hamiltonian the covariance is $N R^2$.  Replacing $H_N$ by $\beta H_N$ replaces $\xi(x)=x^2$ by $\beta^2x^2$, still convex on $[-1,1]$, so the preceding abstract result is precisely the displayed convergence of $F_N(\beta)$.

For the conceptual role of these steps and their later uses, see Remark~\ref{rem:sg:gt-intuition}.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/GuerraToninelli.lean}.
\end{proof}

\section{Decoupled Systems, Hypercube Factorization and First Moments (Talagrand Section~1.2)}\label{sec:sg-tal:decoupled}

Section~1.2 of Talagrand establishes the non-interacting baseline: product factorization over the hypercube, local magnetizations under decoupled fields, exact calculation of $\mathbb{E}[Z_N]$, the Jensen upper bound, and the temperature derivative of the free energy.

\begin{remark}[Differences between the Lean statements and the book in Section~1.2]\label{rem:sg-tal:dev-12}
\begin{itemize}
\item The decoupled results are stated for an arbitrary finite index type $\iota$ (with $N=|\iota|$), not only $\mathrm{Fin}\,N$. The decoupled free energy requires the fields $Y_i$ to be identically distributed and $\log\cosh Y_{i_0}$ integrable, which is implicit in the book.
\item Jensen's bound and the $\beta$-derivative carry explicit integrability hypotheses on $\log Z_N$ (the book does not mention them).
\item The Lean $\beta$-derivative theorem ends at $N^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\langle u_\sigma\rangle]$. The evaluation in Eq.~(1.84), $\frac{\beta}{2}(1-\mathbb{E}[\langle R_{1,2}^2\rangle])$, obtained by Gaussian integration by parts, is not formalized, and no later theorem uses it.
\end{itemize}
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Hypercube product factorization]\label{lem:sg-tal:hypercube-prod}
  \lean{sum_units_int,
        hypercube_sum_prod_eq_prod_add,
        hypercube_sum_prod_erase_eq_prod_add}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.30)}
For a finite index set $\iota$, a commutative semiring $R$ and a family $a : \iota \to \mathbb{Z}^\times \to R$,
\[
\sum_{\sigma : \iota \to \mathbb{Z}^\times} \prod_{i \in \iota} a(i, \sigma_i) = \prod_{i \in \iota} (a(i, 1) + a(i, -1)),
\]
and, singling out a coordinate $j \in \iota$,
\[
\sum_{\sigma : \iota \to \mathbb{Z}^\times} \prod_{i \in \iota} a(i, \sigma_i) = (a(j, 1) + a(j, -1)) \prod_{i \neq j} (a(i, 1) + a(i, -1)).
\]
Also $\sum_{s\in\mathbb Z^\times}f(s)=f(1)+f(-1)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Interchange of sum and product over the finite product set (\texttt{Finset.prod\_univ\_sum}); the second identity splits off the factor $j$.
Formalized in \texttt{ForMathlib/Algebra/BigOperators/HypercubeProduct.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Decoupled partition function and local observables]\label{thm:sg-tal:decoupled-partition}
  \lean{Real.continuous_tanh,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_tanh_sq,
        SpinGlasses.decoupled_partition_function_eq,
        SpinGlasses.decoupled_partition_function_eq_mul_erase,
        SpinGlasses.decoupled_sum_spin_mul_exp_eq,
        SpinGlasses.decoupled_gibbs_spin_eq,
        SpinGlasses.decoupled_gibbs_replica_two_eq,
        SpinGlasses.decoupled_free_energy_eq}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.29)--(1.36): partition function (1.32), free energy (1.33), local averages (1.34)--(1.36)}
For the decoupled cavity-free Hamiltonian $-H_0(\sigma) = \sum_{i \le N} \sigma_i Y_i$, the partition function factorizes as
\[
Z_0 = 2^N \prod_{i=1}^N \cosh Y_i.
\]
Splitting off the coordinate $j$,
\[
Z_0 = 2\cosh Y_j \prod_{i\neq j} 2\cosh Y_i,
\qquad
\sum_{\sigma}\sigma_j \exp\Big(\sum_i \sigma_i Y_i\Big) = 2\sinh Y_j \prod_{i\neq j} 2\cosh Y_i .
\]
The decoupled Gibbs expectation of a single spin satisfies $\langle \sigma_j \rangle_0 = \tanh Y_j$,
the two-replica overlap satisfies $\langle \sigma_j^1 \sigma_j^2 \rangle_0 = \tanh^2 Y_j$,
and, if the $Y_i$ are random variables with the same law and $\log\cosh Y_{i_0}$ is integrable, the decoupled free energy is
\[
\frac1N\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\log\sum_\sigma\exp\Big(\sum_i\sigma_iY_i\Big)\Big] = \log 2 + \mathbb{E}\big[\log\cosh Y_{i_0}\big].
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:hypercube-prod} with $a(i, \pm 1) = \exp(\pm Y_i)$ and use $\exp(Y) + \exp(-Y) = 2\cosh Y$ and $\exp(Y) - \exp(-Y) = 2\sinh Y$.
For the single-spin sum take $a(j,s)=s\,e^{sY_j}$ and $a(i,s)=e^{sY_i}$ for $i\ne j$; the ratio of the two sums is $\tanh Y_j$.
The integrability of $\tanh^2(Y)$ for any a.e.\ measurable $Y$ (it is bounded by $1$) is recorded separately because every expectation below uses it.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKDecoupled.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Overlaps of the decoupled model]\label{thm:sg-tal:decoupled-overlap}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.decoupled_gibbs_overlap_eq,
        SpinGlasses.decoupled_replica_overlap_eq}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.34)--(1.36); at a fixed point, Eq.~(1.104)}
Let $\iota$ be a finite nonempty index set of size $N$, $Y:\iota\to\mathbb R$ and let $\langle\cdot\rangle_0$ be the Gibbs average for $-H_0(\sigma)=\sum_i\sigma_iY_i$.
For two independent replicas the mean overlap is
\[
\Big\langle \frac1N\sum_{j}\sigma^1_j\sigma^2_j\Big\rangle_0=\frac1N\sum_{j}\tanh^2 Y_j .
\]
If moreover $Y_j$ are random variables on a probability space with the same law for every $j$, then
\[
\mathbb{E}[\frac1N\sum_{j}\tanh^2 Y_j]=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2 Y_{j_0}],
\]
which for $Y_j=\beta z_j\sqrt q+h$ is the right-hand side $\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta z\sqrt q+h)]$ of the self-consistency equation (Eq.~(1.74)): at a fixed point $q$ the decoupled model has expected overlap exactly $q$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Linearity of the integral over the product of the two (identical) Gibbs measures reduces the first claim to the single-site identity $\langle\sigma^1_j\sigma^2_j\rangle_0=\langle\sigma_j\rangle_0^2=\tanh^2Y_j$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:decoupled-partition}.
The second claim is linearity of the expectation together with $\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2Y_j]=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2Y_{j_0}]$, which follows from identical distribution.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKDecoupled.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact first moment of the SK partition function]\label{thm:sg-tal:first-moment}
  \lean{integral_exp_mul_gaussianReal_zero_one,
        integrable_exp_sum_mul_gaussianReal,
        integral_exp_sum_mul_gaussianReal,
        SpinGlasses.sk_first_moment_term_sq,
        SpinGlasses.sum_sk_first_moment_term_sq,
        SpinGlasses.sum_exp_h_mul_spin,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_partition_function,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_partition_function_eq}
  \leanok
\talsource{Exercise~1.2.3, Eq.~(1.25)}
For the SK model with Gaussian disorder $g_p \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)$ on pairs $1 \le i < j \le N$ and constant external field $h \in \mathbb{R}$,
the exact first moment of the partition function is
\[
\mathbb{E}[Z_N] = (2\cosh h)^N \exp\left(N \frac{\beta^2}{4}\left(1 - \frac{1}{N}\right)\right),
\]
which yields the exact normalized log-moment (Talagrand Exercise~1.2.3, Eq.~(1.25)):
\[
\frac{1}{N} \log \mathbb{E}[Z_N] = \frac{\beta^2}{4}\left(1 - \frac{1}{N}\right) + \log 2 + \log\cosh h.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
For each configuration $\sigma$, the Gaussian linear combination $\sum_{i<j} \frac{\beta}{\sqrt{N}} g_{ij} \sigma_i \sigma_j$ has variance $\sum_{i<j} \frac{\beta^2}{N} = \binom{N}{2}\frac{\beta^2}{N} = N \frac{\beta^2}{4}(1 - \frac{1}{N})$, independent of $\sigma$.
Integrating out the Gaussian disorder factors the sum into $(2\cosh h)^N \exp(N \frac{\beta^2}{4}(1 - 1/N))$.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKFirstMoment.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First-moment Jensen upper bound on the free energy]\label{thm:sg-tal:first-moment-bound}
  \lean{MeasureTheory.integral_log_le_log_integral,
        SpinGlasses.sk_free_energy_le_first_moment}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.23)--(1.26)}
Let $N\ge1$ and assume $\log Z_N$ is integrable under the disorder. By Jensen's inequality for the concave logarithm, the expected free energy satisfies (Eqs.~(1.24) and (1.26)):
\[
p_N(\beta, h) \le \frac{1}{N} \log \mathbb{E}[Z_N] = \frac{\beta^2}{4}\left(1 - \frac{1}{N}\right) + \log 2 + \log\cosh h.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
$p_N(\beta, h) = N^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\log Z_N] \le N^{-1}\log \mathbb{E}[Z_N]$, and apply Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:first-moment}.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKFirstMoment.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Temperature derivative of the expected free energy]\label{thm:sg-tal:beta-derivative}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_log_sum_exp_beta,
        SpinGlasses.measurable_disorder_u,
        SpinGlasses.memLp_finPairs_entry,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_finPairs_entry_abs,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_sk_free_energy_beta}
  \leanok
\talsource{Lemma~1.3.11: Eq.~(1.83) and the first line of Eq.~(1.85); the evaluation (1.84) is not formalized}
Let $N\ge1$, $h\in\mathbb R$, $u_\sigma(g)=N^{-1/2}\sum_{i<j}g_{ij}\sigma_i\sigma_j$ and suppose $\log Z_N(b,h)$ is integrable under the disorder at $b=\beta$. For fixed disorder, $b \mapsto \log Z_N(b)$ has derivative $\langle u_\sigma \rangle_b$, and (Lemma~1.3.11, Eq.~(1.83) after taking expectation, first line of Eq.~(1.85))
\[
\frac{\partial p_N}{\partial \beta} = \frac{1}{N}\, \mathbb{E}\big[\langle u_\sigma \rangle\big].
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Differentiating $b \mapsto \log \sum_\sigma \exp(b\, u_\sigma(g) + C_\sigma)$ yields the Gibbs average.
Dominated differentiation under the integral applies via the integrable bound $B(g) = (\sqrt{N})^{-1}\sum_{i<j} |g_{ij}|$.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKFirstMoment.lean}.
\emph{Not formalized:} the evaluation $\frac1N\mathbb{E}[\langle u_\sigma\rangle]=\frac{\beta}{2}(1-\mathbb{E}[\langle R_{1,2}^2\rangle])$ by Gaussian integration by parts; see Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:dev-12}.
\end{proof}

\section{Guerra's Smart Path Interpolation and the RS Upper Bound (Talagrand Section~1.3)}\label{sec:sg-tal:guerra-rs}

Section~1.3 introduces Guerra's smart path interpolation between the fully interacting SK model and decoupled Gaussian spins, leading to the replica-symmetric upper bound.

\begin{remark}[Deviations from the book in Section~1.3]\label{rem:sg-tal:dev-13}
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Parametrization of the path.} Definition~\ref{def:sg-tal:smart-path} uses the trigonometric path $\cos s\,X+\sin s\,Y+h$, $s\in[0,\pi/2]$, with $s=0$ the SK model and $s=\pi/2$ the decoupled model, whereas the book uses $\sqrt t\,X+\sqrt{1-t}\,Y+h$ with $t=1$ the SK end; they are related by $t=\cos^2 s$ and all derivative formulas carry the factor $\sin s\cos s$ instead of the book's $dt$ (Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:book-path}).
\item \emph{Joint Gaussian space.} The pair disorder $g$ and the site disorder $z$ are realized on the product space $(\mathrm{FinPairs}\,N\to\mathbb R)\times(\mathrm{Fin}\,N\to\mathbb R)$ with the product measure; joint Gaussianity of $(X_\sigma,Y_\sigma)_\sigma$ is proved from independence (\texttt{HasGaussianLaw.sumElim\_prod\_of\_independent}).
\item \emph{The fixed point $q$: a definition in the book, a hypothesis in Lean.} In the book, $q$ is \emph{the} solution of $q=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta z\sqrt q+h)]$ (Eq.~(1.74)), so statements such as ``$p_N\le\operatorname{SK}(\beta,h)$'' refer to one specific number. In Lean every theorem that uses $q$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-conc}, Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:alr}, and the helper lemmas of Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:latala-alr}) quantifies over an arbitrary real number $q$ together with the hypothesis \texttt{hfix} : $\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt q\,z+h)]=q$. The existence of such a $q\in[0,1]$ is proved (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point}); uniqueness is proved only for $h\ne0$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point-unique}), and the theorems of these sections do not use it. See Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:fixed-point-scope} for the intuition, the reasons, and the consequences. Guerra's bound itself (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:guerra-rs-bound}) holds for every $q\ge0$ and needs no fixed-point hypothesis.
\end{itemize}
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Guerra's smart path Hamiltonian]\label{def:sg-tal:smart-path}
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.59), (1.62), (1.72)}
For $s \in [0, \pi/2]$, independent standard Gaussians $(g_{ij})_{i<j}$ and $(z_i)_{i \le N}$, parameter $q \ge 0$, and bias $h$, the (trigonometric) smart path Hamiltonian is
\[
-H_s(\sigma) = \cos s \,\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{N}} \sum_{1 \le i < j \le N} g_{ij} \sigma_i \sigma_j + \sin s \,\beta \sqrt{q} \sum_{i \le N} z_i \sigma_i + h \sum_{i \le N} \sigma_i.
\]
The interpolating partition function is $Z_s = \sum_\sigma \exp(-H_s(\sigma))$ and $\varphi(s) = N^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\log Z_s]$. At $s = 0$ this is the SK model and at $s = \pi/2$ the decoupled model with fields $\beta\sqrt q\, z_i + h$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[The book's definition of the smart path]\label{rem:sg-tal:book-path}
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.59), (1.62), (1.64), (1.65)}
Talagrand (Section~1.3) uses, for $t \in [0,1]$,
\[
-H_t^{\mathrm{book}}(\sigma)=\sqrt{t}\,\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{N}} \sum_{i<j} g_{ij}\sigma_i\sigma_j+\sqrt{1-t}\,\beta\sqrt{q}\sum_{i} z_i\sigma_i+h\sum_{i}\sigma_i ,
\]
with $Z_t=\sum_\sigma\exp(-H^{\mathrm{book}}_t(\sigma))$ and $\varphi_{\mathrm{book}}(t)=N^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\log Z_t]$; here $t=1$ is the SK model and $t=0$ the decoupled one. The two paths are related by $t=\cos^2 s$, i.e.\ $\sqrt t=\cos s$ and $\sqrt{1-t}=\sin s$, so $H^{\mathrm{book}}_{\cos^2 s}=H_s$ and $\varphi(s)=\varphi_{\mathrm{book}}(\cos^2 s)$. The Lean development uses $s$ because $s\mapsto\cos s$, $\sin s$ are smooth on all of $\mathbb R$, whereas $t\mapsto\sqrt t$ and $\sqrt{1-t}$ are not differentiable at the endpoints, which would force a separate argument for differentiability at $t=0,1$. By the chain rule $\frac{d}{ds}=-2\sin s\cos s\,\frac{d}{dt}$, so every book formula in $\frac{d}{dt}$ becomes the same formula in $\frac{d}{ds}$ with an extra factor $\sin s\cos s$ and the opposite orientation (the SK end is $s=0$, not $t=1$). Integrals $\int_0^1 \cdots\,dt$ become $\int_0^{\pi/2}\cdots\,2\sin s\cos s\,ds$.
In the book's variable the derivative formula (Eq.~(1.65)) reads
\[
\frac{d}{dt}\varphi_{\mathrm{book}}(t) = \frac{1}{2N} \left(\mathbb{E}\big[\langle U(\sigma, \sigma) \rangle_t\big] - \mathbb{E}\big[\langle U(\sigma^1, \sigma^2) \rangle_t\big]\right) = -\frac{\beta^2}{4} \mathbb{E}\big[\langle (R_{1,2} - q)^2 \rangle_t\big] + \frac{\beta^2}{4}(1 - q)^2 ,
\]
with the book's covariance difference $U=\mathbb{E}[uu]-\mathbb{E}[vv]$, where $u_\sigma=\frac{\beta}{\sqrt N}\sum_{i<j}g_{ij}\sigma_i\sigma_j$ and $v_\sigma=\beta\sqrt q\sum_iz_i\sigma_i$ (Eq.~(1.64)), i.e.\ $\frac1NU(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)=\frac{\beta^2}{2}\big((R_{1,2}-q)^2-q^2-\frac1N\big)$. The Lean development uses the opposite sign $V=-U$, because the pair field $X$ multiplies $\cos s$.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Covariance of the decoupled Gaussian cavity field]\label{lem:sg-tal:decoupled-covariance}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_decoupled_gaussian_field_mul}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.62) for $v_\sigma$, entering Eq.~(1.64)}
For arbitrary finite real vectors $\sigma, \tau$ and independent standard Gaussian variables
$(z_i)$, the cavity-field part of Guerra's path satisfies
\[
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\beta\sqrt{q}\sum_i z_i\sigma_i\right)
  \left(\beta\sqrt{q}\sum_i z_i\tau_i\right)\right]
= \beta^2 q \sum_i \sigma_i\tau_i,
\qquad q \geq 0.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Expand the product and use that $\mathbb{E}[z_iz_j]=\delta_{ij}$ for independent standard Gaussians:
\[
\mathbb{E}\Big[\beta^2 q\sum_{i,j}\sigma_i\tau_j\,z_iz_j\Big]
=\beta^2 q\sum_{i,j}\sigma_i\tau_j\,\delta_{ij}=\beta^2 q\sum_i\sigma_i\tau_i .
\]
For Ising spins this is $\beta^2 q\,N\,R(\sigma,\tau)$, where $R(\sigma,\tau)=N^{-1}\sum_i\sigma_i\tau_i$ is the overlap.
The Lean statement is deliberately for arbitrary finite real vectors, not only hypercube spins,
so it is reusable for relaxed and spherical spin models.  Formalized in
\texttt{SpinGlasses/SKReplicaSymmetricBound.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Pair-indexed Gaussian covariance]\label{lem:sg-tal:pair-covariance}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_normalized_gaussian_linear_form_mul,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_pair_field_mul,
        SpinGlasses.sum_finPairs_mul_eq_sub_sum_sq_div_two,
        SpinGlasses.sum_sk_pair_coefficient_eq_overlap,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_pair_field_mul_eq_overlap}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.63); for $\beta=1$, Eq.~(1.6)}
For pair-indexed iid standard Gaussian disorder and two spin configurations, integrating out the
disorder gives
\[
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i<j}
  g_{ij}\sigma_i\sigma_j\right)
  \left(\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i<j}
  g_{ij}\tau_i\tau_j\right)\right]
= \frac{\beta^2}{N}\sum_{i<j}\sigma_i\sigma_j\tau_i\tau_j.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Two steps.

\emph{Step 1 (orthogonality of the $g_{ij}$).} Since $\mathbb{E}[g_{ij}g_{kl}]=1$ if $(i,j)=(k,l)$ and $0$ otherwise,
\[
\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{\beta^2}{N}\sum_{i<j}\sum_{k<l} g_{ij}g_{kl}\,\sigma_i\sigma_j\tau_k\tau_l\Big]
=\frac{\beta^2}{N}\sum_{i<j}\sigma_i\sigma_j\tau_i\tau_j .
\]
This is the general Lean theorem (covariance of two normalized linear forms in an iid Gaussian vector), instantiated with the coefficients $\sigma_i\sigma_j$ and $\tau_i\tau_j$.

\emph{Step 2 (pair sum $=$ half the square minus the diagonal).} Put $a_i=\sigma_i\tau_i\in\{\pm1\}$. Then $\sigma_i\sigma_j\tau_i\tau_j=a_ia_j$ and
\[
\sum_{i<j}a_ia_j=\frac12\Big(\big(\textstyle\sum_i a_i\big)^2-\sum_i a_i^2\Big)
=\frac12\big((N R(\sigma,\tau))^2-N\big),
\]
because $\sum_ia_i=N R(\sigma,\tau)$ and $a_i^2=1$. Hence the covariance equals
\[
\frac{\beta^2}{2N}\big(N^2R^2-N\big)=\frac{N\beta^2}{2}\Big(R(\sigma,\tau)^2-\frac1N\Big).
\]
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKReplicaSymmetricBound.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Algebraic assembly of the smart-path covariance difference]
  \label{lem:sg-tal:path-covariance-algebra}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sum_mul_comm_eq_dotProduct,
        SpinGlasses.inv_sqrt_mul_self,
        SpinGlasses.smart_path_half_covariance_difference_eq,
        SpinGlasses.sk_smart_path_half_covariance_difference_eq}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.55), (1.64)}
For Ising configurations, the two concrete Gaussian fields have covariances
\[
C = \frac{N\beta^2}{2}\left(R(\sigma,\tau)^2 - \frac1N\right),
\qquad
D = \beta^2 q\, \sigma\mathbin{\cdot}\tau.
\]
Then their normalized half-difference is exactly
\[
\frac{C-D}{2N} = \frac{\beta^2}{4}
  \left((R(\sigma,\tau)-q)^2-q^2-\frac1N\right).
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The two covariances are those of Lemmas~\ref{lem:sg-tal:pair-covariance} and~\ref{lem:sg-tal:decoupled-covariance} with $\sigma\mathbin{\cdot}\tau=N R$, $R=R(\sigma,\tau)$. Subtract and complete the square in $R$:
\begin{align*}
C-D&=\frac{N\beta^2}{2}\Big(R^2-\frac1N\Big)-\beta^2 q\,N R
=\frac{N\beta^2}{2}\Big(R^2-2qR-\frac1N\Big)\\
&=\frac{N\beta^2}{2}\Big((R-q)^2-q^2-\frac1N\Big),
\end{align*}
using $R^2-2qR=(R-q)^2-q^2$. Dividing by $2N$ gives $\frac{C-D}{2N}=\frac{\beta^2}{4}\big((R-q)^2-q^2-\frac1N\big)$.
The two special values used later are $R=1$ (a configuration with itself), where this equals $\frac{\beta^2}{4}\big((1-q)^2-q^2-\frac1N\big)$, and a general overlap $R=R_{1,2}$ of two replicas.
Two elementary facts are used throughout the covariance computations and are stated as separate lemmas: a linear form $\sum_k g_k a_k$ equals the dot product $a\cdot g$, and $(\sqrt n)^{-1}(\sqrt n)^{-1}=n^{-1}$ for $n\ge0$ (this is how the factor $N^{-1/2}$ in the Hamiltonian squares to the $1/N$ of the covariance).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Normalized trigonometric smart-path derivative]
  \label{lem:sg-tal:trig-smart-path-derivative}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_normalized_log_sum_exp_trig_of_covariance_difference}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.60), (1.65), in the variable $t=\cos^2 s$}
Let $X_\sigma$ and $Y_\sigma$ be centered, jointly Gaussian, independent fields on a finite
configuration space, and let $r(\sigma,\tau)$ satisfy $r(\sigma,\sigma)=1$.  Suppose
\[
\frac1N\frac{\operatorname{Cov}(X_\sigma,X_\tau)-
  \operatorname{Cov}(Y_\sigma,Y_\tau)}2
= \frac{\beta^2}{4}\left((r(\sigma,\tau)-q)^2-q^2-\frac1N\right).
\]
For the smooth path $\cos s\,X+\sin s\,Y+h$, the normalized log-partition function has
derivative
\[
\frac{\beta^2}{2}\sin s\cos s\,
\mathbb{E}[\left\langle (r(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)-q)^2-(1-q)^2\right\rangle_s].
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Write $C(\sigma,\tau)=\operatorname{Cov}(X_\sigma,X_\tau)$, $D(\sigma,\tau)=\operatorname{Cov}(Y_\sigma,Y_\tau)$, $V=D-C$, and along the path
\[
W_\sigma(s)=\cos s\,X_\sigma+\sin s\,Y_\sigma+h_\sigma,\quad
W'_\sigma(s)=-\sin s\,X_\sigma+\cos s\,Y_\sigma,\quad
w_\sigma=\frac{e^{W_\sigma}}{\sum_\tau e^{W_\tau}},
\]
so that $Z_s=\sum_\sigma e^{W_\sigma}$ and $\langle f\rangle_s=\sum_\sigma w_\sigma f(\sigma)$.

\emph{Step 1 (chain rule for fixed disorder).} $\frac{d}{ds}\log Z_s=\sum_\sigma w_\sigma W'_\sigma$.
Differentiation under $\mathbb{E}$ is justified by Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:path-derivative} (the integrand has exponential growth), so $\frac{d}{ds}\mathbb{E}\log Z_s=\sum_\sigma\mathbb{E}[w_\sigma W'_\sigma]$.

\emph{Step 2 (Gaussian integration by parts).} $W'_\sigma$ and $W_\tau-h_\tau$ are centered and jointly Gaussian, so for a smooth function $G$ of the vector $(W_\tau)_\tau$ of moderate growth, $\mathbb{E}[W'_\sigma G]=\sum_\tau\mathbb{E}[W'_\sigma W_\tau]\,\mathbb{E}[\partial_\tau G]$. Since $X$ and $Y$ are independent,
\[
\mathbb{E}[W'_\sigma W_\tau]=-\sin s\cos s\,C(\sigma,\tau)+\cos s\sin s\,D(\sigma,\tau)=\sin s\cos s\;V(\sigma,\tau).
\]
Apply this to $G=w_\sigma$, for which $\partial_\tau w_\sigma=w_\sigma(\delta_{\sigma\tau}-w_\tau)$.

\emph{Step 3 (sum over $\sigma$).}
\[
\frac{d}{ds}\mathbb{E}\log Z_s=\sin s\cos s\sum_{\sigma,\tau}V(\sigma,\tau)\,\mathbb{E}\big[w_\sigma\delta_{\sigma\tau}-w_\sigma w_\tau\big]
=\sin s\cos s\Big(\mathbb{E}\langle V(\sigma,\sigma)\rangle_s-\mathbb{E}\langle V(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)\rangle_s\Big),
\]
where $\langle f(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)\rangle_s=\sum_{\sigma^1,\sigma^2}w_{\sigma^1}w_{\sigma^2}f(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)$ is the average over two independent replicas.

\emph{Step 4 (insert the hypothesis).} $V=-(C-D)$, so by hypothesis $V(\sigma,\tau)=-\frac{N\beta^2}{2}\big((r-q)^2-q^2-\frac1N\big)$ with $r=r(\sigma,\tau)$. For $r(\sigma,\sigma)=1$ and $r=r(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)$ the terms $-q^2-\frac1N$ cancel in the difference:
\[
V(\sigma,\sigma)-V(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)=\frac{N\beta^2}{2}\Big((r(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)-q)^2-(1-q)^2\Big).
\]
Dividing by $N$ (the normalization of $\varphi$) gives the displayed derivative. The lemma applies to arbitrary finite Gaussian fields, so the SK covariance calculation is only one specialization.
The SK instance (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:smart-path-deriv}) is obtained by realizing the pair field and the cavity field as a jointly Gaussian pair.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Trigonometric replica-symmetric endpoint estimate]
  \label{lem:sg-tal:trig-rs-endpoint}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.le_endpoint_of_trig_square_deriv}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.66)--(1.67)}
If a differentiable function satisfies
\[
\phi'(s)=\frac{\beta^2}{2}\sin s\cos s
  \left(A(s)-(1-q)^2\right), \qquad A(s)\geq 0,
\]
then
\[
\phi(0)\leq \phi(\pi/2)+\frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q)^2.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Put $\psi(s)=\phi(s)+\frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q)^2\sin^2 s$. Since $\frac{d}{ds}\sin^2 s=2\sin s\cos s$,
\[
\psi'(s)=\frac{\beta^2}{2}\sin s\cos s\,\big(A(s)-(1-q)^2\big)+\frac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q)^2\sin s\cos s
=\frac{\beta^2}{2}\sin s\cos s\,A(s)\ \ge 0
\]
on $[0,\pi/2]$, where $\sin s\cos s\ge0$ and $A\ge0$. So $\psi$ is monotone on $[0,\pi/2]$ (Mathlib's \texttt{monotoneOn\_of\_deriv\_nonneg}), hence $\psi(0)\le\psi(\pi/2)$, i.e.\ $\phi(0)\le\phi(\pi/2)+\frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q)^2$ because $\sin^2 0=0$, $\sin^2\frac\pi2=1$.
Equivalently, integrating $\phi'(s)\le\frac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q)^2\sin s\cos s$ over $[0,\pi/2]$ and using $\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin s\cos s\,ds=\frac12$ gives the same bound; the trick $\psi$ avoids integrating.
\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[Derivative along the smart path]\label{thm:sg-tal:smart-path-deriv}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_sk_smart_path_profile,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_normalized_log_sum_exp_trig_of_covariance_difference}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.65)--(1.66), with endpoints (1.69) and (1.72)}
Let $N\ge1$, $\beta,h\in\mathbb R$, $q\ge0$. The function $\varphi(s) = N^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\log Z_s]$ of Definition~\ref{def:sg-tal:smart-path} is differentiable with
\[
\varphi'(s) = \frac{\beta^2}{2}\sin s\cos s\,\Big(A(s) - (1 - q)^2\Big),\qquad A(s)=\mathbb{E}\big[\langle (R_{1,2} - q)^2 \rangle_s\big]\ge0,
\]
and $\varphi(0)$ is the SK free energy $p_N(\beta,h)$, $\varphi(\pi/2)=\log 2+\mathbb{E}[\log\cosh(\beta z\sqrt q+h)]$. In particular $\varphi'(s) \ge -\frac{\beta^2}{2}\sin s\cos s\,(1 - q)^2$ for all $s \in [0, \pi/2]$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
By Gaussian integration by parts along the path $\cos s\,X+\sin s\,Y+h$ the derivative is $\frac{\sin s\cos s}{N}\,\big(\mathbb{E}[\langle V(\sigma,\sigma)\rangle_s] - \mathbb{E}[\langle V(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)\rangle_s]\big)$ with $V=\mathbb{E}[YY]-\mathbb{E}[XX]$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:trig-smart-path-derivative}).
For the SK fields, Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:path-covariance-algebra} gives $V(\sigma,\tau)=-\frac{N\beta^2}{2}\big((R(\sigma,\tau)-q)^2-q^2-\frac1N\big)$. Hence $V(\sigma,\sigma)=-\frac{N\beta^2}{2}\big((1-q)^2-q^2-\frac1N\big)$, the terms $-q^2-1/N$ cancel in the difference, and
\[
\varphi'(s)=\frac{\sin s\cos s}{N}\cdot\frac{N\beta^2}{2}\,\mathbb{E}\big\langle (R_{1,2}-q)^2-(1-q)^2\big\rangle_s,
\]
which is the displayed formula, since $\mathbb{E}\langle(1-q)^2\rangle_s=(1-q)^2$ ($\langle\cdot\rangle_s$ is a probability measure). The term $A(s)\ge0$ is the Gibbs average of a square. The book's form of this statement in the variable $t=\cos^2 s$ is recorded in Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:book-path}.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKSmartPathProfile.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Guerra's replica-symmetric upper bound]\label{thm:sg-tal:guerra-rs-bound}
  \lean{ProbabilityTheory.HasGaussianLaw.sumElim_prod_of_independent,
        ProbabilityTheory.hasGaussianLaw_dotProduct_family,
        SpinGlasses.hasGaussianLaw_const_mul_sum_family,
        SpinGlasses.sk_pair_sum_eq_dotProduct,
        SpinGlasses.exists_sk_smart_path_fields,
        ProbabilityTheory.integrable_log_cosh_affine_gaussianReal,
        SpinGlasses.sk_replica_symmetric_upper_bound}
  \leanok
\talsource{Theorem~1.3.7, Eq.~(1.73), proved from Eqs.~(1.65)--(1.69)}
For all $N\ge1$, $\beta, h \in \mathbb{R}$, and any parameter $q \ge 0$ (Theorem~1.3.7):
\[
p_N(\beta, h) \le \log 2 + \mathbb{E}[\log\cosh(\beta z \sqrt{q} + h)] + \frac{\beta^2}{4}(1 - q)^2,
\]
where $z \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
The idea: compare the SK free energy ($s=0$) with the decoupled free energy ($s=\pi/2$), whose value is explicit, by showing that the free energy changes along the path by at most $\frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q)^2$. In Lean the path is $s\mapsto\cos s\,X+\sin s\,Y+h$, $s\in[0,\pi/2]$, with $X_\sigma=\frac{\beta}{\sqrt N}\sum_{i<j}g_{ij}\sigma_i\sigma_j$ the SK field and $Y_\sigma=\beta\sqrt q\sum_iz_i\sigma_i$ the cavity field, on the product space of the two independent disorders (jointly Gaussian by
\texttt{HasGaussianLaw.sumElim\_prod\_of\_independent}, which allows different index types for the two fields). Write $\varphi(s)=N^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\log Z_s]$, $Z_s=\sum_\sigma\exp(-H_s(\sigma))$, and $R_{1,2}=N^{-1}\sum_i\sigma^1_i\sigma^2_i$.

\emph{Step 1: covariances.} For Ising configurations $\sigma,\tau$ with overlap $R=R(\sigma,\tau)$,
\[
\mathbb{E}[X_\sigma X_\tau]=\frac{N\beta^2}{2}\Big(R^2-\frac1N\Big)\quad(\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:pair-covariance}}),\qquad
\mathbb{E}[Y_\sigma Y_\tau]=\beta^2 q\,N R\quad(\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:decoupled-covariance}}).
\]

\emph{Step 2: the derivative.} Gaussian integration by parts (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:trig-smart-path-derivative}) turns $\varphi'(s)$ into
$\sin s\cos s\,N^{-1}\big(\mathbb{E}\langle V(\sigma,\sigma)\rangle_s-\mathbb{E}\langle V(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)\rangle_s\big)$ with $V=\mathbb{E}[YY]-\mathbb{E}[XX]$. By Step 1,
$V(\sigma,\tau)=-\frac{N\beta^2}{2}\big((R(\sigma,\tau)-q)^2-q^2-\frac1N\big)$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:path-covariance-algebra}); the terms $-q^2-\frac1N$ cancel between $V(\sigma,\sigma)$ ($R=1$) and $V(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)$, so
\[
\varphi'(s)=\frac{\beta^2}{2}\sin s\cos s\,\Big(A(s)-(1-q)^2\Big),\qquad A(s)=\mathbb{E}\big\langle(R_{1,2}-q)^2\big\rangle_s\ge0 .
\]
$A(s)\ge0$ because it is the expectation of a Gibbs average of a square (a probability measure, the product of two copies of the Gibbs measure). This is the point of the smart path: the unknown overlap enters only through the nonnegative term $A(s)$.

\emph{Step 3: discard the unknown term.} Since $A(s)\ge0$ and $\sin s\cos s\ge0$ on $[0,\pi/2]$, Step 2 gives the lower bound $\varphi'(s)\ge-\frac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q)^2\sin s\cos s$. Integrating over $[0,\pi/2]$, with $\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin s\cos s\,ds=\frac12$, gives $\varphi(\pi/2)-\varphi(0)\ge-\frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q)^2$, i.e.
\[
\varphi(0)\le\varphi(\pi/2)+\frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q)^2 .
\]
Lean avoids the integral (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:trig-rs-endpoint}): the function $\psi(s)=\varphi(s)+\frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q)^2\sin^2 s$ has $\psi'(s)=\frac{\beta^2}{2}\sin s\cos s\,A(s)\ge0$, so it is monotone and $\psi(0)\le\psi(\pi/2)$. The bound is explicit because $\varphi(\pi/2)$ and the correction $\frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q)^2$ are explicit. What is thrown away is exactly $\int_0^{\pi/2}\frac{\beta^2}{2}\sin s\cos s\,A(s)\,ds\ge0$, which is small when the overlap $R_{1,2}$ concentrates near $q$; this is why the best choice of $q$ is the solution of the self-consistency equation (Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:fixed-point-scope}).

\emph{Step 4: the endpoints.} At $s=0$, $\varphi(0)=p_N(\beta,h)$. At $s=\pi/2$ the Hamiltonian is $-H(\sigma)=\sum_i\sigma_iY_i$ with $Y_i=\beta\sqrt q\,z_i+h$, so $Z=\prod_i\big(e^{Y_i}+e^{-Y_i}\big)=\prod_i2\cosh Y_i$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:decoupled-partition}) and
\[
\varphi(\pi/2)=\frac1N\sum_{i\le N}\mathbb{E}\big[\log2+\log\cosh(\beta\sqrt q\,z_i+h)\big]=\log2+\mathbb{E}\log\cosh(\beta z\sqrt q+h),
\]
the $z_i$ being identically distributed. Inserting these values into the inequality of Step 3 gives the theorem.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKGuerraBound.lean} (the free energy profile along the path is \texttt{SKSmartPathProfile.lean}).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Existence of a solution of the self-consistency equation]\label{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.continuous_fixedPointMap,
        SpinGlasses.fixedPointMap_mem_Icc,
        SpinGlasses.fixedPoint_mem_Icc,
        SpinGlasses.abs_fixedPoint_le_one,
        SpinGlasses.exists_fixedPoint}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.74)}
For all $\beta,h\in\mathbb R$ the map $\psi(x)=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta z\sqrt x+h)]$, $z$ standard Gaussian, is continuous on $\mathbb R$ with values in $[0,1]$. Consequently there is a $q\in[0,1]$ with
\[
q=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta z\sqrt q+h)]\qquad\text{(Eq.~(1.74))}.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Continuity follows from dominated convergence (the integrand is bounded by $1$ and continuous in $x$ for each $z$), and $0\le\tanh^2\le1$ gives the range. The function $\psi(x)-x$ is $\ge0$ at $x=0$ and $\le0$ at $x=1$, so the intermediate value theorem gives a zero in $[0,1]$.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKFixedPoint.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Uniqueness of the solution of the self-consistency equation, $h\ne0$]\label{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point-unique}
  \uses{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point, thm:sg-tal:residual}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.fixedPoint_unique,
        SpinGlasses.existsUnique_fixedPoint}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.74) and Proposition~1.3.8, for a deterministic field $h\ne0$ (so $\mathbb{E}h^2>0$)}
Let $\beta\in\mathbb R$ and $h\ne0$. If $q,r$ are solutions of $x=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta z\sqrt x+h)]$, then $q=r$. Consequently there is exactly one $q\in[0,1]$ with
$q=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta z\sqrt q+h)]$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Every solution is positive, because $\mathbb{E}\tanh^2(wz+h)>0$ for $h\ne0$. At a solution $x$ one has $\Phi(x)/x=1$, where $\Phi(x)=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta z\sqrt x+h)]$, and
$x\mapsto\Phi(x)/x$ is strictly decreasing on $(0,\infty)$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:residual}, the case of $\tanh$ of Proposition~A.14.1), hence injective, so $q=r$. Existence in $[0,1]$ is
Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point}. The argument uses neither $\beta<1$ nor the residual bound, and it does not identify the solution with a minimizer of $\operatorname{RS}$ (Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:fixed-point-scope}).
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKFixedPointResidual.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Guerra's bound at the unique solution, $h\ne0$]\label{thm:sg-tal:guerra-rs-bound-unique}
  \uses{thm:sg-tal:guerra-rs-bound, thm:sg-tal:fixed-point-unique}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.existsUnique_fixedPoint_replica_symmetric_upper_bound}
  \leanok
\talsource{Theorem~1.3.7 in the book's form $p_N(\beta,h)\le\operatorname{SK}(\beta,h)=\operatorname{RS}(q)$ with $q$ the solution of Eq.~(1.74), together with Proposition~1.3.8}
Let $\beta\in\mathbb R$ and $h\ne0$. There is exactly one $q\in[0,1]$ such that $q=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta z\sqrt q+h)]$ and, for every $N\ge1$,
\[
p_N(\beta,h)\le \log 2+\mathbb{E}[\log\cosh(\beta z\sqrt q+h)]+\tfrac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q)^2=\operatorname{RS}(q).
\]
Thus $\operatorname{SK}(\beta,h):=\operatorname{RS}(q)$ is well defined for $h\ne0$ and bounds every $p_N(\beta,h)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Take the solution $q\in[0,1]$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point-unique}; since $q\ge0$, Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:guerra-rs-bound} applies. Any other $q'$ with the first property is a
solution of (1.74), hence equals $q$. For $h=0$ this packaging is not available: there can be two solutions (Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:fixed-point-scope}). Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:guerra-rs-bound} itself, for
every $q\ge0$, is unaffected.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKGuerraBoundUnique.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Critical points of the replica-symmetric functional (Eqs.~(1.74)--(1.76), Proposition~1.3.8)]\label{thm:sg-tal:rs-critical}
  \uses{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point, thm:sg-tal:fixed-point-unique}
  \lean{ProbabilityTheory.integral_deriv_comp_affine_mul_id_gaussianReal,
        ProbabilityTheory.hasDerivAt_integral_comp_affine_gaussianReal,
        SpinGlasses.rsFunctional,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_log_cosh_sqrt,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_rsFunctional,
        SpinGlasses.continuousOn_rsFunctional,
        SpinGlasses.fixedPoint_of_isMinOn_rsFunctional,
        SpinGlasses.exists_fixedPoint_isMinOn_rsFunctional,
        SpinGlasses.deriv_fixedPointMap_sqrt_le,
        SpinGlasses.fixedPoint_unique_of_abs_lt_one,
        SpinGlasses.fixedPoint_unique_of_pos}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.74)--(1.76) and Proposition~1.3.8}
Let $\beta,h\in\mathbb R$, $\Phi(x)=\mathbb{E}\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt x\,z+h)$ and $\operatorname{RS}(y)=\log2+\mathbb{E}\log\cosh(\beta\sqrt y\,z+h)+\frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-y)^2$ for $y\ge0$ ($z$ standard Gaussian).
\begin{enumerate}
\item (Eq.~(1.75)) If $f$ has bounded continuous derivatives $f',f''$ and $f(wz+h)$ is integrable, then $\frac{d}{dw}\mathbb{E}f(wz+h)=w\,\mathbb{E}f''(wz+h)$; hence for $x>0$, $\frac{d}{dx}\mathbb{E}\log\cosh(\beta\sqrt x\,z+h)=\frac{\beta^2}{2}\mathbb{E}(1-\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt x\,z+h))$ and $\operatorname{RS}'(x)=\frac{\beta^2}{2}(x-\Phi(x))$.
\item If $\beta\ne0$ and $q\ge0$ minimizes $\operatorname{RS}$ on $[0,\infty)$, then $q=\Phi(q)$. For every $\beta,h$ a minimizer which is a solution of $q=\Phi(q)$ exists.
\item If $|\beta|<1$, then $q=\Phi(q)$, $r=\Phi(r)$ with $q,r\ge0$ imply $q=r$ (for every $h$, including $h=0$).
\item If $\beta\ne0$ or $h\ne0$ and $q,r>0$ both solve (1.74), then $q=r$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
(1) Stein's lemma for the bounded function $f'$ gives $\mathbb{E}[f'(wz+h)z]=w\,\mathbb{E}f''(wz+h)$; differentiation under the integral sign (dominated by $C|z|$) gives the derivative in $w$, and the chain rule with $w=\beta\sqrt x$, $\frac{dw}{dx}=\frac{\beta}{2\sqrt x}$ gives (1.75). For $f=\log\cosh$, $f'=\tanh$, $f''=1-\tanh^2$ and $\mathbb{E}(1-\tanh^2)=1-\Phi$; adding the derivative $-\frac{\beta^2}{2}(1-x)$ of the quadratic term gives $\operatorname{RS}'$. The same lemma now also gives the parameter derivative of $\Phi$ used in the proof of Proposition~1.9.5 (it replaces a copy specialized to $\tanh^2$).
(2) If $q>0$ the minimum is interior, so $\operatorname{RS}'(q)=0$, i.e.\ $q=\Phi(q)$ since $\beta\ne0$. If $q=0$ and $h=0$ then $\Phi(0)=\tanh^2 0=0$. If $q=0$ and $h\ne0$, then $\Phi(0)=\tanh^2h>0$ and $\Phi$ is continuous, so $\operatorname{RS}'(x)<0$ on some $(0,\delta)$; as $\operatorname{RS}$ is continuous on $[0,\delta]$ (dominated convergence) it is strictly decreasing there, contradicting minimality at $0$. For existence: $\operatorname{RS}(y)\ge\log2+\frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-y)^2$ because $\log\cosh\ge0$, so $\operatorname{RS}(y)\ge\operatorname{RS}(1)$ for $y\ge R=1+\sqrt{4(\operatorname{RS}(1)-\log2)/\beta^2}$; a minimizer on the compact $[0,R]$ is a global minimizer and is a solution by the first part. For $\beta=0$, $\operatorname{RS}$ is constant and any solution of (1.74) (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point}) works.
(3) For $x>0$, $\Phi'(x)=\beta^2\mathbb{E}[(1-t)(1-3t)]$ with $t=\tanh^2\in[0,1)$ and $(1-t)(1-3t)=1-4t+3t^2\le1$, so $(\Phi(x)-x)'\le\beta^2-1<0$ on $(0,\infty)$, and $\Phi(x)-x$ is continuous on $[0,\infty)$, hence strictly decreasing; it vanishes at both $q$ and $r$.
(4) $\Phi(x)/x$ is strictly decreasing on $(0,\infty)$ if $\beta\ne0$ or $h\ne0$ (Proposition~A.14.1 for $\tanh$) and equals $1$ at both points. Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point-unique} is the case $h\ne0$ (where positivity is automatic).
\emph{Formalization notes.} $\operatorname{RS}$ is a definition (\texttt{rsFunctional}) because it is differentiated, minimized and bounded in several statements; Guerra's bound (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:guerra-rs-bound}) keeps its written-out form. The claim ``every fixed point minimizes $\operatorname{RS}$'' is false (for $h=0$, $\beta>1$ there are two fixed points, and $\beta=0$ makes every point a minimizer), so the formal statement is that every minimizer is a fixed point when $\beta\ne0$, and that a minimizing fixed point exists. Not claimed: that for $h\ne0$ the unique fixed point is the unique minimizer of $\operatorname{RS}$ (this needs the minimizer to be unique, which is not proved).
Formalized in \texttt{ForMathlib/Probability/Moments/GaussianAffineDerivative.lean}, \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKFixedPointRS.lean} and \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKFixedPointResidual.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Scope of the fixed-point results: intuition, context, reasons and consequences]\label{rem:sg-tal:fixed-point-scope}
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.74)--(1.76), Proposition~1.3.8}
\emph{Context and intuition.} Guerra's bound (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:guerra-rs-bound}) is valid for every parameter $q\ge0$:
\[
p_N(\beta,h)\le \operatorname{RS}(q):=\log 2+\mathbb{E}[\log\cosh(\beta z\sqrt q+h)]+\tfrac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q)^2 .
\]
The parameter $q$ plays the role of a guessed overlap of two replicas. The bound is best when $q$ is chosen to minimize $\operatorname{RS}(q)$. The book computes (using the Gaussian integration by parts identity (1.75), $\frac{d}{dx}\mathbb{E}[f(\beta z\sqrt x+h)]=\frac{\beta^2}{2}\mathbb{E}[f''(\beta z\sqrt x+h)]$, with $f=\log\cosh$ and $(\log\cosh)''=1-\tanh^2$)
\[
\operatorname{RS}'(q)=\tfrac{\beta^2}{2}\big(q-\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta z\sqrt q+h)]\big),
\]
so the optimal $q$ is exactly a solution of (1.74). This also has a physical meaning: at the fixed point the decoupled model has expected overlap exactly $q$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:decoupled-overlap}), which is the reason the decoupled endpoint of the interpolation is the right comparison system. Proposition~1.3.8 then shows that the solution is unique when $\mathbb{E}[h^2]>0$ (Latala--Guerra, hard proof in the book's Appendix A.14), and for $\beta<1$ there is a short argument: with $f=\tanh^2$ one has $f''=2(1-t^2)(1-3t^2)\le2$ for $t=\tanh y$, so $\psi'\le\beta^2<1$, $\psi(x)-x$ is strictly decreasing and has at most one zero. From then on the book writes $q$ for this unique number and $\operatorname{SK}(\beta,h):=\operatorname{RS}(q)$.

\emph{What is formalized.} Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point}: a solution of (1.74) exists; and, for $h\ne0$, the solution is unique (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point-unique}, from the strict decrease of $\Phi(x)/x$, Proposition~A.14.1 for $\tanh$), and Guerra's bound is stated at this unique solution (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:guerra-rs-bound-unique}). \emph{Also formalized} (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:rs-critical}): (a) the derivative formula (1.75) for $x>0$ and the identity $\operatorname{RS}'(x)=\frac{\beta^2}{2}(x-\psi(x))$; (b) a minimizer of $\operatorname{RS}$ over $q\ge0$ is a fixed point when $\beta\ne0$, and a minimizing fixed point always exists; (c) the bound $\psi'\le\beta^2$ and uniqueness of the fixed point for $|\beta|<1$, including $h=0$; (d) uniqueness of positive fixed points whenever $\beta\ne0$ or $h\ne0$. \emph{What is not formalized:} that for $h\ne0$ the unique fixed point is the unique minimizer of $\operatorname{RS}$ (the minimizer need not be unique a priori). Note that ``every fixed point minimizes $\operatorname{RS}$'' is false in general (for $h=0$ and $\beta>1$ there are two fixed points), so the correct formal statement of (b) is about \emph{some} minimizer.

\emph{Why this was originally left out.} None of the later arguments uses minimality or uniqueness. Lata\l{}a's argument (Section~1.4) uses only the identity $q=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta z\sqrt q+h)]$: through the decoupled overlap (Eq.~(1.104), $\nu_0(\sigma^1_i\sigma^2_i)=q$) it makes the decoupled model's overlap concentrate at $q$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:sg-tal:base-case}), and the smart-path derivative needs only the generic $q$ of Guerra's bound. Items (a)--(c) additionally need a differentiation-under-the-integral argument in the variance parameter $x$, with the $\sqrt x$ singularity at $x=0$ (handled by continuity, as the derivative is only needed for $x>0$), and (d) is a long separate argument that the book itself calls ``not really of importance''. The effort was therefore spent on the overlap concentration and the ALR formula.

\emph{Impact on the Lean code.} (i) Every Lean theorem that involves $q$ carries the hypothesis \texttt{hfix} and quantifies over all solutions; this is at least as strong as the book's statement, which is about one particular solution, and the theorems are not vacuous because Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point} produces a solution. (ii) For $\beta<1$ (hence in the whole regime $\beta^2<1/4$ where Theorems~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-conc} and~\ref{thm:sg-tal:alr} are proved) the solution is unique in the book, so Lean's ``for every solution'' is the book's statement; Lean proves this uniqueness for $h\ne0$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point-unique}) and, for $|\beta|<1$, also for $h=0$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:rs-critical}). (iii) Consequently the limit in Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:alr} is stated as $\operatorname{RS}(q)$ for the supplied $q$, and the Lean development does not define a function $(\beta,h)\mapsto\operatorname{SK}(\beta,h)$ or state that the limit is independent of the choice of $q$ (for $h\ne0$ this independence is a consequence of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point-unique}). (iv) Nothing here affects the correctness of the proved statements; it only means that the identification ``$q$ is the minimizer of Guerra's bound, hence canonical'' lives in this blueprint text and not in Lean.
\end{remark}

\section{Lata\l{}a's Overlap Concentration and the ALR Formula (Talagrand Section~1.4)}\label{sec:sg-tal:latala-alr}

Section~1.4 proves R.~Lata\l{}a's exponential overlap concentration theorem and establishes the Aizenman--Lebowitz--Ruelle (ALR) high-temperature free energy formula. Throughout the section the symbol $\nu$ (and $\nu_s$, $\nu_{\mathrm{SK}}$, $\nu_{\mathrm{dec}}$) denotes a \emph{disorder-averaged Gibbs average over several replicas}; it is explained in Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:nu-notation}, and each statement below that uses it points back there.

\begin{remark}[The notation $\nu$, $\nu_s$, $\nu_{\mathrm{SK}}$, $\nu_{\mathrm{dec}}$]\label{rem:sg-tal:nu-notation}
\talsource{notation of Chapter~1; the path averages $\nu_t$ of Sections~1.3--1.4}
The symbol $\nu$ is Talagrand's name for the quantity one actually computes when studying the overlap. It is built in three layers.

\emph{(1) Gibbs average.} Fix the disorder $\omega$ (the Gaussian couplings $g$, and for interpolating systems also the cavity variables $z$). The Gibbs measure on $\Sigma_N=\{-1,1\}^N$ is $G_\omega(\sigma)=e^{-H_\omega(\sigma)}/Z_\omega$, and $\langle f\rangle=\sum_\sigma G_\omega(\sigma)f(\sigma)$. For $n$ \emph{replicas} (``real replicas'': $n$ independent configurations drawn from the \emph{same} $G_\omega$) put
\[
\langle f(\sigma^1,\dots,\sigma^n)\rangle=\sum_{\sigma^1,\dots,\sigma^n}G_\omega(\sigma^1)\cdots G_\omega(\sigma^n)\,f(\sigma^1,\dots,\sigma^n).
\]
For example $\langle R_{1,2}\rangle=\sum_{\sigma^1,\sigma^2}G_\omega(\sigma^1)G_\omega(\sigma^2)\,N^{-1}\sum_i\sigma^1_i\sigma^2_i$ is the mean overlap of two independent samples of the same random Gibbs measure.

\emph{(2) Average over the disorder.}
\[
\nu(f)=\mathbb{E}\big[\langle f\rangle\big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{\sigma^1,\dots,\sigma^n}G_\omega(\sigma^1)\cdots G_\omega(\sigma^n)f(\sigma^1,\dots,\sigma^n)\Big].
\]
The expectation is taken \emph{outside} the product of the Gibbs weights, so all replicas see the same disorder. This is why $\nu(f(\sigma^1,\sigma^2))$ is in general not the product of two single-replica averages: $\nu(R_{1,2})=\mathbb{E}\big[N^{-1}\sum_i\langle\sigma_i\rangle^2\big]$, not $N^{-1}\sum_i(\mathbb{E}\langle\sigma_i\rangle)^2$. The number $n$ of replicas is read off from the arguments of $f$; the statements of this section use $n=2$, with the overlap $R_{1,2}=N^{-1}\sum_i\sigma^1_i\sigma^2_i$.

\emph{(3) Which system.} A subscript or a word says which Hamiltonian $H_\omega$ is meant:
\begin{itemize}
\item $\nu$ with no subscript, and $\nu_{\mathrm{SK}}$: the SK model with field $h$, $-H(\sigma)=\frac{\beta}{\sqrt N}\sum_{i<j}g_{ij}\sigma_i\sigma_j+h\sum_i\sigma_i$.
