\item $\nu_{\mathrm{dec}}$: the decoupled model with independent spins in the random fields $\beta\sqrt q\,z_i+h$, $z_i$ standard Gaussian, i.e.\ the endpoint $s=\pi/2$ of the smart path (Definition~\ref{def:sg-tal:smart-path}).
\item $\nu_s$, $s\in[0,\pi/2]$: the smart path at angle $s$, with energy $\cos s\,\frac{\beta}{\sqrt N}\sum_{i<j}g_{ij}\sigma_i\sigma_j+\sin s\,\beta\sqrt q\sum_iz_i\sigma_i+h\sum_i\sigma_i$ and the expectation over both $g$ and $z$. So $\nu_0=\nu_{\mathrm{SK}}$ and $\nu_{\pi/2}=\nu_{\mathrm{dec}}$. In the book's variable $t=\cos^2 s$ this is the average written $\nu_t$ (Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:book-path}); the book's $\nu_t$ with $t=1$ is the SK end.
\end{itemize}
Warning: in the cavity method (Sections~\ref{sec:sg-tal:cavity} and later) $\nu_t$ and $\nu_0$ denote averages along a different interpolation, between the $(N+1)$-spin system and a decoupled last spin; they are not the objects of this section.

\emph{Lean form.} The sentence ``$\nu(\exp sN(R_{1,2}-q)^2)\le\dots$'' is the iterated integral of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-conc}: the outer integral is over the Gaussian couplings $g$ (the expectation $\mathbb{E}$), and the inner integral is over \texttt{Fin 2 $\to$ (Fin N $\to$ units of $\mathbb{Z}$)} with respect to the product of two copies of the Gibbs measure (\texttt{Measure.count.tilted} of the energy, taken twice, \texttt{Measure.pi}). No separate Lean definition of $\nu$ exists: each statement writes the two integrals out.

\emph{Why $\nu$.} The overlap $R_{1,2}$ is a statistic of two replicas, and the claim that it concentrates around $q$ (bounds of the form $\nu((R_{1,2}-q)^2)\le K/N$) says that two independent samples from the same random Gibbs measure have overlap close to the constant $q$, with high probability over both the samples and the disorder. The cavity method and the Gaussian fluctuation principle in the later sections are likewise formulated with such $\nu$-averages.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Deviations from the book in Section~1.4]\label{rem:sg-tal:dev-14}
Every statement below is proved in Lean, but several proofs differ from Talagrand's, and a few statements are weaker or reorganized.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Lemma~1.4.2: trigonometric form, no $\tfrac12$.} The derivative is taken in the trigonometric parameter $s$, with $U(\sigma,\tau)=\mathbb{E}[Y_\sigma Y_\tau]-\mathbb{E}[X_\sigma X_\tau]$ (no factor $1/2$) and prefactor $\sin s\cos s$. Via $t=\cos^2 s$ this is the book's Eq.~(1.90), with the opposite sign because $s=0$ is the SK end. Differentiation under $\mathbb{E}$ is justified explicitly by domination. The lemma is also proved for an observable that depends differentiably on $s$, which is what the compensated functional requires.
\item \emph{Eq.~(1.97) and Corollary~1.4.6 are not separate statements.} The case $n=2$ is expanded inside the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:replica-decoupling}.
\item \emph{Lemma~1.4.5 by Chebyshev's inequality.} The book expands the exponential in a series, applies H\"older's inequality and uses replica symmetry. Lean applies Chebyshev's association inequality $\int a\int b\le\int ab$ for similarly ordered functions to $a=A$, $b=e^{uA}$, separately for each realization of the disorder. Lemma~1.4.5 is not stated on its own as an inequality between $\nu_t$-averages.
\item \emph{Corollary~1.4.7: direction and generality.} The compensated functional is nondecreasing in the trigonometric parameter $s$, equivalently non-increasing in the book's $t=\cos^2 s$. It is proved abstractly for any nonnegative symmetric overlap function $A$ with constant diagonal and $U=-\kappa(A-c)$, and then specialized to $A=(R_{1,2}-q)^2$.
\item \emph{Proposition~1.4.8.} The one-dimensional inequality $(\cosh u+q\sinh u)e^{-qu}\le e^{u^2/2}$ is obtained from Hoeffding's lemma (Mathlib) applied to an explicit two-point law, not from a direct calculus argument. The randomization $u\sim\mathcal N(0,2\lambda/N)$ is carried out as a Fubini exchange with a standard Gaussian, valid because the overlap is bounded.
\item \emph{Theorem~1.4.1 and the moment bounds.} The exponential bound is proved for the SK endpoint. The uniform bound $\nu_s((R_{1,2}-q)^2)\le K/N$ (Eqs.~(1.89), (1.103); notation Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:nu-notation}) is derived from the compensated inequality and $e^x\ge1+x$ with an explicit constant. Eq.~(1.88) for all even moments of the $N$-spin overlap is derived from the exponential bound by the elementary inequality $x^r\le r!\,e^x$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-moments-all}).
\item \emph{Theorem~1.4.10 by monotonicity.} Instead of integrating $\varphi'$ over $[0,\pi/2]$, Lean shows that $\varphi+c\sin^2$ is monotone for two choices of $c$ (using $0\le A\le K/N$), which gives the same two-sided $O(1/N)$ bound without any integrability check. The convergence $p_N\to\operatorname{SK}(\beta,h)$ is derived from this bound directly for the pair disorder with field, not from the thermodynamic limit of Theorem~1.3.9.
\item \emph{Hypotheses.} $q$ is a hypothesis (fixed-point equation), and the book's $\beta<1/2$ is stated as $\beta^2<1/4$.
\end{itemize}
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[General replica derivative under Gaussian interpolation]\label{lem:sg-tal:replica-deriv}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.abs_neg_sin_mul_add_cos_mul_le_sum,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_pi_count_tilted_path,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_pi_count_tilted_path_moving,
        SpinGlasses.aemeasurable_gibbsWeight,
        SpinGlasses.aemeasurable_integral_pi_count_tilted,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_replica_path_moving,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_replica_path,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_integral_pi_count_tilted_mul_eval,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_integral_pi_count_tilted_mul_integral,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_integral_pi_count_tilted_det,
        SpinGlasses.integral_pi_option_count_tilted_marginal,
        SpinGlasses.integral_replica_trig_key,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_replica_trig,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_replica_derivative_term,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_replica_trig_moving}
  \leanok
\talsource{Lemma~1.4.2, Eq.~(1.90); for constant $U(\sigma,\sigma)$ see Eq.~(1.95)}
Let $\alpha$ be a finite nonempty configuration space, $R$ a finite set of $n$ replicas, $(\Omega,P)$ a probability space, and $X, Y : \alpha \to \Omega \to \mathbb{R}$ measurable with $(X_\sigma, Y_\sigma)_\sigma$ jointly Gaussian, centered, and $\mathbb{E}[X_\sigma Y_\tau] = 0$ for all $\sigma,\tau$.
Put $U(\sigma,\tau) = \mathbb{E}[Y_\sigma Y_\tau] - \mathbb{E}[X_\sigma X_\tau]$, let $h : \alpha \to \mathbb{R}$, and let $\langle\cdot\rangle_{s,\omega}$ be the average over $n$ independent replicas of the Gibbs measure with weights $\exp(\cos s\, X_\sigma(\omega) + \sin s\, Y_\sigma(\omega) + h_\sigma)$.
For every observable $\Phi : \alpha^R \to \mathbb{R}$ (Lemma~1.4.2, trigonometric form),
\[
\frac{d}{ds}\mathbb{E}[\langle\Phi\rangle_s]
= \sin s\cos s\;\mathbb{E}[\Big\langle \Phi\Big(\sum_{\ell,\ell'\le n} U(\sigma^\ell,\sigma^{\ell'}) - 2n\sum_{\ell\le n} U(\sigma^\ell,\sigma^{n+1}) - n\,U(\sigma^{n+1},\sigma^{n+1}) + n(n+1)\,U(\sigma^{n+1},\sigma^{n+2})\Big)\Big\rangle_s],
\]
where the right-hand side is a Gibbs average over $n+2$ replicas indexed by $\mathrm{Option}(\mathrm{Option}\,R)$.
More generally, if $\Phi_s$ depends differentiably on $s$ with $|\Phi_s|, |\partial_s\Phi_s|$ bounded uniformly in $s$, then $\frac{d}{ds}\mathbb{E}[\langle\Phi_s\rangle_s]$ is the displayed expression for $\Phi = \Phi_s$ plus $\mathbb{E}[\langle\partial_s\Phi_s\rangle_s]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Differentiate the replica average $\prod_\ell w(\sigma^\ell)/Z^n$ along the path (finite-dimensional calculus, \texttt{fderiv\_integral\_pi\_count\_tilted}) and pass the derivative inside $\mathbb{E}$ by dominated differentiation (the path derivative is dominated by $\sum_\sigma |X_\sigma|+|Y_\sigma|$, which is integrable for a Gaussian field; this uniform bound $|-\sin s\,x_\sigma+\cos s\,y_\sigma|\le\sum_{\tau}(|x_\tau|+|y_\tau|)$ is a separate lemma, used in all three derivative statements and in the Slepian comparison).
This gives $\mathbb{E}[\langle\Phi(\sum_\ell u'(\sigma^\ell) - n\langle u'\rangle)\rangle]$ with $u'=-\sin s\,X+\cos s\,Y$.
Two Gaussian integrations by parts (\texttt{integral\_gibbs\_replica\_average\_gaussian\_option}, applied to $X$ and to $Y$) turn this into the displayed covariance combination, with $\mathbb{E}[X Y] = 0$ killing the cross terms; the extra replicas $n+1,n+2$ appear exactly as in Panchenko's Eq.~(1.29), and a marginalization lemma removes the replicas on which the observable does not depend.
In the parametrization $t = \cos^2 s$ (with $t=1$ the SK end) this is Talagrand's Eq.~(1.90); Eq.~(1.97) for $n=2$ is the specialization used in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:replica-decoupling}.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/GaussianInterpolationReplica.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Replica decoupling]\label{lem:sg-tal:replica-decoupling}
  \lean{MeasureTheory.integral_mul_integral_le_integral_mul,
        MeasureTheory.integral_comp_mul_integral_comp_le,
        MeasureTheory.integral_pi_fin_two,
        SpinGlasses.replica_decoupling_comb_nonneg}
  \leanok
\talsource{Lemma~1.4.5, Eq.~(1.98); Corollary~1.4.6, Eq.~(1.100); the $n=2$ derivative is Eq.~(1.97)}
Let $\mu$ be a Gibbs measure on a finite set $\alpha$, $A : \alpha\times\alpha\to[0,\infty)$ symmetric with constant diagonal, $U(\sigma,\tau) = -\kappa (A(\sigma,\tau) - c)$ with $\kappa\ge0$, and $u\ge 0$.
Let $\mu=\mu_f$ be the Gibbs measure of an energy $f:\alpha\to\mathbb R$, i.e.\ $\mu(\sigma)\propto e^{f(\sigma)}$, and $\Phi(\sigma^1,\sigma^2) = \exp(u A(\sigma^1,\sigma^2))$. Writing $\tau=(\sigma^1,\sigma^2,\sigma^3,\sigma^4)$ for the four replicas indexed by $\mathrm{Option}(\mathrm{Option}\,\mathrm{Fin}\,2)$, the combination of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:replica-deriv} (for $n=2$) and the moving-observable term for $u'=8\kappa\sin s\cos s$ is nonnegative:
\[
\int \Phi(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)\Big(\sum_{\ell,\ell'\le2} U(\sigma^\ell,\sigma^{\ell'}) - 4\sum_{\ell\le2} U(\sigma^\ell,\sigma^{3}) - 2\,U(\sigma^{3},\sigma^{3}) + 6\,U(\sigma^{3},\sigma^{4})\Big)\,d\mu^{\otimes4} + 8\kappa\int A(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)\,\Phi(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)\,d\mu^{\otimes2} \ge 0 .
\]
(For $A=(R_{1,2}-q)^2$ this contains the book's Lemma~1.4.5 and Corollary~1.4.6; see Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:dev-14}.)
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
\emph{Differs from the book:} Talagrand proves Lemma~1.4.5 by expanding the exponential, H\"older's inequality and replica symmetry.
The Lean proof is shorter and works pointwise in the disorder: since $U$ is $-\kappa(A-c)$ with $A$ symmetric and of constant diagonal, the four-replica combination collapses to $-\kappa(2A_{12}-4A_{13}-4A_{23}+6A_{34})$ (the constants $c$ and the diagonal values cancel because the coefficients sum to zero).
The terms with $A_{13}, A_{23}$ are integrals of nonnegative functions; the term with $A_{34}$ factors by independence of the replicas $3,4$ from $1,2$ into $\langle\Phi\rangle\langle A\rangle$, and Chebyshev's association inequality (for two similarly ordered functions, $\int a\int b\le\int ab$; here $a=A$ and $b=e^{uA}$ with $u\ge0$) gives $\langle A\rangle\langle e^{uA}\rangle\le\langle A e^{uA}\rangle$.
Hence the combination is at least $\kappa(6\langle\Phi A\rangle - 6\langle\Phi\rangle\langle A\rangle)\ge0$.
\emph{Formalization note.} Lemma~1.4.5 is not stated on its own as an inequality between $\nu_t$-averages; only the combination above (Lemma~1.4.2 for $n=2$ plus the moving-observable term), which is what Corollary~\ref{cor:sg-tal:compensated-mono} uses, is a Lean statement.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKLatalaCompensated.lean} (Chebyshev's inequality in \texttt{ForMathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/ChebyshevAssociation.lean}).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Compensated functional monotonicity]\label{cor:sg-tal:compensated-mono}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_sk_smart_path_fields,
        SpinGlasses.monotoneOn_compensated_functional,
        SpinGlasses.sk_compensated_path_le_decoupled,
        SpinGlasses.sk_compensated_le_decoupled}
  \leanok
\talsource{Corollary~1.4.7, Eq.~(1.101)}
Abstract form: under the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:replica-deriv} with covariance difference $U=-\kappa(A-c)$ as in Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:replica-decoupling} and $u_0\ge4\kappa$, the function
$s\mapsto\mathbb{E}[\langle\exp((u_0-4\kappa\cos^2 s)A(\sigma^1,\sigma^2))\rangle_s]$ is nondecreasing on $[0,\pi/2]$.
SK form (Corollary~1.4.7 in the endpoint version used for Theorem~1.4.1): for $N\ge1$, $q\ge0$, $s\ge0$,
\[
\nu_{\mathrm{SK}}\big(\exp sN(R_{1,2}-q)^2\big) \le \nu_{\mathrm{dec}}\big(\exp (s+2\beta^2) N (R_{1,2}-q)^2\big),
\]
where (notation of Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:nu-notation}) $\nu_{\mathrm{SK}}$ is the disorder-averaged two-replica Gibbs average of the SK model with field $h$ and $\nu_{\mathrm{dec}}$ that of the decoupled model with fields $\beta\sqrt q\, z_i + h$.
Path form: for $\lambda\ge2\beta^2$ and $s\in[0,\pi/2]$, the average of $\exp\big(N(\lambda-2\beta^2\cos^2 s)(R_{1,2}-q)^2\big)$ under the smart-path Gibbs measure at time $s$ is at most $\nu_{\mathrm{dec}}(\exp \lambda N (R_{1,2}-q)^2)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:replica-deriv} (moving-observable form, $\Phi_s=\exp((u_0-4\kappa\cos^2s)A)$, $\partial_s u = 8\kappa\sin s\cos s$) the derivative of the functional is $\sin s\cos s$ times the nonnegative quantity of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:replica-decoupling} (note $u_0-4\kappa\cos^2 s\ge0$), so it is nonnegative on $[0,\pi/2]$.
For the SK model take $X$ the pair field and $Y$ the cavity field (\texttt{exists\_sk\_smart\_path\_fields} packages their Gaussianity and Eq.~(1.64)), $A=(R-q)^2$, $\kappa=N\beta^2/2$, $c=q^2+1/N$, $u_0=N(s+2\beta^2)$, so $u_0-4\kappa=Ns$; the endpoints $s=0$ and $s=\pi/2$ are the SK and decoupled models.
The path version compares the smart-path energy at any time $s\in[0,\pi/2]$ with the decoupled endpoint, which gives the uniform moment bounds of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-conc}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Base case at $t = 0$]\label{prop:sg-tal:base-case}
  \lean{ProbabilityTheory.cosh_add_mul_sinh_nonneg,
        ProbabilityTheory.cosh_add_mul_sinh_mul_exp_neg_le,
        ProbabilityTheory.gaussianPDFReal_zero_one_mul_exp_sq,
        ProbabilityTheory.integral_exp_mul_sq_gaussianReal,
        ProbabilityTheory.integrable_exp_mul_sq_gaussianReal,
        SpinGlasses.integral_exp_sq_le_of_mgf_le,
        SpinGlasses.sum_sum_exp_overlap_site,
        SpinGlasses.sum_sum_decoupled_overlap_exp,
        SpinGlasses.integral_decoupled_pair_exp,
        SpinGlasses.decoupled_field_eq,
        SpinGlasses.integral_decoupled_pair_exp_le,
        SpinGlasses.integral_decoupled_exp_overlap_sq_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.4.8, Eq.~(1.102), proved via Eqs.~(1.104)--(1.107)}
Let $N\ge1$, $\beta,h\in\mathbb R$, and let $q$ satisfy the fixed-point equation $q = \mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt q\,z+h)]$ (so $q\in[0,1]$).
For the decoupled model (non-interacting spins with fields $\beta\sqrt q\, z_i + h$, two independent replicas) and every $0 \le \lambda < 1/2$ (Proposition~1.4.8):
\[
\mathbb{E}[\big\langle \exp\big(\lambda N (R_{1,2} - q)^2\big)\big\rangle] \le \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - 2\lambda}} .
\]
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
\emph{Step 1 (one site).} For $\varepsilon=\pm1$ with $\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon]=q$, Hoeffding's lemma (Mathlib, applied to the explicit two-point law) gives $(\cosh u + q\sinh u)e^{-qu}=\mathbb{E}[e^{u(\varepsilon-q)}]\le e^{u^2/2}$.
\emph{Step 2 (replicas factorize).} For fixed fields $Y_i$, summing first over $\sigma^2$ and then over $\sigma^1$ with the hypercube factorization gives
$\langle\exp(u\sum_i(\sigma^1_i\sigma^2_i-q))\rangle=\prod_i e^{-uq}(\cosh u+\tanh^2(Y_i)\sinh u)$, because $\sum_{s,t=\pm1}e^{(s+t)y+u(st-q)}=4\cosh^2 y\;e^{-uq}(\cosh u+\tanh^2 y\sinh u)$.
\emph{Step 3 (disorder).} The coordinates $z_i$ are independent, so the expectation over $z$ is the $N$-th power of $e^{-uq}(\cosh u+\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(Y)]\sinh u)=e^{-uq}(\cosh u+q\sinh u)$, which is at most $e^{Nu^2/2}$ by Step~1 (Eq.~(1.105)).
\emph{Step 4 (Gaussian linearization).} For $X=N(R_{1,2}-q)$ one has $\mathbb{E}[\langle e^{uX}\rangle]\le e^{Nu^2/2}$ for all $u$; writing $e^{\theta X^2/2}=\mathbb{E}_g[e^{\sqrt\theta\, g X}]$ with $g\sim\mathcal N(0,1)$, exchanging expectations (Fubini; $X$ is bounded) and using the bound with $u=\sqrt\theta g$ gives $\mathbb{E}[\langle e^{\theta X^2/2}\rangle]\le\mathbb{E}_g[e^{N\theta g^2/2}]=(1-N\theta)^{-1/2}$.
With $\theta=2\lambda/N$ this is the claim.
\emph{Formalization note.} The fixed-point equation is a hypothesis; only existence of a solution is formalized (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point}, Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:fixed-point-scope}).
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKLatalaBase.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exponential overlap concentration]\label{thm:sg-tal:overlap-conc}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_overlap_concentration_exp,
        MeasureTheory.one_add_mul_integral_le_integral,
        MeasureTheory.one_add_mul_integral_le_integral_exp,
        SpinGlasses.sk_path_expected_sq_overlap_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{Theorem~1.4.1, Eq.~(1.87); moment bounds, Eqs.~(1.89) and (1.103)}
Let $N\ge1$, $\beta,h\in\mathbb R$ and let $q=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt q\,z+h)]$. Here $\nu(f)=\mathbb{E}\langle f\rangle$ is the disorder average of the two-replica Gibbs average and $\nu_s$ that along the smart path (see Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:nu-notation}).
For $s\ge0$ with $2s+4\beta^2<1$ (Theorem~1.4.1):
\[
\nu\left(\exp s N (R_{1,2} - q)^2\right) \le \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - 2s - 4\beta^2}} .
\]
Moreover, if $\beta^2<1/4$ then for every $s\in[0,\pi/2]$ the expected squared overlap under the smart-path energy at time $s$ satisfies (Eqs.~(1.89) and (1.103))
\[
\nu_s\big((R_{1,2}-q)^2\big)\le \frac{K}{N},\qquad K=\frac{(1/2-2\beta^2)^{-1/2}-1}{1/4-\beta^2}.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Apply Corollary~\ref{cor:sg-tal:compensated-mono} with $\lambda=s+2\beta^2<1/2$ and then Proposition~\ref{prop:sg-tal:base-case}:
$\nu(\exp sN(R_{1,2}-q)^2)\le\nu_{\mathrm{dec}}(\exp\lambda N(R_{1,2}-q)^2)\le(1-2\lambda)^{-1/2}=(1-2s-4\beta^2)^{-1/2}$.
For the second claim take $\lambda=1/4+\beta^2$ and $s\in[0,\pi/2]$.
The compensated inequality at time $s$ gives $\nu_s(\exp(aN(R_{1,2}-q)^2))\le(1-2\lambda)^{-1/2}$ with $a=\lambda-2\beta^2\cos^2s\ge 1/4-\beta^2$.
From $e^x\ge1+x$ (packaged as the general inequality $1+c\,\mathbb{E}[X]\le\mathbb{E}[e^{cX}]$ on a probability space, applied first to the Gibbs average for each disorder and then to the disorder average) we get $1+aN\,\nu_t((R_{1,2}-q)^2)\le(1-2\lambda)^{-1/2}$, hence $\nu_s((R_{1,2}-q)^2)\le K/N$.
\emph{Formalization notes.} (i) The fixed-point equation $q=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt q\,z+h)]$ is a hypothesis of both statements; only the existence of a solution is proved (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point}).
(ii) The book's assumption $\beta<1/2$ is stated as $\beta^2<1/4$ (in the first claim it is implied by $2s+4\beta^2<1$ with $s\ge0$).
(iii) Eq.~(1.88), the bound on all even moments, is a consequence of the exponential bound and is proved separately as Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-moments-all}; the constant there is $r!\,s^{-r}(\cdot)^{-1/2}$ rather than the book's $(Kk/N)^k$.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKOverlapConcentration.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A pointwise inequality for the exponential]\label{lem:sg-tal:pow-le-exp}
  \lean{Real.pow_le_factorial_mul_exp}
  \leanok
\talsource{no counterpart in the book (the elementary step behind Eq.~(1.88))}
For every real $y\ge0$ and every $r\in\mathbb N$, $y^r\le r!\,e^y$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
A single term $y^r/r!$ of the series $\sum_n y^n/n!=e^y$ with nonnegative terms is at most its sum (Mathlib's \texttt{Real.pow\_div\_factorial\_le\_exp}).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[All even moments of the overlap (Eq.~(1.88))]\label{thm:sg-tal:overlap-moments-all}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_overlap_moment_le_of_exp,
        SpinGlasses.sk_overlap_moment_le_of_exp_unif}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.88)}
Let $\beta,h\in\mathbb R$ with $4\beta^2<1$ and let $q$ satisfy $q=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt q\,z+h)]$. For every $r\in\mathbb N$ there is $K>0$ such that for every $N\ge1$, in the $N$-spin SK model at inverse temperature $\beta$ and field $h$ (with $\nu$ as in Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:nu-notation}: disorder average of the two-replica Gibbs average),
\[
\nu\big((R_{1,2}-q)^{2r}\big)=\mathbb{E}\big\langle(R_{1,2}-q)^{2r}\big\rangle\le\frac{K}{N^{r}},\qquad
K=r!\,s^{-r}\,(1-2s-4\beta^2)^{-1/2},\quad s=\frac{1-4\beta^2}{4},
\]
so that $K=r!\,\bigl(\tfrac{4}{1-4\beta^2}\bigr)^{r}\bigl(\tfrac{2}{1-4\beta^2}\bigr)^{1/2}$ does not depend on $N$ (it is the book's ``$K$ depends on $r$ and $\beta$ only'').
\emph{Uniform version.}  Let $\beta_0$ satisfy $4\beta_0^2<1$ and $r\in\mathbb N$.  The same constant with $\beta$ replaced by $\beta_0$, $K_0=r!\,s_0^{-r}(1-2s_0-4\beta_0^2)^{-1/2}$, $s_0=\frac{1-4\beta_0^2}{4}$, works for \emph{every} $\beta$ with $\beta^2\le\beta_0^2$, every $h$, and every fixed point $q$ at $\beta$: $\nu\big((R_{1,2}-q)^{2r}\big)\le K_0/N^r$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Put $X=(R_{1,2}-q)^2\ge0$. Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:pow-le-exp} with $y=sNX$ gives the pointwise bound $X^r\le r!\,(sN)^{-r}e^{sNX}$. Integrate against the Gibbs measure of two replicas and then against the disorder; the integral of the right-hand side is controlled by Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-conc}: $\nu(\exp sN(R_{1,2}-q)^2)\le(1-2s-4\beta^2)^{-1/2}$, which applies because $2s+4\beta^2=(1+4\beta^2)/2<1$. Since $(R_{1,2}-q)^{2r}=X^r$ the claim follows.
For the uniform version use $s_0$ in place of $s$: $2s_0+4\beta^2\le2s_0+4\beta_0^2<1$ so Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-conc} still applies, and its bound $(1-2s_0-4\beta^2)^{-1/2}$ is at most $(1-2s_0-4\beta_0^2)^{-1/2}$ because $\beta^2\le\beta_0^2$; nothing else in the argument depends on $\beta$, $h$ or $q$.  The case $\beta_0=|\beta|$ is the first statement.
\emph{Formalization note.} The statement is for the genuine $N$-spin system with the same normalization as Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-conc}, not for the $(N+1)$-spin system with $\beta_+$ of Part~D of Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:cavity}; the latter (Proposition~1.6.7, Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-moments}) concerns the $(N+1)$-spin system and assumes the stronger smallness condition $16\beta^2e^{64\beta^2}\le\tfrac12$, whereas Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:fluct} needs the $N$-spin system for every $4\beta^2<1$, which is why it uses this theorem.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKOverlapConcentration.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The Aizenman--Lebowitz--Ruelle high-temperature free energy formula]\label{thm:sg-tal:alr}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.ge_endpoint_of_trig_square_deriv,
        SpinGlasses.exists_sk_smart_path_profile,
        SpinGlasses.sk_free_energy_sub_rs_le,
        SpinGlasses.tendsto_sk_free_energy_of_lt}
  \leanok
\talsource{Theorem~1.4.10, Eq.~(1.108)}
For $\beta^2<1/4$ and $q=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt q\,z+h)]$ (Theorem~1.4.10):
\[
\Big|p_N(\beta, h) - \Big(\log2+\mathbb{E}[\log\cosh(\beta z\sqrt q+h)]+\tfrac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q)^2\Big)\Big| \le \frac{\beta^2}{4}\cdot\frac{(1/2-2\beta^2)^{-1/2}-1}{(1/4-\beta^2)\,N}.
\]
Consequently $p_N(\beta,h)\to \log2+\mathbb{E}[\log\cosh(\beta z\sqrt q+h)]+\frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q)^2$ as $N\to\infty$ (the book calls this limit $\operatorname{SK}(\beta,h)$; Lean does not define it, see Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:fixed-point-scope}).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Let $\varphi(s)=N^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\log Z_s]$ along the trigonometric smart path.
By Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:guerra-rs-bound} (profile lemma) $\varphi'(s)=\frac{\beta^2}{2}\sin s\cos s\,(A(s)-(1-q)^2)$ with $A(s)=\nu_s((R_{1,2}-q)^2)\ge0$ (the smart-path average of Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:nu-notation}), $\varphi(0)=p_N$ and $\varphi(\pi/2)=\log 2+\mathbb{E}[\log\cosh(\beta z\sqrt q+h)]$.
Since $0\le A(s)\le K'/N$ on $[0,\pi/2]$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-conc}, the functions $\varphi(s)+\frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q)^2\sin^2 s$ (nondecreasing) and $\varphi(s)+\frac{\beta^2}{4}((1-q)^2-K'/N)\sin^2 s$ (nonincreasing) give $\varphi(0)\le\varphi(\pi/2)+\frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q)^2$ and $\varphi(0)\ge\varphi(\pi/2)+\frac{\beta^2}{4}((1-q)^2-K'/N)$, i.e.\ the claim.
\emph{Formalization note.} This is done by monotonicity instead of integrating the derivative (same bound, no integrability to check).
As in Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-conc}, $q$ is a hypothesis (fixed-point equation) and $\beta<1/2$ is stated as $\beta^2<1/4$.
\emph{Not formalized:} the identification of the solution $q$ with the minimizer of Guerra's bound and the uniqueness of the fixed point for $\beta<1$ and $h=0$ (it is proved for $h\ne0$, Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point-unique}); the thermodynamic limit of the Guerra--Toninelli theorem is stated there for the full-matrix disorder without field, while the limit here is derived directly from the $O(1/N)$ bound for the pair disorder with field.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKAizenmanLebowitzRuelle.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The SK energy is a linear form of the disorder]\label{lem:sg-tal:sk-linear-data}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_sk_linear_data}
  \leanok
\talsource{implicit in Eq.~(1.113); no displayed counterpart}
For every $N\ge1$ and $\beta,h\in\mathbb R$ there are coefficient vectors $a(\sigma)\in\mathbb R^{\{i<j\}}$, $\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^N$, such that for every disorder $g=(g_{ij})_{i<j}$
\[
-H_N(\sigma)=\beta N^{-1/2}\sum_{i<j}g_{ij}\sigma_i\sigma_j+h\sum_i\sigma_i=\langle a(\sigma),g\rangle+h\sum_i\sigma_i,\qquad \langle a(\sigma),a(\sigma)\rangle\le \tfrac12\beta^2N ,
\]
the exact value of the squared norm being $\beta^2(N-1)/2$, the variance of the pair field at $\sigma$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Take $a(\sigma)_{ij}=\beta N^{-1/2}\sigma_i\sigma_j$. Every coordinate squares to $\beta^2/N$ because $\sigma_i^2=\sigma_j^2=1$, and there are $N(N-1)/2$ pairs.
This replaces a hand-written computation of the same kind that was inside the two-replica data of Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:beyond-at}; that statement (\texttt{exists\_sk\_coupled\_data}) is now derived from this lemma with $a(\sigma,\tau)=a(\sigma)+a(\tau)$ and $\langle a+a',a+a'\rangle\le2(\langle a,a\rangle+\langle a',a'\rangle)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Moments of the free energy fluctuation (Lemma~1.4.12, Eq.~(1.113))]\label{thm:sg-tal:free-energy-moments}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.hasSubgaussianMGF_log_sum_exp_tilt,
        SpinGlasses.integral_pow_abs_free_energy_sub_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{Lemma~1.4.12, Eq.~(1.113)}
Let $\beta,h\in\mathbb R$, $k\in\mathbb N$, $N\ge1$, $A_N=N^{-1}\log Z_N$ and $p_N=\mathbb{E}A_N$ for the $N$-spin SK model with deterministic field $h$ (disorder $g_{ij}$, $i<j$, i.i.d.\ standard Gaussian). Then $|A_N-p_N|^k$ is integrable and
\[
\sqrt N^{\,k}\;\mathbb{E}\,|A_N-p_N|^k\;\le\;2\,k!\,e^{\beta^2/2},\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad \mathbb{E}\,|A_N-p_N|^k\le \frac{K_k}{N^{k/2}},\ K_k=2k!\,e^{\beta^2/2}.
\]
The constant depends only on $k$ and $|\beta|$, not on $h$ or $N$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:sk-linear-data} the free energy $\log Z_N=\log\sum_\sigma\exp(\langle a(\sigma),g\rangle+h\sum_i\sigma_i)$ is the log-partition function of a centered Gaussian field of variance at most $v=\beta^2N/2$. The Gaussian concentration engine (\texttt{hasSubgaussianMGF\_log\_sum\_exp}, the symmetrization argument of Panchenko's Theorem~1.2) says that $\log Z_N-\mathbb{E}\log Z_N$ is sub-Gaussian with parameter $2v=\beta^2N$, hence $\mathbb{E}\exp(t|\log Z_N-\mathbb{E}\log Z_N|)\le2\exp(\beta^2Nt^2/2)$; for $t=N^{-1/2}$ this is at most $2e^{\beta^2/2}$. Now $\sqrt N\,|A_N-p_N|=N^{-1/2}|\log Z_N-\mathbb{E}\log Z_N|$ and $y^k\le k!\,e^y$ for $y\ge0$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:pow-le-exp}), so $\sqrt N^{\,k}|A_N-p_N|^k\le k!\,\exp(N^{-1/2}|\log Z_N-\mathbb{E}\log Z_N|)$; integrate.
\emph{Deviation from the book.} The book proves (1.113) by integrating its tail bound (1.114) against $kt^{k-1}\,dt$; we bound the exponential moment instead, which gives the same $N^{-k/2}$ scaling with the explicit constant $2k!e^{\beta^2/2}$, and avoids a Gamma-function integral. Only the deterministic field ($c=0$ in the book's notation $h_i=cy_i+d$) is formalized; the Gaussian random field is not.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKFreeEnergyMoments.lean}; the shared sub-Gaussian statement for a Gaussian field tilted by a deterministic observable (also used for the tail bound in Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:beyond-at}) is \texttt{SpinGlasses.hasSubgaussianMGF\_log\_sum\_exp\_tilt}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A smooth function of the free energy along the smart path (Eq.~(1.125))]\label{thm:sg-tal:profile-comp}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.freeEnergyCompHessian,
        SpinGlasses.fderiv_comp_log_sum_exp_single,
        SpinGlasses.fderiv_fderiv_comp_log_sum_exp_single,
        SpinGlasses.abs_hessian_comp_log_sum_exp_le,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_freeEnergyCompHessian,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_comp_log_sum_exp_trig,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_comp_log_sum_exp_trig_integral,
        SpinGlasses.sum_hessian_comp_eq,
        SpinGlasses.sum_freeEnergyCompHessian_eq,
        SpinGlasses.exists_sk_smart_path_profile_comp,
        SpinGlasses.sub_pow_growth,
        SpinGlasses.exists_sk_smart_path_profile_pow}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.125), proof of Theorem~1.4.11}
Let $N\ge1$, $q\ge0$, $A_t=N^{-1}\log\sum_\sigma\exp(\cos t\,X_\sigma+\sin t\,Y_\sigma+h\sum_i\sigma_i)$ be the free energy along the smart path (SK pair field $X$, cavity field $Y$), $Q_t=\langle(R_{1,2}-q)^2\rangle_t$ and $W$ a $C^2$ function such that $W,W',W''$ have at most exponential growth. Then $\psi(t)=\mathbb{E}\,W(A_t)$ is differentiable and
\[
\psi'(t)=\frac{\beta^2}{2}\sin t\cos t\;\mathbb{E}\Big[W'(A_t)\big(Q_t-(1-q)^2\big)-\frac1N\,W''(A_t)\big(Q_t-q^2-\tfrac1N\big)\Big],
\]
with $\psi(0)=\mathbb{E}\,W(A_N)$ (SK model) and $\psi(\pi/2)=\mathbb{E}\,W\big(\log2+N^{-1}\sum_i\log\cosh(\beta\sqrt q\,z_i+h)\big)$ (decoupled model). For $W(x)=(x-a)^k$ this reads
\[
\psi'(t)=\frac{\beta^2}{2}\sin t\cos t\;\mathbb{E}\Big[k(A_t-a)^{k-1}\big(Q_t-(1-q)^2\big)-\frac{k(k-1)}N(A_t-a)^{k-2}\big(Q_t-q^2-\tfrac1N\big)\Big].
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Put $L(y)=\log\sum_\sigma e^{y_\sigma+h_\sigma}$, $n=N^{-1}$ and $F_W(y)=W(nL(y))$. With the Gibbs weights $w_\sigma$, $\partial_lF_W=nW'(nL)w_l$ and
$\partial_k\partial_lF_W=n^2W''(nL)w_lw_k+nW'(nL)w_l(\mathbf 1_{l=k}-w_k)=:H_{lk}$ (\texttt{freeEnergyCompHessian}); the bound $|H_{lk}|\le C e^{c\|y\|_1}$ follows from $|L(y)|\le|L(0)|+\|y\|_1$ and the growth of $W',W''$. The generic Gaussian interpolation formula (\texttt{hasDerivAt\_integral\_comp\_trig}, Gaussian integration by parts) gives $\psi'(t)=\sin t\cos t\,\sum_{l,k}(\mathbb{E}Y_lY_k-\mathbb{E}X_lX_k)\,\mathbb{E}H_{lk}$; the entries are integrable, so the expectation can be moved outside the sum. By the covariance computation of the smart path (Eq.~(1.64)), $\mathbb{E}Y_\sigma Y_\tau-\mathbb{E}X_\sigma X_\tau=-\frac{N\beta^2}{2}\big((R(\sigma,\tau)-q)^2-q^2-\frac1N\big)$, and since $R(\sigma,\sigma)=1$ and $\sum_\sigma w_\sigma=1$ the double sum equals $\frac{\beta^2}{2}\big[W'(A)(Q-(1-q)^2)-\frac1NW''(A)(Q-q^2-\frac1N)\big]$ (\texttt{sum\_hessian\_comp\_eq}). The endpoints use $\cos0=1,\sin0=0$ and, at $\pi/2$, the factorization $\log Z=N\log2+\sum_i\log\cosh(\beta\sqrt q z_i+h)$ of the decoupled system (\texttt{log\_decoupled\_partition\_function\_eq}). For $W(x)=(x-a)^k$ the growth bounds follow from $y^m\le m!e^y$ (\texttt{sub\_pow\_growth}).
\emph{Remarks.} For $W=\mathrm{id}$ the second term vanishes and this is the profile used for Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:alr}; that profile is proved by a different route (Gaussian integration by parts on Gibbs averages), which does not apply to nonlinear $W$. The book writes the path with $t\mapsto\sin^2t$; the formula above is in the angle, and the conversion is a change of variable that is not yet done.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/GaussianInterpolationNonlinear.lean} (generic) and \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKSmartPathProfileComp.lean} (SK).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Even overlap moments along the whole smart path (Eqs.~(1.88), (1.103))]\label{thm:sg-tal:path-overlap-moments}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_path_expected_exp_overlap_le,
        SpinGlasses.sk_path_expected_pow_overlap_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.88), (1.103); the fourth moment is used in Eq.~(1.121)}
Let $\beta^2<1/4$, $q=\mathbb{E}\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt q\,z+h)$ and $t\in[0,\pi/2]$. With $a_t=\frac14+\beta^2-2\beta^2\cos^2t\ge\frac14-\beta^2$,
\[
\nu_t\big(\exp(N a_t(R_{1,2}-q)^2)\big)\le(\tfrac12-2\beta^2)^{-1/2},\qquad
\nu_t\big((R_{1,2}-q)^{2r}\big)\le \frac{r!\,(\frac12-2\beta^2)^{-1/2}}{(\frac14-\beta^2)^r\,N^r}\quad(r\in\mathbb N).
\]
In particular the fourth moment ($r=2$) is at most $K/N^2$ uniformly in $t$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The first bound is the compensated comparison of the path with the decoupled system (\texttt{sk\_compensated\_path\_le\_decoupled}) followed by the decoupled base case (Proposition~1.4.8) with $\lambda=\frac14+\beta^2$. For the second, $y^r\le r!e^y$ with $y=Na_tX$, $X=(R_{1,2}-q)^2$, gives $X^r\le r!(Na_t)^{-r}e^{Na_tX}\le r!\,((\frac14-\beta^2)N)^{-r}e^{Na_tX}$; integrate.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKPathOverlapMoments.lean}. The endpoint version at $t=0$ is Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-moments-all}; the second moment along the path was already used in Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:alr}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Centered moments along the smart path with the moving center $\mathrm{SK}(s)$ (Eqs.~(1.124)--(1.126))]\label{thm:sg-tal:centered-profile}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_sk_smart_path_profile_centered,
        Finset.sum_choose_mul_deriv_pow_zero,
        MeasureTheory.integrable_sub_pow_of_integrable_abs_pow,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_smart_path_free_energy_sub_pow,
        SpinGlasses.measurable_smart_path_free_energy}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proof of Theorem~1.4.11, Eqs.~(1.124)--(1.126)}
Let $N>0$, $0\le q\le1$, $A_s$ the free energy of the smart path, $Q_s=\langle(R_{1,2}-q)^2\rangle_s$, $V_s=A_s-\mathrm{SK}(s)$ with $\mathrm{SK}(s)=\log 2+\mathbb E\log\cosh Y+\frac{\beta^2}4(1-q)^2\cos^2s$. Then $s\mapsto\mathbb E V_s^k$ has at every $t$ the derivative
$\frac{\beta^2}2\sin t\cos t\big(\mathbb E[kV^{k-1}(Q-(1-q)^2)-N^{-1}k(k-1)V^{k-2}(Q-q^2-N^{-1})]+k(1-q)^2\,\mathbb E V^{k-1}\big)$;
its value at $s=0$ is the $k$-th moment of $A_N-\mathrm{RS}(q)$ in the SK model and at $s=\pi/2$ that of the decoupled system.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Eq.~(1.125) (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:profile-comp}) with the fixed center $a_0=\mathrm{SK}(t)$ gives $\partial_t\varphi(t,a_0)$. Write $(A_s-\mathrm{SK}(s))^k=\sum_i\binom ki z(s)^i(A_s-a_0)^{k-i}$ with $z(s)=a_0-\mathrm{SK}(s)$, $z(t)=0$; at $s=t$ only the terms $i=0,1$ have a nonzero derivative, and $z'(t)=\frac{\beta^2}2(1-q)^2\sin t\cos t=-\mathrm{SK}'(t)$ (\texttt{sum\_choose\_mul\_deriv\_pow\_zero}). Integrability of all powers is Lemma~1.4.12 along the path (\texttt{integrable\_smart\_path\_free\_energy\_sub\_pow}).
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKCenteredPathMoments.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The differential equation of the centered moments, Eq.~(1.120)]\label{thm:sg-tal:centered-ode}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_sk_centered_path_ode,
        ProbabilityTheory.abs_integral_pow_mul_le,
        SpinGlasses.abs_remainder_combination_le,
        SpinGlasses.abs_integral_overlap_sq_le_four,
        SpinGlasses.sq_integral_overlap_sq_le,
        SpinGlasses.aemeasurable_gibbs_overlap_sq,
        SpinGlasses.integral_gibbs_overlap_sq_sq_le,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sq_integral_pi_count_tilted_le,
        ProbabilityTheory.abs_integral_mul_le_weighted_add,
        ProbabilityTheory.abs_integral_mul_le_of_integral_sq_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proof of Theorem~1.4.11, $\psi'(t)=\mathrm I+\mathrm{II}$ and $\mathrm{II}=O(k+1)$}
Let $\beta^2<1/4$, $q=\mathbb E\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt q z+h)$ and $\psi_k(s)=\mathbb E(A_N(s)-\mathrm{SK}(s))^k$. There are constants $K_m$, depending only on $\beta$, such that for every $N>0$: $\psi_0=1$; for $m\ge1$ and every $s$ there is $D$ with $\psi_m'(s)=\sin s\cos s\big(\frac{m(m-1)}2N^{-1}\beta^2q^2\psi_{m-2}(s)+D\big)$ and $|D|\le K_mN^{-(m+1)/2}$ if $s\in[0,\pi/2]$; $\psi_k(0)$ is the $k$-th moment of $A_N-\mathrm{rsFunctional}$ in the SK model and $\psi_k(\pi/2)$ that of $N^{-1}\sum_i(\log\cosh Y_i-\mathbb E\log\cosh Y)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
By Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:centered-profile}, the $(1-q)^2$ terms cancel and the derivative is $\frac{\beta^2}2\sin s\cos s\,[m\,\mathbb E V^{m-1}Q-\frac{m(m-1)}N\mathbb E V^{m-2}Q+\frac{m(m-1)}N(q^2+\frac1N)\mathbb E V^{m-2}]$; the $q^2$ term is $\mathrm I$. For $\mathrm{II}$: $|\mathbb E V^{m-1}Q|\le(\mathbb E V^{2(m-1)})^{1/2}(\mathbb E Q^2)^{1/2}$ (Cauchy--Schwarz with explicit scales), $\mathbb E V^{2j}\le K_{2j}N^{-j}$ (Lemma~1.4.12 and Theorem~1.4.10 along the path, \texttt{integral\_pow\_abs\_smart\_path\_V\_le}) and $\mathbb E Q^2\le\mathbb E\langle(R_{1,2}-q)^4\rangle\le C/N^2$ (Jensen for the Gibbs measure and Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:path-overlap-moments}, $r=2$). The other two terms carry an extra factor $N^{-1}$.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKCenteredPathODE.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Moments of an i.i.d.\ centered sum (Eq.~(1.122))]\label{thm:sg-tal:iid-moments}
  \lean{ProbabilityTheory.iid_sum_moment_expansion,
        ProbabilityTheory.abs_integral_pow_avg_sub_le,
        ProbabilityTheory.integral_pow_sum_pi_succ,
        ProbabilityTheory.integrable_pow_sum_pi,
        pow_succ_sub_pow_sub_mul_le,
        ProbabilityTheory.exists_abs_cancellation_le,
        ProbabilityTheory.abs_le_of_abs_sub_le_sqrt_pow}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proof of Theorem~1.4.11, Eq.~(1.122)}
Let $\nu$ be a probability measure on a measurable space $\Omega$, $f:\Omega\to\mathbb R$ with all powers integrable and $\int f\,d\nu=0$, $\sigma^2=\int f^2d\nu$, and $a(0)=1$, $a(1)=0$, $a(k+2)=(k+1)a(k)$. For every $m\ge1$ there is $C$ such that for all $N>0$: $\big|\int(N^{-1}\sum_{i\le N}f(y_i))^m\,d\nu^{\otimes N}-N^{-m/2}a(m)\sigma^{2\lfloor m/2\rfloor}\big|\le CN^{-(m+1)/2}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Splitting off the first coordinate, $M_k(n+1)=\sum_j\binom kj\mu_jM_{k-j}(n)$ with $\mu_j=\int f^jd\nu$, $\mu_0=1$, $\mu_1=0$. Induct on $k$: the increment of $e_k(n)=M_k(n)-a(k)\sigma^{2\lfloor k/2\rfloor}n^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}$ is $\binom k2\sigma^2e_{k-2}(n)+\sum_{j\ge3}\binom kj\mu_jM_{k-j}(n)+\Theta_n$, where the leading terms cancel in $\Theta_n$ because $\binom{2m}2a(2m-2)=m\,a(2m)$ and what is left is the Taylor remainder of $n\mapsto n^m$ ($0\le(u+1)^p-u^p-pu^{p-1}\le p(p-1)(u+1)^{p-2}$). All increments are $O((n+1)^{(k-3)/2})$, and summing gives $e_k(n)=O((n+1)^{(k-1)/2})$.
Formalized in \texttt{ForMathlib/Probability/Moments/IidSumMoments.lean} and \texttt{IidSumExpansion.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Central limit theorem for the SK free energy (Theorem~1.4.11, Eq.~(1.109))]\label{thm:sg-tal:free-energy-clt}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_free_energy_clt, SpinGlasses.clt_induction, SpinGlasses.abs_sub_ref_le_of_ode,
        SpinGlasses.exists_clt_const, SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_clt_ref,
        Nat.eq_zero_of_odd_of_two_step_mul,
        ProbabilityTheory.integrable_pow_log_cosh_affine_gaussianReal,
        ProbabilityTheory.integral_pow_add_two_gaussianReal}
  \leanok
\talsource{Theorem~1.4.11, Eq.~(1.109); Lemma~1.4.12}
Let $\beta^2<1/4$, let the field $h$ be deterministic, $q=\mathbb E\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt qz+h)$, $Y=\beta\sqrt qz+h$, $a(k)=\mathbb E g^k$ and $b=\mathbb E(\log\cosh Y)^2-(\mathbb E\log\cosh Y)^2-\frac{\beta^2q^2}2$. For every $k$ there is $C$ such that for all $N>0$,
$$\Big|\mathbb E\Big(\frac1N\log Z_N(\beta,h)-\mathrm{SK}(\beta,h)\Big)^k-\frac{a(k)\,b^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}}{N^{k/2}}\Big|\le\frac{C}{N^{(k+1)/2}},$$
where $\mathrm{SK}(\beta,h)=\mathrm{rsFunctional}(\beta,h,q)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Along the path let $b(s)=\mathrm{Var}(\log\cosh Y)-\frac{\beta^2q^2}2\cos^2s$, so $b'=\beta^2q^2\sin s\cos s$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:centered-ode} the moments $\psi_m$ satisfy $\psi_m'=\sin s\cos s(\frac{m(m-1)}{2N}\beta^2q^2\psi_{m-2}+D_m)$ with $|D_m|\le K_mN^{-(m+1)/2}$; at $s=\pi/2$ they are the moments of an i.i.d.\ centered average and Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:iid-moments} gives the endpoint estimate with $b(\pi/2)=\mathrm{Var}(\log\cosh Y)$. The abstract induction \texttt{clt\_induction} (strong induction on $k$, integrating from $\pi/2$ to $s$ by \texttt{Real.abs\_sub\_le\_of\_exists\_hasDerivAt\_Icc}) concludes for all $s\in[0,\pi/2]$ with constants given by the recursion of \texttt{exists\_clt\_const}, which depends only on $\beta$, $q$ and the constants above; take $s=0$. The case $k=1$ is Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:alr} (centered at $\mathrm{SK}$).
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKFreeEnergyCLT.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Scope of Theorem~1.4.11 in Lean]\label{rem:sg-tal:free-energy-clt-status}
\talsource{Theorem~1.4.11, Eq.~(1.109); source lines 1660--1895}
Theorem~1.4.11 is proved for a \emph{deterministic} field $h$, which is the case $c=0$ of the book's Gaussian field $h_i=cy_i+d$ (the book uses the Gaussian hypothesis only through Lemma~1.4.12, and notes that it can be weakened). Not formalized: the random Gaussian field ($c\ne0$), for which $q$, $\mathbb E\log\cosh Y$ and the path field also average over $h$, so that the fixed-point, profile and overlap-moment results of this chapter would need random-field versions. The change from the angle to the book's linear $t$ is not needed. The book states that Theorem~1.4.11 ``will not be used in the sequel''; its method (the a priori estimate for $V(s)$, the induction on $k$ and the i.i.d.\ base case) is reused in Theorem~2.5.3, and the abstract induction and the i.i.d.\ moment theorem are stated generally for that purpose.
\end{remark}

\section{Spin Decorrelation, Asymptotic Product Measures and Projections (Talagrand Sections~1.4--1.5)}\label{sec:sg-tal:decorrelation}

The final component of the high-temperature theory connects overlap concentration to local spin independence and to the geometry of Gaussian projections.
Everything in this section is proved in Lean (Milestone T5).
The results on spin decorrelation and on the asymptotic product structure are proved first for an \emph{arbitrary} random Gibbs state on a finite configuration space whose law is symmetric between sites, and then specialized to the SK model with the overlap bound of Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:latala-alr}.

\begin{remark}[Deviations from the book in Sections~1.4 (end) and~1.5]\label{rem:sg-tal:dev-15}
Each item states what differs from Talagrand's text and why the Lean proof was organized that way.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{General setting.} The book remarks that Proposition~1.4.14 only uses the symmetry between sites; Lean takes this literally. A \emph{site-symmetric random Gibbs state} consists of a finite set $\alpha$ of configurations, observables $\xi_1,\dots,\xi_N:\alpha\to[-1,1]$, weights $w_\omega(a)\ge0$ with $\sum_a w_\omega(a)=1$ depending measurably on the disorder $\omega$, and, for every permutation $\pi$ of the sites, a relabelling $a\mapsto\pi\cdot a$ of $\alpha$ with $\xi_i(\pi\cdot a)=\xi_{\pi(i)}(a)$ and a measure-preserving map $\varphi_\pi$ of the disorder with $w_{\varphi_\pi(\omega)}(a)=w_\omega(\pi\cdot a)$. For the SK model $\pi\cdot\sigma=\sigma\circ\pi$, and $\varphi_\pi$ relabels the Gaussian couplings $g_{ij}$ through the sorted pair $\{\pi(i),\pi(j)\}$ (Lean: \texttt{pairPerm}).
\emph{Why:} this is exactly the hypothesis the book's proof uses, it makes the statement reusable for other site-symmetric models, and it avoids any measurability theory of random measures: with finitely many configurations every Gibbs average is a finite sum of the weights, so measurability and integrability in $\omega$ follow from the continuity of the relevant expressions in the weight vector, which lies in the compact cube $[0,1]^\alpha$.

\item \emph{Proof of Proposition~1.4.14: pointwise in the disorder, site symmetry used last.}
In the book the chain is: (a) site symmetry turns $N(N-1)\cdots(N-p+1)\,\nu(\cdots)$ into a sum over tuples of distinct sites; (b) that sum is bounded by the sum over \emph{all} tuples, which equals $N^p\,\nu\big(R_{1,2}^{p-1}\,\dot{\boldsymbol\sigma}^1\!\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol\sigma}^2/N\big)$; (c) $R_{1,2}^{p-1}$ is replaced by $R_{1,2}^{p-1}-q^{p-1}$ because $\langle\dot{\boldsymbol\sigma}^1\!\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol\sigma}^2\rangle=0$; (d) the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality is applied for the measure $\nu$ (that is, after averaging over the disorder), together with a bound of $\nu\big((\dot{\boldsymbol\sigma}^1\!\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol\sigma}^2/N)^2\big)$.
Lean performs (b)--(d) \emph{before} averaging over the disorder, for each fixed weight vector $w$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:decorr-det}): the sum over all $(k+1)$-tuples of the squared centered correlation is at most $4kN^{k+1}\langle(R_{1,2}-q)^2\rangle$, where only the elementary inequality $|ab|\le 2a^2+b^2/8$ is used in place of Cauchy--Schwarz for $\nu$. Only afterwards is the disorder integrated, and site symmetry (a) is used to compare the $N(N-1)\cdots(N-k)$ tuples of distinct sites, which all carry the same expected value, with the sum over all tuples.
\emph{Why:} (i) The deterministic statement holds for every probability vector, so it needs no hypothesis on the law of the disorder and no square roots of expectations (hence no integrability bookkeeping for products of square roots); the only place where the randomness and the symmetry enter is the last step, which is a short counting argument. (ii) The result has the same shape as the book's, since in both cases the final bound is a constant times $\nu((R_{1,2}-q)^2)$: Cauchy--Schwarz followed by $\nu(b^2)^{1/2}\le c\,\nu((R_{1,2}-q)^2)^{1/2}$ gives $\nu\big(|R_{1,2}-q|\,|b|\big)\le c\,\nu((R_{1,2}-q)^2)$, and the weighted AM--GM inequality gives the same with a different constant. (iii) Nonnegativity of each term in the step ``distinct tuples $\le$ all tuples'' is automatic, because each term is the square of a Gibbs average.

\item \emph{The constant $16$ instead of $4$ in the four-replica step.}
The book writes $\langle(\mathbf R_{1,2}-R_{1,4}-R_{3,2}+R_{3,4})^2\rangle\le4\langle(R_{1,2}-q)^2\rangle$ after ``using that $(\sum_{i\le4}x_i)^2\le4\sum_{i\le4}x_i^2$''. Here $x_1,\dots,x_4$ are $\pm(R_{\ell\ell'}-q)$ for the four pairs of replicas, and the $q$'s cancel because the signs sum to zero. The quoted inequality gives $4\sum_{i\le4}x_i^2$, and each $\langle x_i^2\rangle$ equals $\langle(R_{1,2}-q)^2\rangle$ (replicas are independent and identically distributed), so the bound obtained from the quoted inequality is $4\cdot4=16$, not $4$. Lean proves exactly this: $\langle b^2\rangle\le16\langle(R_{1,2}-q)^2\rangle$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:decorr-det}, Step~4), and I did not try to improve the constant by other means.
\emph{Why it does not matter:} the constant enters only through $K(p)$, which is an unspecified number depending on $p$ alone in the book; the proof structure and the conclusion are unchanged.

\item \emph{Explicit constants and tuples instead of sets.}
$K(p)$ is the explicit number defined by $K(0)=0$, $K(p+1)=8p(p+1)^{p+1}+2K(p)$ (Lean: \texttt{spinDecorrConst}), obtained from $N^p\le p^p\,N(N-1)\cdots(N-p+1)$ for $p\le N$ (each factor $N-i$, $i<p$, is at least $N/p$) in place of the book's $\sup_{N\ge p}N^p/(N(N-1)\cdots(N-p+1))<\infty$. The induction is over tuples of distinct sites, because that is what the counting argument naturally controls; the statement for a set $S$ is deduced by enumerating $S$.
\emph{Why:} explicit constants make the statement checkable and let Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:asymp-product} carry an explicit constant; a constant ``depending on $p$ only'' would require quantifying over a family of models inside an existential, which is awkward in Lean.

\item \emph{Total variation distance.} In Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:asymp-product} the distance $\|G_{N,n}-\mu_n\|$ is the sum $\sum_\eta|G_{N,n}(\{\eta\})-\mu_n(\{\eta\})|$ (the book's Eq.~(A.79)), the first $n$ spins are the sites $0,\dots,n-1$, and $\mu_n(\{\eta\})=\prod_{i<n}(1+\eta_i\langle\sigma_i\rangle)/2$. The constant is $4^nK(n)$ times the overlap constant: the book's chain gives $2^n\sum_{I\subseteq[n]}\mathbb{E}(\langle\sigma_I\rangle-\prod_{i\in I}\langle\sigma_i\rangle)^2$, and each of the $2^n$ terms is bounded by $K(n)\nu((R_{1,2}-q)^2)$, using that $K$ is nondecreasing. \emph{Why:} the bound by the maximum over subsets is the simplest uniform one, and the book itself only claims a constant $K(n)$.

\item \emph{Section~1.5: a quantitative statement that is linear, not quadratic, in the overlap deviation.}
The book gives no formal statement in Section~1.5 (``we shall never need a formal statement''); it argues that $\mathbb{E}U^2$, $\mathbb{E}UV$ and $\mathbb{E}V^2$ all tend to the same Gaussian quantity because the Gaussian pair $(\mathbf g\cdot\mathbf x^1,\mathbf g\cdot\mathbf x^2)/\sqrt N$ has covariance $\simeq\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\rho&q\\q&\rho\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ and the law of a Gaussian vector depends on its covariance only. To turn this into a theorem one needs a quantitative dependence of $\mathbb{E}F(Z)$ on the covariance of $Z$. The standard tool, and the one proved in Lean (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:gaussian-interp}), is Gaussian interpolation: $\frac{d}{ds}\mathbb{E}F(\cos s\,X+\sin s\,Y)=\sin s\cos s\sum_{l,k}(B_{lk}-A_{lk})\,\mathbb{E}\,\partial_k\partial_lF$, which yields a bound that is \emph{linear} in the entries of the covariance difference, namely $\tfrac12\sum_{l,k}|B_{lk}-A_{lk}|\,\|\partial_k\partial_lF\|_\infty$. Here the covariance differences are the overlap deviations $R-q$ and $\|\mathbf x\|^2/N-\rho$ themselves, not their squares. Hence the proved bound is $\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf g}(U-V)^2\le C(f)\big(\nu|R_{1,2}-q|+\langle|\|\mathbf x\|^2/N-\rho|\rangle\big)$ with $C(f)=2(2M_1^2+M_0M_2)$, and for the SK model the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality $\nu|R_{1,2}-q|\le\nu((R_{1,2}-q)^2)^{1/2}$ gives the rate $\sqrt{K_{\mathrm{ov}}/N}$. The earlier planning document stated a bound of the form $L(f)^2\,\nu((R_{1,2}-q)^2)$ (rate $1/N$); that form is a guess of the plan and not a statement of the book. It would require the first-order terms in the covariance deviation to cancel in $\mathbb{E}U^2-2\,\mathbb{E}UV+\mathbb{E}V^2$, which the interpolation estimate does not show (each of the three terms is compared with the same reference value $T$ separately, so only the triangle inequality is available), and I did not establish such a cancellation. I do not claim the quadratic bound is false; it is simply not proved here.
\emph{Regularity of $f$.} The book allows ``a bounded, continuous function $f$''. The interpolation formula involves the second derivatives of $F(y_0,y_1)=f(y_0)f(y_1)$, so Lean requires $f\in C^2$ with $f$, $f'$, $f''$ bounded. Extending to bounded continuous $f$ would need an additional smoothing and approximation argument, which is not carried out.
\emph{Averaging.} The statement in Lean is for the mean square over $\mathbf g$ (and, for SK, over the disorder), not for ``typical $\mathbf g$ outside an exceptional set'' as in (1.137); the latter follows by Chebyshev's inequality but is not stated.

\item \emph{The earlier outline of this section is not formalized.} Before Milestone T5 this section contained a sketch based on the conditional exponential moment $\langle\exp(t\,\mathbf g\cdot(\sigma-\langle\sigma\rangle)/\sqrt N)\rangle\to\exp(t^2(1-q)/2)$ and a second-order Taylor argument. That sketch is not the argument of Talagrand's Section~1.5 (which compares $U$ and $V$ through Gaussian pairs), it was never a statement of the book, and it has been replaced by Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:random-projection}. The exponential-moment convergence is not proved.

\item \emph{Hypotheses.} As in Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:latala-alr}, the fixed point $q$ is a hypothesis (the equation $q=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt q\,z+h)]$ is assumed, and only the existence of a solution is proved), because these results do not use the uniqueness of the solution that makes ``the'' $q$ of the book well defined (uniqueness is proved for $h\ne0$ in Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:fixed-point-unique}). The condition $\beta<1/2$ is stated as $\beta^2<1/4$, which is the same condition for $\beta\ge0$ and is the form in which the overlap bound is proved (it also allows $\beta<0$). The abstract results of this section need only $|q|\le1$ (for Proposition~\ref{prop:sg-tal:spin-decorr}) or $0\le q\le\rho$ (for Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:random-projection}); the SK corollaries use $q\in[0,1]$ from the fixed-point equation.

\item \emph{Not formalized.} Models with non-Ising spins: Proposition~\ref{prop:sg-tal:spin-decorr} and the abstract part of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:random-projection} are stated for $[-1,1]$-valued observables, respectively for arbitrary vectors in $\mathbb{R}^N$ with an arbitrary self-overlap $\rho$, but Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:asymp-product} uses $\sigma_i^2=1$ (the Walsh expansion of cylinders) and no non-Ising SK-type model is instantiated. Also not formalized is the uniqueness of the fixed point for $h=0$ (Eq.~(1.88) for all even moments is Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-moments-all}).
\end{itemize}
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Replica computation for centered correlations]\label{lem:sg-tal:decorr-det}
  \lean{Weights.sum_mul_const_of_sum_eq_one,
        Weights.sq_sum_mul_eq,
        Weights.sq_sum_mul_le_sum_mul_sq,
        Weights.sum_mul_sub_avg,
        Weights.sum_sum_mul_centered,
        Weights.abs_sum_mul_le,
        Weights.sq_sum_sum_mul_le,
        sum_piFinSucc_mul_prod,
        SpinGlasses.sum_sq_avg_tuple,
        SpinGlasses.sum_mul_inv_mul_sum,
        SpinGlasses.sum_mul_overlap_sub,
        SpinGlasses.centered_overlap_eq,
        SpinGlasses.sum_mul_mul_const,
        SpinGlasses.sum_mul_mul_const_left,
        SpinGlasses.sum_sum_sq_decoupled_le,
        SpinGlasses.sum_sum_sq_centered_overlap_le,
        SpinGlasses.abs_inv_mul_sum_mul_le_one,
        SpinGlasses.sum_sq_avg_centered_monomial_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{proof of Proposition~1.4.14, Eqs.~(1.129)--(1.131)}
Let $\alpha$ be a finite set, $N\ge1$, $\xi_1,\dots,\xi_N:\alpha\to[-1,1]$, and $w$ a probability vector on $\alpha$. Write $\langle f\rangle=\sum_a w_af(a)$, $R(a,b)=N^{-1}\sum_{i\le N}\xi_i(a)\xi_i(b)$ and $\dot\xi_i=\xi_i-\langle\xi_i\rangle$. For every $q\in[-1,1]$ and $k\ge0$,
\[
\sum_{u\in[N]^{k+1}}\Big\langle\dot\xi_{u_0}\,\xi_{u_1}\cdots\xi_{u_k}\Big\rangle^2\le 4k\,N^{k+1}\Big\langle (R_{1,2}-q)^2\Big\rangle ,
\]
where $\langle(R_{1,2}-q)^2\rangle=\sum_{a,b}w_aw_b(R(a,b)-q)^2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
\emph{Step 1 (replicas).} Expanding the squares, the left side is $\langle\!\langle(\sum_i\xi_i^1\xi_i^2)^k\sum_j\dot\xi_j^1\dot\xi_j^2\rangle\!\rangle$, because a sum over tuples of a product is a power of a sum.
\emph{Step 2 (centering).} Since $\langle\dot\xi_j\rangle=0$, $\langle\!\langle\sum_j\dot\xi^1_j\dot\xi^2_j\rangle\!\rangle=0$, so $(NR)^k$ may be replaced by $(NR)^k-(Nq)^k$.
\emph{Step 3 (Lipschitz bound).} For $|x|,|y|\le1$, $|x^k-y^k|\le k|x-y|$, and $|ab|\le2a^2+b^2/8$ with $a=R_{1,2}-q$, $b=\dot\xi^1\cdot\dot\xi^2/N$ bounds the left side by $kN^{k+1}\big(2\langle(R_{1,2}-q)^2\rangle+\tfrac18\langle b^2\rangle\big)$.
\emph{Step 4 (four replicas).} By direct expansion, $b=(R_{1,2}-q)-\langle R_{1,3}-q\rangle_3-\langle R_{3,2}-q\rangle_3+\langle R_{3,4}-q\rangle_{3,4}$, where the subscripts name the averaged replicas. Hence $b^2\le 4\sum$ of the squares of the four terms and, by Jensen's inequality for each averaged replica, $\langle b^2\rangle\le16\langle(R_{1,2}-q)^2\rangle$.
Combining the steps gives $kN^{k+1}(2+2)\langle(R_{1,2}-q)^2\rangle$.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKSpinDecorrelation.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Spin decorrelation from overlap concentration]\label{prop:sg-tal:spin-decorr}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integrable_comp_weights,
        SpinGlasses.integral_comp_weights_equiv,
        SpinGlasses.sq_sum_comp_equiv,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sq_centered_perm,
        Nat.pow_le_pow_mul_descFactorial,
        Fin.descFactorial_mul_le_sum,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sq_centered_le,
        SpinGlasses.spinDecorrConst,
        SpinGlasses.spinDecorrConst_nonneg,
        SpinGlasses.spinDecorrConst_mono,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sq_prod_sub_prod_le,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sq_prod_sub_prod_le_finset}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.4.14, Eqs.~(1.127)--(1.128)}
Consider a site-symmetric random Gibbs state (Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:dev-15}) with $N\ge1$ sites, and write $\nu(\cdot)=\mathbb{E}\langle\cdot\rangle^{\otimes2}$. For every $q\in[-1,1]$ and every set $S$ of sites (Proposition~1.4.14, Eq.~(1.127)),
\[
\mathbb{E}\Big(\Big\langle\prod_{i\in S}\xi_i\Big\rangle-\prod_{i\in S}\langle\xi_i\rangle\Big)^2\le K(|S|)\,\nu\big((R_{1,2}-q)^2\big),
\]
with $K(0)=0$, $K(p+1)=8p(p+1)^{p+1}+2K(p)$. The same holds with $\prod_{l\le p}\xi_{u_l}$ for any $p$ distinct sites $u_1,\dots,u_p$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
\emph{Step 1 (one centered site).} Let $u_0,\dots,u_k$ be distinct sites and $T(u)=\mathbb{E}\langle\dot\xi_{u_0}\xi_{u_1}\cdots\xi_{u_k}\rangle^2$. By site symmetry $T(\pi\circ u)=T(u)$ for every permutation $\pi$ (the squared correlation is a continuous functional of the weights, and the weights of $\varphi_\pi(\omega)$ are those of $\omega$ relabelled). Any two tuples of distinct sites differ by a permutation, so $T$ is constant on the $N(N-1)\cdots(N-k)$ injective tuples. Integrating Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:decorr-det} over the disorder and dropping the non-injective tuples ($T\ge0$) gives $N(N-1)\cdots(N-k)\,T(u)\le4kN^{k+1}\nu((R_{1,2}-q)^2)$. With $N^{k+1}\le(k+1)^{k+1}N(N-1)\cdots(N-k)$ (each factor $N-i$ is at least $N/(k+1)$) we get $T(u)\le4k(k+1)^{k+1}\nu((R_{1,2}-q)^2)$.
\emph{Step 2 (induction on $p$).} Write $\langle\xi_{u_0}\cdots\xi_{u_k}\rangle-\prod_{l}\langle\xi_{u_l}\rangle=\langle\dot\xi_{u_0}\xi_{u_1}\cdots\xi_{u_k}\rangle+\langle\xi_{u_0}\rangle\big(\langle\xi_{u_1}\cdots\xi_{u_k}\rangle-\prod_{l\ge1}\langle\xi_{u_l}\rangle\big)$. Since $|\langle\xi_{u_0}\rangle|\le1$ and $(x+y)^2\le2x^2+2y^2$, the induction gives the constant $2\cdot4k(k+1)^{k+1}+2K(k)=K(k+1)$.
\emph{Step 3 (sets).} Enumerate $S$ and use that $\prod_{i\in S}f(i)=\prod_{l\le|S|}f(u_l)$.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKSpinDecorrelation.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Site symmetry of the SK model]\label{lem:sg-tal:sk-site-sym}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.min_max_sort_sort,
        SpinGlasses.pairPerm,
        SpinGlasses.sum_pairs_pairPerm,
        SpinGlasses.measurePreserving_comp_pairPerm,
        SpinGlasses.exists_measurePreserving_sk_gibbsWeight,
        SpinGlasses.sk_gibbsWeight_siteSymmetric}
  \leanok
\talsource{the symmetry between sites used in Eq.~(1.130); no separate statement in the book}
Let $N\ge1$ and $\beta,h\in\mathbb R$. The Gibbs weights $G_g(\sigma)=\exp(-H_N(\sigma))/Z_N$ of the SK model, as functions of the Gaussian couplings $g\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{FinPairs}\,N}$, are nonnegative, sum to one, depend measurably on $g$, and for every permutation $\pi$ of $\{1,\dots,N\}$ there is a map $\varphi_\pi$ of the couplings preserving the law $\mathcal N(0,1)^{\otimes\mathrm{FinPairs}\,N}$ with $G_{\varphi_\pi(g)}(\sigma)=G_g(\sigma\circ\pi)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
The map $\{i<j\}\mapsto\{\pi(i),\pi(j)\}$, sorted, is a permutation of $\mathrm{FinPairs}\,N$ (its inverse is the same construction for $\pi^{-1}$). Reindexing the sum over pairs by this permutation gives $\sum_{i<j}g_{ij}\sigma_{\pi(i)}\sigma_{\pi(j)}=\sum_{i<j}g_{\tau^{-1}(ij)}\sigma_i\sigma_j$, so $H_N(\sigma\circ\pi)$ for the couplings $g$ is $H_N(\sigma)$ for the relabelled couplings $\varphi_\pi(g)=g\circ\tau^{-1}$; the field term $h\sum_i\sigma_{\pi(i)}$ is invariant, and so is the partition function. A coordinate permutation preserves a product of identical measures.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKSiteSymmetry.lean} and \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKCorrelationDecay.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Decay of truncated spin correlations in the SK model]\label{thm:sg-tal:sk-correlation-decay}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_expected_sq_overlap_le,
        MeasureTheory.one_add_mul_integral_integral_le,
        MeasureTheory.integral_pi_fin_two_of_integrable,
        MeasureTheory.integral_pi_fin_two,
        SpinGlasses.integral_pi_fin_two_count_tilted,
        SpinGlasses.sk_expected_sq_overlap_le_weights,
        SpinGlasses.sk_correlation_decay_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.132)}
Let $N\ge1$, $\beta,h\in\mathbb R$ with $\beta^2<1/4$, and let $q=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt q\,z+h)]$. Then (Eq.~(1.132)) for every set $S$ of sites,
\[
\mathbb{E}\Big(\Big\langle\prod_{i\in S}\sigma_i\Big\rangle-\prod_{i\in S}\langle\sigma_i\rangle\Big)^2\le K(|S|)\,\frac{K_{\mathrm{ov}}}{N},\qquad K_{\mathrm{ov}}=\frac{(1/2-2\beta^2)^{-1/2}-1}{1/4-\beta^2},
\]
and in particular $\nu((R_{1,2}-q)^2)\le K_{\mathrm{ov}}/N$ for the SK Gibbs measure.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-conc} with $s=1/4-\beta^2$ gives $\nu(\exp(sN(R_{1,2}-q)^2))\le(1/2-2\beta^2)^{-1/2}$, and $e^x\ge1+x$ (for the two averages) gives $1+sN\nu((R_{1,2}-q)^2)\le(1/2-2\beta^2)^{-1/2}$. Apply Proposition~\ref{prop:sg-tal:spin-decorr}, whose hypotheses are Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:sk-site-sym}, with $q\in[0,1]$ (fixed point of a $[0,1]$-valued map). Two replicas of the Gibbs measure integrate as the double sum $\sum_{\sigma,\tau}G(\sigma)G(\tau)(\cdot)$.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKOverlapConcentration.lean} and \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKCorrelationDecay.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Walsh expansion of cylinders]\label{lem:sg-tal:walsh}
  \lean{Weights.abs_sum_mul_le_sum_mul_abs,
        Weights.abs_sum_sum_mul_le,
        prod_one_add_mul_eq,
        SpinGlasses.sum_mul_indicator_sub_prod_eq,
        SpinGlasses.sq_sum_abs_le_sum_sq}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.133)--(1.134), with (A.79)}
Let $\xi_1,\dots,\xi_N:\alpha\to\{-1,1\}$, $w$ a probability vector, $n\le N$ and $\eta\in\{-1,1\}^n$. With $G(A_\eta)=\langle\mathbf 1\{\xi_i=\eta_i,\ i<n\}\rangle$ and $\mu_n(\{\eta\})=\prod_{i<n}(1+\eta_i\langle\xi_i\rangle)/2$,
\[
G(A_\eta)-\mu_n(\{\eta\})=2^{-n}\sum_{I\subseteq[n]}\eta_I\Big(\Big\langle\prod_{i\in I}\xi_i\Big\rangle-\prod_{i\in I}\langle\xi_i\rangle\Big),\qquad
\Big(\sum_\eta\big|G(A_\eta)-\mu_n(\{\eta\})\big|\Big)^2\le2^n\sum_{I\subseteq[n]}\Big(\Big\langle\prod_{i\in I}\xi_i\Big\rangle-\prod_{i\in I}\langle\xi_i\rangle\Big)^2.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
For signs $x,e$ one has $1+xe\in\{0,2\}$ with $1+xe=2$ iff $x=e$, so $\mathbf 1\{\xi=\eta\}=2^{-n}\prod_i(1+\xi_i\eta_i)=2^{-n}\sum_I\eta_I\xi_I$ (Eq.~(1.134)); averaging gives the first identity (the density $\prod_i(1+\eta_i\langle\xi_i\rangle)$ expands in the same way). The second claim is the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality twice: over the $2^n$ values of $\eta$, and over the $2^n$ subsets $I$ (using $\eta_I^2=1$).
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKAsymptoticProduct.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Gibbs measure is asymptotically product on finite marginals]\label{thm:sg-tal:asymp-product}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_sq_sum_abs_le,
        SpinGlasses.sk_gibbs_marginal_totalVariation_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{Theorem~1.4.15, with Eqs.~(1.133)--(1.134)}
Let $N\ge1$, $\beta,h\in\mathbb R$ with $\beta^2<1/4$, $q=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt q\,z+h)]$, and $n\le N$. Let $G_{N,n}(\{\eta\})=G_N\{\sigma:\sigma_i=\eta_i,\ i<n\}$ be the law of the first $n$ spins and $\mu_n(\{\eta\})=\prod_{i<n}\frac{1+\eta_i\langle\sigma_i\rangle}{2}$ the product measure with the same one-site means. Then (Theorem~1.4.15)
\[
\mathbb{E}\Big(\sum_{\eta\in\{-1,1\}^n}\big|G_{N,n}(\{\eta\})-\mu_n(\{\eta\})\big|\Big)^2\le4^nK(n)\,\frac{K_{\mathrm{ov}}}{N}.
\]
More generally, for any site-symmetric random Gibbs state with $\{-1,1\}$-valued observables and any $n$ distinct sites, the left side is at most $4^nK(n)\,\nu((R_{1,2}-q)^2)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:walsh} the squared total variation distance is at most $2^n\sum_{I\subseteq[n]}(\langle\sigma_I\rangle-\prod_{i\in I}\langle\sigma_i\rangle)^2$. Take expectations and apply Proposition~\ref{prop:sg-tal:spin-decorr} to each of the $2^n$ sets $I$ ($K$ is nondecreasing), then Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:sk-correlation-decay}.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKAsymptoticProduct.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Gaussian interpolation for smooth functionals]\label{lem:sg-tal:gaussian-interp}
  \uses{lem:sg-tal:path-derivative}
  \lean{Real.sub_le_half_of_deriv_le,
        Real.neg_half_le_sub_of_le_deriv,
        Real.abs_sub_le_half_of_abs_deriv_le,
        Real.abs_sub_le_half_of_exists_hasDerivAt,
        SpinGlasses.abs_integral_comp_sub_le,
        SpinGlasses.integral_integral_dotProduct_add,
        SpinGlasses.abs_integral_dotProduct_sub_le,
        SpinGlasses.contDiff_pair_mul,
        SpinGlasses.fderiv_pair_mul,
        SpinGlasses.fderiv_fderiv_pair_mul,
        SpinGlasses.abs_integral_mul_sub_le,
        Real.hasDerivAt_sin_sq,
        Real.abs_sub_add_half_le_of_exists_hasDerivAt}
  \leanok
\talsource{Lemma~1.3.1, Eq.~(1.40), applied quantitatively to the argument of Section~1.5, Eqs.~(1.139)--(1.141)}
Let $F:\mathbb R^\kappa\to\mathbb R$ be $C^2$ with $|F|\le B$, $|\partial_lF|\le D_l$, $|\partial_k\partial_lF|\le D_{kl}$, and let $a_l\in\mathbb R^K$, $a'_l\in\mathbb R^{K'}$, $l\in\kappa$, and $\xi,\xi'$ standard Gaussian vectors. Then
\[
\Big|\mathbb{E}F\big((a'_l\cdot\xi')_l\big)-\mathbb{E}F\big((a_l\cdot\xi)_l\big)\Big|\le\frac12\sum_{l,k}\big|a'_l\cdot a'_k-a_l\cdot a_k\big|\,D_{kl}.
\]
In particular, for $f\in C^2(\mathbb R)$ with $|f|\le M_0$, $|f'|\le M_1$, $|f''|\le M_2$ and vectors $u,v\in\mathbb R^K$, $u',v'\in\mathbb R^{K'}$,
\[
\Big|\mathbb{E}f(u'\cdot\xi')f(v'\cdot\xi')-\mathbb{E}f(u\cdot\xi)f(v\cdot\xi)\Big|\le M_1^2\,|u'\cdot v'-u\cdot v|+\frac{M_0M_2}{2}\big(|u'\cdot u'-u\cdot u|+|v'\cdot v'-v\cdot v|\big).
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Realize the two vectors as independent centered Gaussian vectors $X=(a_l\cdot\xi)_l$, $Y=(a'_l\cdot\xi')_l$ with covariances $A_{lk}=a_l\cdot a_k$, $B_{lk}=a'_l\cdot a'_k$ (Gram transfer). Along $Z_s=\cos s\,X+\sin s\,Y$ differentiate under the expectation (the derivative of the integrand is bounded by a multiple of $\|(X,Y)\|_1$) and apply Gaussian integration by parts to the jointly Gaussian pair $(-\sin s\,X_l+\cos s\,Y_l,\,Z_s)$, whose covariance is $\sin s\cos s\,(B_{lk}-A_{lk})$:
\[
\frac d{ds}\mathbb{E}F(Z_s)=\sin s\cos s\sum_{l,k}(B_{lk}-A_{lk})\,\mathbb{E}\,\partial_k\partial_lF(Z_s).
\]
The derivative is at most $\sin s\cos s\sum|B_{lk}-A_{lk}|D_{kl}$ in absolute value; since $\varphi\pm\frac K2\sin^2$ is monotone, $|\varphi(\pi/2)-\varphi(0)|\le K/2$ (the same calculus step as in Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:alr}). For the second claim apply this to $F(y)=f(y_0)f(y_1)$ whose partial derivatives are $f'(y_0)f(y_1)$, $f(y_0)f'(y_1)$ and second partials $f''f$, $f'f'$, $ff''$.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/GaussianCovarianceInterpolation.lean} and \texttt{ForMathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/SinCosEndpoint.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A kind of central limit theorem for a finite Gibbs state]\label{thm:sg-tal:random-projection}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.abs_integral_block_sub_le,
        SpinGlasses.integral_pi_fin_two_gaussianReal,
        SpinGlasses.integral_pi_fin_two_fst,
        SpinGlasses.integral_pi_fin_two_mul,
        SpinGlasses.continuous_integral_comp_add_mul,
        MeasureTheory.integral_sq_sub,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sq_sub_le,
        SpinGlasses.sk_random_projection_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{Section~1.5, Eqs.~(1.135)--(1.139), main statement Eq.~(1.138)}
Let $\alpha$ be finite, $x_a\in\mathbb R^N$ for $a\in\alpha$ ($N\ge1$), $w$ a probability vector on $\alpha$, $\mathbf b=\sum_aw_ax_a$ the barycenter, $0\le q\le\rho$, and $f\in C^2(\mathbb R)$ with $|f|\le M_0$, $|f'|\le M_1$, $|f''|\le M_2$. Let $\mathbf g\sim\mathcal N(0,I_N)$ and $\xi\sim\mathcal N(0,1)$ independent, and
\[
U=\sum_aw_af\Big(\frac{\mathbf g\cdot x_a}{\sqrt N}\Big),\qquad V=\mathbb{E}_\xi f\Big(\frac{\mathbf g\cdot\mathbf b}{\sqrt N}+\xi\sqrt{\rho-q}\Big).
\]
Then (Eq.~(1.138), quantitative form)
\[
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf g}(U-V)^2\le2\,(2M_1^2+M_0M_2)\Big(\sum_{a,a'}w_aw_{a'}\Big|\frac{x_a\cdot x_{a'}}N-q\Big|+\sum_aw_a\Big|\frac{\|x_a\|^2}N-\rho\Big|\Big).
\]
For the SK model with $\beta^2<1/4$ and $q=\mathbb{E}[\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt q\,z+h)]$ (so $\rho=1$, $x_\sigma=\sigma$), the same quantities with $\mathbf g$ also averaged over the disorder satisfy
\[
\mathbb{E}\,\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf g}(U-V)^2\le2\,(2M_1^2+M_0M_2)\sqrt{\frac{K_{\mathrm{ov}}}N}.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Expand $(U-V)^2=U^2-2UV+V^2$. Each of $\mathbb{E}U^2=\sum_{a,a'}w_aw_{a'}\mathbb{E}f(\frac{\mathbf g\cdot x_a}{\sqrt N})f(\frac{\mathbf g\cdot x_{a'}}{\sqrt N})$, $\mathbb{E}UV$ and $\mathbb{E}V^2$ is an expectation $\mathbb{E}f(u_1\cdot\mathbf g+u_2\cdot\boldsymbol\xi)f(v_1\cdot\mathbf g+v_2\cdot\boldsymbol\xi)$ with $\boldsymbol\xi\sim\mathcal N(0,I_2)$ (for $UV$ and $V^2$ the average over $\xi$ is written as an integral over the coordinates of $\boldsymbol\xi$ by Fubini). The reference value is $T=\mathbb{E}f(z\sqrt q+\xi\sqrt{\rho-q})f(z\sqrt q+\xi'\sqrt{\rho-q})$, the Gaussian integral for the Gram matrix $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\rho&q\\q&\rho\end{smallmatrix}\right)$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:gaussian-interp} each term differs from $T$ by $M_1^2|u\cdot v-q|+\frac{M_0M_2}2(|u\cdot u-\rho|+|v\cdot v-\rho|)$, where $u\cdot v=u_1\cdot v_1+u_2\cdot v_2$. The covariances are overlaps: $x_a\cdot x_{a'}/N$ for $U^2$, $\sum_{a'}w_{a'}x_a\cdot x_{a'}/N$ for $UV$, and $\|\mathbf b\|^2/N=\sum_{a,a'}w_aw_{a'}x_a\cdot x_{a'}/N$ for $V^2$, and the noise adds $\rho-q$ to the variance of the second factor. Since $\mathbb{E}(U-V)^2=(\mathbb{E}U^2-T)-2(\mathbb{E}UV-T)+(\mathbb{E}V^2-T)$, the three bounds sum to the claim. For the SK model $\|\sigma\|^2/N=1$, and by the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality for the weights, $\mathbb{E}\sum w_aw_{a'}|R-q|\le(\nu((R_{1,2}-q)^2))^{1/2}\le\sqrt{K_{\mathrm{ov}}/N}$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:sk-correlation-decay}).
\emph{Formalization note.} The bound is linear, not quadratic, in the overlap deviation, so the rate is $N^{-1/2}$; $f$ must be $C^2$ with $f,f',f''$ bounded. The identification of the barycenter vector with the magnetizations $(\langle\sigma_i\rangle)_i$ is used in the SK statement.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKRandomProjectionCLT.lean}.
\end{proof}

\section{The cavity method (Talagrand Section~1.6)}
\label{sec:sg-tal:cavity}

\paragraph{The idea.}
The cavity method compares the SK system on $N+1$ spins with the one on $N$ spins by singling
out the last spin.  Splitting the Hamiltonian into the old pairs $i<j<N$ and the $N$ new edges
$(i,N)$ writes the $(N+1)$-spin Gibbs average as an $N$-spin average whose energy is perturbed
by a Gaussian field $X_\sigma$ coming from the new edges.  Replacing $X_\sigma$ by an independent
field $Y_\sigma=\beta z\sqrt q$, whose covariance has $q$ where $X_\sigma$ has the overlap,
decouples the last spin.  The error of this replacement is controlled by $|R_{1,2}-q|$ through
Gaussian interpolation along the path $s\mapsto\cos(s)X+\sin(s)Y$.
The section uses this one mechanism twice:
\begin{itemize}
\item to show that the overlap concentrates around $q$ (Lemmas~1.6.2--1.6.4 and
  Propositions~1.6.5--1.6.7), by interpolating a Gibbs average of an observable of the overlap;
\item to compute the free-energy increment $(N+1)p_{N+1}-Np_N$ up to $O(N^{-1}+\nu(|R_{1,2}-q|))$
  (Proposition~1.6.8), by interpolating the logarithm of two Gibbs averages.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{How the section is organized.}
The parts are ordered by logical dependence, which is not always the order of the book.
Each part opens with a short paragraph saying what it proves, which earlier parts it uses and its
formalization status.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[A.] \emph{Setting up the cavity} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:cavity-setup}; Proposition~1.6.1,
  Eqs.~(1.143)--(1.149), (1.153)): the exact algebra of splitting off the last spin and the
  covariances of the two fields.  Fully proved.
\item[B.] \emph{Gaussian interpolation tools} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:cavity-tools}): model-independent
  lemmas used by every later part: dominated differentiation under the old disorder, Gr\"onwall's
  inequality, and the Gaussian derivative of the logarithm of a $\cosh$ Gibbs average.  Fully proved.
\item[C.] \emph{The overlap interpolation} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:cavity-path}; Lemmas~1.6.2--1.6.4,
  Eqs.~(1.150)--(1.155)): the endpoints, the derivative and its domination.  Fully proved.
\item[D.] \emph{Concentration of the overlap} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:cavity-overlap};
  Propositions~1.6.5--1.6.7): consequences of C.  Fully proved.
\item[E.] \emph{The free-energy increment} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:cavity-prop168};
  Proposition~1.6.8, Eqs.~(1.170)--(1.182)).  The exact identity (1.176) and both interpolation
  estimates (1.181) and (1.182) are proved, hence so is Proposition~1.6.8 (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:prop-168}).
  Fully proved; the deviations from the book are collected in Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:dev-168}.
\end{enumerate}
Dependencies: A is used by all later parts, B by C and E, C by D, and A, B and the algebra of E
by the remainder of E.  Part E does not use C or D; it only uses D afterwards, to turn the
quantity $\nu(|R_{1,2}-q|)$ into a rate in $N$, which the proposition itself does not need.

\begin{remark}[Conventions and deviations from the book in Section~1.6]
\label{rem:sg-tal:dev-16}
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{$N$ versus $N+1$ spins.}  In A--D the Gibbs average is over $N+1$ spins with
  the old-pair energy carrying the full prefactor $\beta/\sqrt{N+1}$, and the \emph{truncated}
  overlap $R^-=(N+1)^{-1}\sum_{i<N}\sigma_i\tau_i$ divides by $N+1$.  In E the Gibbs average
  $\langle\cdot\rangle$ and $\nu$ are those of the genuine $N$-spin SK system at $\beta$ with
  overlap $R=N^{-1}\sum_{i\le N}\sigma_i\tau_i$; the $(N+1)$-spin system is taken at
  $\beta_+=\beta\sqrt{1+1/N}$ so that $\beta_+/\sqrt{N+1}=\beta/\sqrt N$ (Eq.~(1.174)).  There is no
  $\beta_-$ in A--D.  Bounds proved for $N+1$ spins (e.g.\ the overlap moments of Part~D) apply to
  the $\nu$ of Part~E with $N$ replaced by $N-1$.  The moments of the genuine $N$-spin overlap for every $4\beta^2<1$ are
  Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-moments-all} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:latala-alr}).
\item \emph{Random old energy.}  The old-pair energy is random.  The book applies Lemma~1.4.2 to
  the whole system; here it is applied \emph{conditionally} on the old-pair disorder to the new-edge
  Gaussians (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-conditional-deriv} and its $\cosh$ analogue), and the resulting
  derivative is integrated with Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:conditional-derivative}.
\item \emph{Parameter.}  The interpolation is proved in the angle $s$ and then changed to the book's
  parameter $t=\cos^2 s$ on the open interval $(0,1)$; all derivatives carry the factor
  $\sin(s)\cos(s)$.
\item \emph{Status of Proposition~1.6.8.}  Fully proved, with explicit constants: the exact increment
  identity (1.176), the cavity $\cosh$ estimate (1.181) with constant $\beta^2\cosh(2h)e^{2\beta^2}$, the
  pair-perturbation estimate (1.182) with constants $\beta^2$ and $\beta^2\cosh(2h)e^{12\beta^2}$, and their
  assembly.  The reductions that take the two estimates as hypotheses are kept as intermediate
  steps and say so.  The deviations of the proof from the book are listed in
  Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:dev-168}.
\end{itemize}
\end{remark}

\subsection{Setting up the cavity: splitting off the last spin}
\label{sec:sg-tal:cavity-setup}

\emph{Content.} Proposition~1.6.1 and the algebra of Eqs.~(1.143)--(1.149), (1.153).  We show
that the $(N+1)$-spin Gibbs average with the last spin integrated out is an $N$-spin average
reweighted by $\cosh$ of a Gaussian field; that the pair disorder splits into independent
old and new parts; that the two fields $X,Y$ that will be interpolated are centered and jointly
Gaussian with the covariance $\beta^2\sigma_N\tau_N R^-$ resp.\ $\beta^2\sigma_N\tau_N q$; and the
elementary deterministic bounds on the truncated overlap.  \emph{Uses:} nothing from this section.
\emph{Used by:} every later part.  \emph{Status:} fully proved.

\begin{theorem}[Finite-fibre Gibbs disintegration and cavity identity]
\label{thm:sg-tal:cavity-finite}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_count_tilted_fiber,
        SpinGlasses.integral_count_tilted_equiv,
        SpinGlasses.integral_count_tilted_cavity_units,
        SpinGlasses.integral_count_tilted_fin_snoc_cavity_units}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.6.1, Eq.~(1.145); see also Eqs.~(1.143)--(1.144)}
Let $\alpha$ be a nonempty finite measurable space with measurable singletons.
For $A,L:\alpha\to\mathbb R$ and $f:\alpha\times\{-1,1\}\to\mathbb R$,
write $G_E$ for the probability measure obtained by tilting counting measure
with energy $E$. Then
\[
\int f(a,\varepsilon)\,dG_{A(a)+\varepsilon L(a)}
=\frac{\int \frac12\bigl(f(a,1)e^{L(a)}+f(a,-1)e^{-L(a)}\bigr)\,dG_A(a)}
{\int\cosh(L(a))\,dG_A(a)}.
\]
The result also holds on configurations $\sigma:\mathrm{Fin}(N+1)\to\{-1,1\}$:
replace $a$ by $\rho=(\sigma_i)_{i<N}$, $\varepsilon$ by $\sigma_N$,
and reconstruct $\sigma$ using $\mathrm{Fin.snoc}(\rho,\varepsilon)$.
The more general finite-fibre theorem applies to arbitrary finite last-spin types
and arbitrary fibre energies $B(a,\varepsilon)$; a finite equivalence transports
both the energy and observable without changing their Gibbs averages.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Expand the tilted counting measure as a normalized finite sum. Split its sum
into base and fibre coordinates, cancel the base partition function, and use
$\cosh x=(e^x+e^{-x})/2$. The last-coordinate form follows by reindexing
with the canonical $\mathrm{Fin.snoc}$ equivalence.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Split of the SK pair sum and Gaussian disorder]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-disorder-split}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.finPairsSuccEquiv,
        SpinGlasses.sum_finPairs_succ_cavity,
        SpinGlasses.sum_sk_pairs_succ_cavity,
        SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_energy_coupled_eq,
        SpinGlasses.measurePreserving_sk_cavity_disorder}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.143)--(1.144)}
For $N\ge0$, the pairs $i<j$ in $\mathrm{Fin}(N+1)$ are in bijection
with the disjoint union of the old pairs $i<j<N$ and the new edges $(i,N)$.
For arbitrary $g$ and real spin coordinates $s$ this gives
\[
\sum_{i<j\le N}g_{ij}s_i s_j
=\sum_{i<j<N}g_{ij}s_i s_j+s_N\sum_{i<N}g_{iN}s_i.
\]
The statement also holds for Ising spins, including the full coupled endpoint
energy with the common prefactor $\beta/\sqrt{N+1}$ and magnetic field
$h\sum_i\sigma_i$. If all the $g_{ij}$ are independent
standard Gaussians, the restriction $(g_{ij})_{i<j<N}$ and the new-edge family
$(g_{iN})_{i<N}$ jointly have the product of their respective independent
Gaussian laws (equality via a measure-preserving map).
The embeddings of the old pairs and of the new edges into the pairs of $\mathrm{Fin}(N+1)$ are written out in Lean, with no separate definitions.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
