Classify a pair by whether its second coordinate is the last site; reindex
the finite sum by this bijection. Reindex the product Gaussian law with
\texttt{measurePreserving\_arrowCongr'} and split it using
\texttt{measurePreserving\_sumPiEquivProdPi}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Full and truncated cavity overlaps]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-overlap-split}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_overlap_succ_eq_cavity,
        SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_self_overlap}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.148)--(1.149)}
For $N\ge0$ and $\sigma,\tau\in\{-1,1\}^{N+1}$, put
$R^-(\sigma,\tau)=(N+1)^{-1}\sum_{i<N}\sigma_i\tau_i$ and
$R(\sigma,\tau)=(N+1)^{-1}\sum_{i\le N}\sigma_i\tau_i$. Then
\[
R(\sigma,\tau)=R^-(\sigma,\tau)
 +(N+1)^{-1}\sigma_N\tau_N,
\qquad R^-(\sigma,\sigma)=N/(N+1).
\]
These are the deterministic overlap identities (1.149) and the diagonal
value required for the cancellation in the future replica derivative.
They do not assert the derivative formula itself.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Split the finite dot-product sum at its last coordinate using
\texttt{Fin.sum\_univ\_castSucc}; each Ising spin has square one.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Covariance of the interacting last-spin field]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-covariance}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_last_spin_cavity_field_mul}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.153), the fields $u_\sigma,v_\sigma$}
Let $N\ge0$, $M\ge0$, $\beta\in\mathbb R$, and
$\sigma,\tau:\mathrm{Fin}(N+1)\to\{-1,1\}$. For independent standard
Gaussians $g_i$ indexed by $i<N$, the fields
$U_g(\sigma)=\beta M^{-1/2}\sigma_N\sum_{i<N}g_i\sigma_i$ satisfy
\[
\mathbb E_g[U_g(\sigma)U_g(\tau)]
=\beta^2\sigma_N\tau_N M^{-1}\sum_{i<N}\sigma_i\tau_i.
\]
In the SK system of size $N+1$ take $M=N+1$. In particular the truncated
overlap divides by $N+1$, not by the number $N$ of retained coordinates.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Use the already-proved covariance of two normalized Gaussian linear forms
with coefficient vectors $i\mapsto\sigma_N\sigma_i$ and
$i\mapsto\tau_N\tau_i$; factor the last spins out of their dot product.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Gaussianity and centering of the two last-spin fields]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-fields-gaussian}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.hasGaussianLaw_sk_cavity_fields,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_cavity_edge_field_eq_zero,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_cavity_scalar_field_eq_zero,
        SpinGlasses.integral_linear_form_prod_eq_zero,
        SpinGlasses.integral_scalar_prod_eq_zero}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.147), (1.153)}
Fix $N\ge0$, $\beta,q\in\mathbb R$, and independent $g_i\sim N(0,1)$
for $i<N$ and $z\sim N(0,1)$. For configurations
$\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^{N+1}$, write
\[
X_\sigma=(N+1)^{-1/2}\beta\sigma_N\sum_{i<N}g_i\sigma_i,
\qquad Y_\sigma=\beta\sigma_N z\sqrt q.
\]
The concatenated family $(X_\sigma,Y_\sigma)_\sigma$ is jointly Gaussian
under the product disorder law, and each of its components has mean zero.
No restriction on $q$ is needed for Gaussianity and centering: $\sqrt q$ is real
for all real $q$. The old-pair disorder does not enter either field.
These are inputs to the \emph{conditional} derivative, not yet Lemma~1.6.3.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The edge family is a finite system of Gaussian linear forms. The scalar
family is the continuous linear image of $z\sim N(0,1)$; the product
of independent Gaussian vectors is jointly Gaussian. Both means vanish
by the centered Gaussian coordinate integrals.
The same facts for the $N$-spin fields of Part~E and for all covariances (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-field-covariance-difference},
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-N-fields}) are one-line specializations of five generic lemmas about a Gaussian linear
form $c\cdot g$ and an independent standard Gaussian scalar $z$ (with $d,d'\in\mathbb R$):
$\mathbb E[c\cdot g]=0$, $\mathbb E[dz]=0$, $\mathbb E[(c\cdot g)(c'\cdot g)]=c\cdot c'$,
$\mathbb E[(c\cdot g)(dz)]=0$ and $\mathbb E[(dz)(d'z)]=dd'$ (\texttt{integral\_linear\_form\_prod\_eq\_zero} and its companions).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Covariances of the last-spin cavity fields]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-field-covariance-difference}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_sk_cavity_edge_field_mul,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_cavity_edge_mul_scalar_eq_zero,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_cavity_scalar_field_mul,
        SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_covariance_difference,
        SpinGlasses.integral_linear_form_mul_prod,
        SpinGlasses.integral_linear_form_mul_scalar_prod,
        SpinGlasses.integral_scalar_mul_prod}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.153), (1.55); the book's $U$ equals $-\tfrac12$ of the $U$ used here}
With the fields $X_\sigma,Y_\sigma$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-fields-gaussian} and $q\ge0$, integration over the
product of new-edge disorder and the scalar standard Gaussian gives
\[
\mathbb E[X_\sigma X_\tau]=\beta^2\sigma_N\tau_N R^-(\sigma,\tau),
\quad \mathbb E[X_\sigma Y_\tau]=0,\quad
\mathbb E[Y_\sigma Y_\tau]=\beta^2 q\sigma_N\tau_N.
\]
Thus the sign convention of the trigonometric replica theorem is
$U(\sigma,\tau)=\mathbb E[Y_\sigma Y_\tau]-\mathbb E[X_\sigma X_\tau]
=\beta^2\sigma_N\tau_N(q-R^-(\sigma,\tau))$, where $R^-$ uses the
full-system normalization $N+1$. These are covariance inputs, not the
conditional or disorder-averaged derivative formula of Lemma~1.6.3.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The edge covariance is the existing normalized Gaussian linear-form lemma,
transported along the projection from the product disorder. Independence and
centering make the mixed covariance zero. The scalar square integrates to one;
$\sqrt q^{\,2}=q$ uses $q\ge0$. Distributivity gives the difference.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform truncated-overlap and covariance bounds]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-covariance-bound}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.abs_sk_cavity_overlap_le_one,
        SpinGlasses.abs_sk_cavity_covariance_le,
        SpinGlasses.abs_sk_cavity_kernel_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{proof of Lemma~1.6.4: the bound $|R^-_{\ell,\ell'}-q|\le2$ before Eq.~(1.155)}
For $N\ge0$, $q\in[0,1]$, $\beta\in\mathbb R$, and arbitrary Ising
configurations $a,b$ of size $N+1$, the truncated overlap and the cavity
covariance kernel satisfy
\[
|R^-(a,b)|\le1,\qquad
|\beta^2 a_N b_N(q-R^-(a,b))|\le2\beta^2.
\]
Both bounds are deterministic, uniform in the old-pair disorder, and valid
when $N=0$. In Part~C (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:cavity-path}) they are combined with
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-combination-bound} to give the uniform bound needed for dominated
differentiation under the old disorder.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Bound the absolute value of the truncated sum by the sum of the absolute
values of its $N$ Ising terms. Divide by $N+1$ and use $N/(N+1)\le1$.
The covariance bound follows by the triangle inequality and $|a_N|=|b_N|=1$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Gaussian interpolation tools}
\label{sec:sg-tal:cavity-tools}

\emph{Content.} Seven model-independent lemmas.  Differentiating under the integral over the old
disorder (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:conditional-derivative}); the derivative of the expected logarithm
of a $\cosh$ Gibbs average along the angle path, obtained by signing a Gaussian field
(Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:log-cosh-gaussian-derivative}, Eq.~(1.178) for $f=\cosh$); Gr\"onwall's
inequality run backwards from an endpoint, in the book's parameter $t$ and in the angle $s$
(Lemmas~\ref{lem:sg-tal:gronwall-backward} and~\ref{lem:sg-tal:gronwall-angle}); the domination of
a triple iterated integral and of a nested two-replica Gibbs average
(Lemmas~\ref{lem:sg-tal:iterated-bound} and~\ref{lem:sg-tal:nested-two-replica}), which
turn a derivative bound into an integrated one; and Lemma~1.4.2 for an observable of $n$ replicas
with a random base energy (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:conditional-replica-derivative}).
\emph{Uses:} the Gaussian interpolation theorem of Lemma~1.4.2.  \emph{Used by:} C and E.
No spin-glass model is needed here, which is why these lemmas come before the cavity-specific
arguments.  \emph{Status:} fully proved.

\begin{lemma}[Integrating a uniformly bounded conditional derivative]
\label{lem:sg-tal:conditional-derivative}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_conditional_of_deriv_bound,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_conditional_log_sum_exp_trig,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_conditional_log_integral_cosh_trig}
  \leanok
\talsource{no counterpart in the book; an analytic step inside the proof of Lemma~1.6.3, Eq.~(1.151)}
Let $P_0$ be a probability measure on a nonempty measurable space of old
disorder, and let $F,D:\mathbb R\times\Omega_0\to\mathbb R$.
Suppose $F(s,\cdot)$ is almost everywhere strongly measurable for every $s$,
$D(t,\cdot)$ is almost everywhere strongly measurable, $F(t,\cdot)$ is
integrable, and $\partial_s F(s,\eta)=D(s,\eta)$ for every $s,\eta$.
If $|D(s,\eta)|\le M$ uniformly, then
\[
\frac{d}{ds}\bigg|_{s=t}\int F(s,\eta)\,dP_0(\eta)
=\int D(t,\eta)\,dP_0(\eta).
\]
The specialization \texttt{hasDerivAt\_integral\_conditional\_log\_sum\_exp\_trig}
combines this Fubini step with the Gaussian log-partition derivative.  It keeps the three
outer measurability, integrability, and uniform derivative-bound obligations explicit, so it
applies to a random base energy without a cavity-path wrapper.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Apply Mathlib's dominated differentiation theorem with constant dominating
function $M$, which is integrable under a probability measure. This isolates
the Fubini/differentiation step needed after conditioning the Gaussian
replica formula on the old couplings. No spin-glass model is required.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Gaussian derivative of a logarithmic $\cosh$ Gibbs average]
\label{lem:sg-tal:log-cosh-gaussian-derivative}
  \lean{ProbabilityTheory.HasGaussianLaw.reindex_mul,
        SpinGlasses.log_integral_cosh_count_tilted_eq,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_log_integral_cosh_trig}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.6.8, Eq.~(1.178), specialized to $f=\cosh$}
Let $X=(X_a)$ and $Y=(Y_a)$ be a centered jointly Gaussian pair of fields on a finite
configuration space, with vanishing cross-covariance, and let $A$ and $h$ be deterministic.
Then
\[
 s\longmapsto \mathbb E\log\left\langle
   \cosh(\cos(s)X_\sigma+\sin(s)Y_\sigma+h_\sigma)\right\rangle_A
\]
is differentiable.  Its derivative is $\sin(s)\cos(s)$ times the two-replica covariance
difference for the signed fields $\varepsilon X_a$ and $\varepsilon Y_a$ on
$(a,\varepsilon)\in\alpha\times\{-1,1\}$, under their explicit tilted product measure.
This is the model-independent derivative needed by the cavity $\cosh$ path.  The covariance
bracket identity and signed-overlap domination have also been integrated over arbitrary
measurable old disorder.  General Gibbs lemmas now provide the outer measurability and
integrability of the logarithmic cosh path.  The remaining step for Eq.~(1.181) is to assemble
these declarations into the conditional derivative theorem, verify its derivative-integrand
measurability and uniform bound, and identify the two endpoints.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The identity $\cosh x=(e^x+e^{-x})/2$ turns the logarithmic Gibbs average into a signed log
partition function minus the constant base log partition function and $\log 2$.
The signed fields are obtained from $(X,Y)$ by deterministic reindexing and coordinatewise
scaling, so \texttt{HasGaussianLaw.reindex\_mul} preserves their joint Gaussian law.
Apply \texttt{hasDerivAt\_integral\_log\_sum\_exp\_trig}; the subtracted terms have zero derivative.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Gr\"onwall's inequality run backwards from the endpoint]
\label{lem:sg-tal:gronwall-backward}
  \lean{le_exp_mul_endpoint_of_neg_mul_le_deriv,
        le_exp_mul_endpoint_of_exists_hasDerivAt}
  \leanok
\talsource{the integration of Eq.~(1.155) in the proof of Lemma~1.6.4}
Let $g$ be continuous on $[0,1]$ and differentiable on $(0,1)$ with $g'\ge-Kg$ there. Then
$g(t)\le e^{K(1-t)}g(1)$ for $t\in[0,1]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
This is the time reversal $u\mapsto g(1-u)$ of the existing interval Gr\"onwall lemma
\texttt{NTK.gronwall\_exponential\_decay\_Icc}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Domination of a triple iterated integral]
\label{lem:sg-tal:iterated-bound}
  \lean{abs_integral_integral_integral_le,
        integral_prod_fun_fst_of_isProbabilityMeasure,
        integral_prod_fun_snd_of_isProbabilityMeasure}
  \leanok
\talsource{no counterpart in the book; the integration step of Lemma~1.6.4}
Let $\mu,\nu$ be measures and $\kappa(x,y)$ a family of measures. If
$|G(x,y,z)|\le H(x,y,z)$ pointwise and the iterated partial integrals of $H$ are integrable, then
$\bigl|\iiint G\,d\kappa\,d\nu\,d\mu\bigr|\le\iiint H\,d\kappa\,d\nu\,d\mu$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Apply the bound $\|\int g\|\le\int h$ for $\|g\|\le h$ with $h$ integrable three times.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Gr\"onwall's inequality along the trigonometric path]
\label{lem:sg-tal:gronwall-angle}
  \lean{Real.le_exp_mul_sin_sq_of_exists_hasDerivAt}
  \leanok
\talsource{the integration of $|\psi'(t)|\le K\psi(t)$, giving $\psi(t)\le K\psi(1)$, in the proof of Eq.~(1.182)}
Let $\varphi:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R$ be differentiable and suppose that
$\varphi'(s)\le K\sin(s)\cos(s)\,\varphi(s)$ for $s\in[0,\pi/2]$.  Then
$\varphi(s)\le e^{K\sin^2(s)/2}\varphi(0)$ for $s\in[0,\pi/2]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The function $s\mapsto\varphi(s)e^{-K\sin^2(s)/2}$ has derivative
$(\varphi'-K\sin s\cos s\,\varphi)e^{-K\sin^2(s)/2}\le0$ on $[0,\pi/2]$, so it is nonincreasing.
\emph{Deviation:} the book parametrizes the interpolation by $t=\cos^2 s$ and integrates in $t$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:gronwall-backward}); here the inequality is run directly in the angle, where
every derivative carries the factor $\sin s\cos s$, which avoids the change of variable on the
open interval $(0,1)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Nested two-replica Gibbs averages]
\label{lem:sg-tal:nested-two-replica}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integrable_nested_prod_gibbs,
        SpinGlasses.abs_integral_nested_prod_gibbs_le,
        SpinGlasses.integral_nested_prod_gibbs_affine,
        abs_integral_integral_le_of_forall_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{no counterpart in the book; the bookkeeping behind ``taking expectations in $g_i$, $z$ and $h_{N+1}$'' in the proof of Eq.~(1.181)--(1.182)}
Let $E(\eta,\omega)$ be an energy on a finite configuration space $\alpha$, depending measurably on
two independent sources of disorder $\eta\sim P_0$ and $\omega\sim P$ (probability measures), and write
$\langle\Theta\rangle_{\eta,\omega}=\int\Theta(a,b)\,d(\mu_{E(\eta,\omega)}\otimes\mu_{E(\eta,\omega)})$ for
the two-replica Gibbs average.  For deterministic $\Theta,G,H:\alpha^2\to\mathbb R$:
(i) the inner averages $\omega\mapsto\langle\Theta\rangle_{\eta,\omega}$ and
$\eta\mapsto\mathbb E_\omega\langle\Theta\rangle_{\eta,\omega}$ are integrable;
(ii) if $|G|\le H$ then
$|\mathbb E_\eta\mathbb E_\omega\langle G\rangle|\le\mathbb E_\eta\mathbb E_\omega\langle H\rangle$;
(iii) $\mathbb E_\eta\mathbb E_\omega\langle c_1\Theta+c_2\rangle=c_1\,\mathbb E_\eta\mathbb E_\omega\langle\Theta\rangle+c_2$.
Moreover, if $|F|\le M$ pointwise, then the iterated average of $F$ over any family of probability
kernels is bounded by $M$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Gibbs weights on a finite space are $e^{E_a}/\sum_b e^{E_b}$, which are measurable in
$(\eta,\omega)$, so every two-replica average is a finite sum of measurable bounded terms; this gives
(i).  Parts (ii) and (iii) follow from (i) and the domination lemma
(Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:iterated-bound}) and the linearity of the integral, using that every
measure involved is a probability measure.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Lemma~1.4.2 for an observable of $n$ replicas with a random base energy]
\label{lem:sg-tal:conditional-replica-derivative}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_conditional_replica_trig}
  \leanok
\talsource{Lemma~1.4.2 applied inside the proof of $|\psi'(t)|\le K\psi(t)$ below Eq.~(1.182)}
Let $X,Y$ be centered jointly Gaussian fields on a finite space $\alpha$ with
$\mathbb E X_aY_b=0$, $U(a,b)=\mathbb E Y_aY_b-\mathbb E X_aX_b$, and let $B(\eta)$ be a base energy
depending measurably on disorder $\eta\sim P_0$ independent of $(X,Y)$.  For a bounded observable $\Phi$ of
$n$ replicas, the function
$s\mapsto\mathbb E_\eta\mathbb E_\omega\langle\Phi\rangle_{\cos s X+\sin s Y+B(\eta)}$ has derivative at $t$
\[
 \mathbb E_\eta\Bigl[\sin t\cos t\;\mathbb E_\omega\Bigl\langle\Phi(\sigma^1,\dots,\sigma^n)
 \Bigl(\sum_{\ell,\ell'\le n}U(\sigma^\ell,\sigma^{\ell'})-2n\sum_{\ell\le n}U(\sigma^\ell,\sigma^{n+1})
 -nU(\sigma^{n+1},\sigma^{n+1})+n(n+1)U(\sigma^{n+1},\sigma^{n+2})\Bigr)\Bigr\rangle_t\Bigr],
\]
provided the conditional derivative, for fixed $\eta$, is bounded by a constant $D_m$ uniformly in
$\eta$ and the angle.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
For fixed $\eta$ the base energy $B(\eta)$ is a deterministic shift, so the replica derivative
formula of Lemma~1.4.2 (in the angle $s$) applies; the outer derivative is then taken under the
$\eta$-integral with the constant domination $D_m$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:conditional-derivative}).  The measurability of the outer integrands is
the measurability of finite Gibbs sums in $\eta$.
\emph{Deviation:} the book applies Lemma~1.4.2 to the whole $(N+1)$-spin system, whose Hamiltonian
$-H_t=\log w+u(t)$ contains the random old energy in the weights $w$; here the old couplings are
frozen first (they are independent of the interpolated Gaussians) and integrated afterwards.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The overlap interpolation path (Lemmas~1.6.2--1.6.4)}
\label{sec:sg-tal:cavity-path}

\emph{Content.} The path $\nu_s(f)=\mathbb E\langle f\rangle_{E_s}$ with
$E_s=\cos(s)X+\sin(s)Y+A$ interpolates between the true $(N+1)$-spin system ($s=0$, coupled
endpoint) and a system in which the last spin is decoupled ($s=\pi/2$, Lemma~1.6.2).
Its derivative, conditionally on the old disorder, is a combination of replica covariances
(Eq.~(1.151)); the diagonal terms cancel exactly, the full overlap replaces the truncated one
(Eq.~(1.152)), and the combination is bounded uniformly, which allows integrating over the old
disorder (Lemma~1.6.3) and then closing with Gr\"onwall (Lemma~1.6.4).
\emph{Uses:} A and B.  \emph{Used by:} D.  \emph{Status:} fully proved.  The statements here are for
observables of the overlap of the $(N+1)$-spin system; Part~E repeats the same mechanism for the logarithm
of a $\cosh$ Gibbs average on $N$ spins, so its proofs do not depend on this part.

\begin{lemma}[Coupled endpoint of the cavity path]\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-coupled-endpoint}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_sk_cavity_coupled_endpoint}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.147) at $t=1$, where $\nu_1=\nu$; see also Eq.~(1.143)}
Let $N,n\ge0$, $\beta,h\in\mathbb R$, and let $f$ be any real observable
of $n$ replicas of $N+1$ Ising spins. Split the pair disorder into independent
old pairs $g'$ and edges $g_i$, and introduce an independent standard normal
$z$. The expectation of the $n$-replica Gibbs average for energy
\[
E_0(\sigma)=\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{N+1}}
  \sum_{i<j<N}g'_{ij}\sigma_i\sigma_j
 +\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{N+1}}\sigma_N\sum_{i<N}g_i\sigma_i
 +h\sum_{i\le N}\sigma_i
\]
over $(g',g,z)$ equals the expectation of the same observable under the
full $(N+1)$-spin SK pair-disorder Gibbs measure. The variable $z$ does not
occur at this endpoint. The equality holds also for $n=0$ and $N=0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The unused scalar Gaussian integrates to one. Apply Fubini to the bounded
finite-replica Gibbs average, then transport the pair-disorder law using
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-disorder-split}. The pointwise equality of the
two energies is \texttt{sk\_cavity\_energy\_coupled\_eq}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Decoupled last spin in the finite Gibbs average]
\label{lem:sg-tal:decoupled-spin}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_pi_count_tilted_equiv,
        SpinGlasses.integral_pi_count_tilted_decoupled_spin}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.150), finite form}
Let $\alpha$ be a finite nonempty set, $A:\alpha\to\mathbb R$, $Y\in\mathbb R$, $F$ a function of
$n$ replicas of $\alpha$ and $I\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}$. Under $n$ independent copies of the Gibbs
measure on $\alpha\times\{-1,1\}$ with energy $A(a)+\varepsilon Y$,
\[
\mathbb E\Bigl[F(a^1,\dots,a^n)\prod_{r\in I}\varepsilon^r\Bigr]
=(\tanh Y)^{|I|}\;\mathbb E\,F(a^1,\dots,a^n),
\]
the last expectation being under $n$ copies of the Gibbs measure with energy $A$. Replica averages
are transported along any finite equivalence of the configuration space.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Write the replica average as a finite sum, split $(a,\varepsilon)$ coordinatewise, and factor the sum
over the signs by the hypercube product formula. Each sign factor equals $\tanh Y$ if its replica is in
$I$ and $1$ otherwise, since $\sum_{\varepsilon=\pm1}\varepsilon e^{\varepsilon Y}/(e^Y+e^{-Y})=\tanh Y$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The decoupled endpoint of the cavity path (Lemma 1.6.2)]
\label{thm:sg-tal:cavity-decoupled}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_decoupled_endpoint}
  \leanok
\talsource{Lemma~1.6.2, Eq.~(1.150)}
Let $f^-$ be a function of $n$ replicas of the first $N$ spins, $I\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}$ and
$Y=\beta z\sqrt q+h$ with $z$ standard Gaussian. At the decoupled endpoint $t=0$ of the cavity path,
\[
\nu_0\Bigl(f^-\prod_{r\in I}\varepsilon_r\Bigr)=\mathbb E(\tanh Y)^{|I|}\,\nu_0(f^-),
\]
where $\nu_0(f^-)$ is the average of $f^-$ under the $N$-spin Gibbs measure with the old-pair energy
(prefactor $(N+1)^{-1/2}\beta$ and field $h$), averaged over the old pairs.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
At the angle $\pi/2$ the energy is $A(\rho)+\varepsilon(\beta\omega_2\sqrt q+h)$. Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:decoupled-spin}
(transported along the $\mathrm{Fin.snoc}$ equivalence) gives the factor $(\tanh Y)^{|I|}$ at fixed disorder;
the old pairs and the scalar Gaussian are independent, so the two integrals factor.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Conditional cavity replica derivative]\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-conditional-deriv}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_cavity_conditional}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.151) (conditional precursor); path, Eq.~(1.147); fields, Eq.~(1.153)}
Fix $N,n\ge0$, $\beta\in\mathbb R$, $q\ge0$, and an arbitrary
base energy $A:\{-1,1\}^{N+1}\to\mathbb R$. Let $f$ be any real
observable of $n$ replicas. The independent new-edge Gaussian couplings
$g_i$ and scalar Gaussian $z$ define
\[
X_\sigma=\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{N+1}}\sigma_N\sum_{i<N}g_i\sigma_i,
\qquad Y_\sigma=\beta\sigma_N z\sqrt q,
\quad E_s(\sigma)=\cos(s)X_\sigma+\sin(s)Y_\sigma+A(\sigma).
\]
With $\nu_s^A(f)=\mathbb E_{g,z}\langle f\rangle_{E_s}^{\otimes n}$,
put $U(a,b)=\beta^2a_Nb_N(q-R^-(a,b))$, where
$R^-(a,b)=(N+1)^{-1}\sum_{i<N}a_i b_i$. Then for every $s\in\mathbb R$,
\[
\frac{d}{ds}\nu_s^A(f)=\sin(s)\cos(s)\,
\mathbb E_{g,z}\left\langle f(\tau^1,\ldots,\tau^n)
\left(\sum_{r,r'\le n}U(\tau^r,\tau^{r'})
-2n\sum_{r\le n}U(\tau^r,\tau^{n+1})
-nU(\tau^{n+1},\tau^{n+1})
+n(n+1)U(\tau^{n+1},\tau^{n+2})\right)
\right\rangle_{E_s}^{\otimes(n+2)}.
\]
The Lean statement writes both Gaussian and Gibbs expectations as explicit
integrals; it allows an arbitrary deterministic $A$, in particular the
old-pair energy after conditioning on its disorder. This is the conditional
precursor of Eq.~(1.151), not the fully averaged Lemma~1.6.3.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Use Lemma~1.4.2 for $X,Y$ with deterministic shift $A$.
Joint Gaussianity and all covariance hypotheses are supplied by
Lemmas~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-fields-gaussian} and
\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-field-covariance-difference}; there is no new
Gaussian integration by parts.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Diagonal cancellation for cavity replica covariances]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-diagonal-cancel}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.replica_covariance_combination_cancel_diagonal,
        sum_sum_offDiag_eq_two_sum_lt,
        sum_filter_lt_eq_sum_subtype,
        filter_offDiag_lt,
        SpinGlasses.replica_covariance_combination_eq_two_sum_lt,
        SpinGlasses.neg_half_replica_covariance_offDiag,
        SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_covariance_diagonal,
        SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_covariance_symmetric,
        SpinGlasses.neg_sk_cavity_covariance}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.95) and (1.151)}
Let $R$ be a finite replica index type and $U:\alpha\times\alpha\to\mathbb R$
have a constant diagonal $U(a,a)=C$. For any extended replica family,
the diagonal of the double sum in the four-term Gaussian replica combination
cancels exactly against the term $-|R|U(\tau_{n+1},\tau_{n+1})$.
Only the off-diagonal double sum, the $-2|R|$ cross term and the
$|R|(|R|+1)$ extra-replica term remain. For the explicit cavity kernel,
$C=\beta^2(q-N/(N+1))$ because Ising spins square to one and
$R^-(a,a)=N/(N+1)$. This proves the diagonal algebra required in
Eq.~(1.151), not the disorder-averaged derivative or its change of parameter.
When $U$ is symmetric, the remaining ordered off-diagonal sum is twice the
sum over strictly ordered pairs; this finite-order identity is factored into
a general `ForMathlib` lemma and then composed with cancellation, rather than
reproved for replica indices.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Split each inner finite sum at the diagonal; sum the common value $C$
and cancel it. The Ising instance uses
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-overlap-split}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform bound on the replica covariance combination]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-combination-bound}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.abs_replica_covariance_combination_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{Lemma~1.6.4, Eq.~(1.155): the term count after Eq.~(1.152)}
Let $R$ be any finite replica index type of cardinality $n$, let $\alpha$ be
any type of configurations, and suppose $|U(a,b)|\le C$ for every $a,b\in\alpha$.
For any extended replica family $\tau:\operatorname{Option}(\operatorname{Option}R)
\to\alpha$, the absolute value of the four-term replica combination in
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-conditional-deriv} is at most
$2n(2n+1)C$. In particular, the cavity covariance bound
$C=2\beta^2$ yields a bound independent of the old-pair disorder.
This does not yet establish differentiation under the old-disorder integral.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Apply the triangle inequality to the four finite sums and bound each
summand by $C$; the resulting counts are $n^2$, $2n^2$, $n$ and $n(n+1)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Pointwise count for the three replica families]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-family-count}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.abs_book_replica_combination_le,
        SpinGlasses.card_filter_lt_fin_prod}
  \leanok
\talsource{text after Eq.~(1.152): the right-hand side of (1.151) has $2n^2$ simple terms}
Let $K$ be any real function of two configurations with $|K|\le c$ and let $\tau$ be an
extended replica family indexed by $\mathrm{Option}(\mathrm{Option}(\mathrm{Fin}\,n))$. Then
\[
\Bigl|\sum_{\ell<\ell'\le n}K(\tau^\ell,\tau^{\ell'})-n\sum_{\ell\le n}K(\tau^\ell,\tau^{n+1})
 +\frac{n(n+1)}2K(\tau^{n+1},\tau^{n+2})\Bigr|\le 2n^2c.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The three families have $\binom n2$, $n$ and $1$ terms with weights $1$, $n$ and $n(n+1)/2$;
by the triangle inequality the total is at most $\bigl(\tfrac{n(n-1)}2+n^2+\tfrac{n(n+1)}2\bigr)c
=2n^2c$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform domination of the conditional cavity derivative]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-outer-bound}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.abs_cavity_conditional_derivative_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{Lemma~1.6.3, Eq.~(1.151); domination as in Eq.~(1.155)}
Fix $N,n\ge0$, $\beta\in\mathbb R$, $q\in[0,1]$, an arbitrary deterministic
base energy $A$ on $N+1$ Ising spins, and an observable $f$ of $n$ replicas
with $|f|\le M$. For the trigonometric interpolation of
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-conditional-deriv}, its explicit Gaussian
replica-derivative expression has absolute value at most
\[
M\,2n(2n+1)\,(2\beta^2).
\]
The estimate is uniform in $A$: in particular it does not depend on the
old-pair disorder after conditioning. This is a domination input, not the
old-disorder-averaged derivative (1.151).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Bound every covariance by $2\beta^2$, the four-term replica combination
by $2n(2n+1)$ times this bound, and then apply the probability-measure
bound on the tilted replica integral. The integral over the independent
Gaussian variables has the same bound, and $|\sin s\cos s|\le1$.
Measurability follows from the finite Gibbs-average measurability lemma.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Averaging a conditional cavity derivative]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-outer-derivative}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_cavity_outer,
        SpinGlasses.measurable_integral_pi_count_tilted}
  \leanok
\talsource{Lemma~1.6.3, Eq.~(1.151) (outer averaging step)}
Let $P_0$ be any probability law for independent base disorder, and let
$B(\eta,\sigma)$ be a jointly measurable base energy.  For $q\in[0,1]$ and
a bounded $n$-replica observable $|f|\le M$, average the conditional cavity
path of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-conditional-deriv} first over the
new-edge/scalar Gaussian variables and then over $\eta\sim P_0$.  The result
is differentiable in the angle, and its derivative is the $P_0$-average of
the explicit four-term conditional replica expression.

This is deliberately stated for an arbitrary base-disorder probability law,
not only SK old-pair couplings.  Taking $B$ to be the old-pair energy is the
outer analytic step required before converting the angle derivative to
Talagrand's Eq.~(1.151).  It does not itself perform the diagonal cancellation
or identify either endpoint.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The finite Gibbs formula makes both the conditional expectation and its
derivative measurable in $\eta$.  The uniform bound of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-outer-bound} is independent
of $\eta$, so the proof applies the general dominated-differentiation lemma
\texttt{hasDerivAt\_integral\_conditional\_of\_deriv\_bound} to the conditional
derivative already proved in Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-conditional-deriv}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The cavity path derivative (Lemma 1.6.3)]
\label{thm:sg-tal:cavity-path-derivative}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_sk_cavity_path_angle,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_sk_cavity_path_angle_offDiag,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_sk_cavity_path_square,
        HasDerivAt.comp_arccos_sqrt_of_sin_cos,
        Real.arccos_sqrt_cos_sq}
  \leanok
\talsource{Lemma~1.6.3, Eq.~(1.151)}
Let $N,n\ge0$, $\beta,h\in\mathbb R$, $q\in[0,1]$, and let $f$ be any real function of $n$
replicas of $N+1$ Ising spins. For the energy
$E_s=\cos s\,X+\sin s\,Y+B$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-conditional-deriv}, whose base
energy $B$ is the old-pair energy with the prefactor $(N+1)^{-1/2}\beta$ and the field $h$,
put $\nu_t(f)=\mathbb E\langle f\rangle_{E_s}^{\otimes n}$ for $t=\cos^2 s$, the expectation
being over the old pairs, the new edges and the scalar Gaussian. Then for $0<t<1$
\[
\nu_t'(f)=\beta^2\sum_{\ell<\ell'\le n}\nu_t\bigl(f\varepsilon_\ell\varepsilon_{\ell'}
 (R^-_{\ell,\ell'}-q)\bigr)
-\beta^2 n\sum_{\ell\le n}\nu_t\bigl(f\varepsilon_\ell\varepsilon_{n+1}(R^-_{\ell,n+1}-q)\bigr)
+\beta^2\frac{n(n+1)}2\nu_t\bigl(f\varepsilon_{n+1}\varepsilon_{n+2}(R^-_{n+1,n+2}-q)\bigr),
\]
where $\varepsilon_\ell$ is the last spin of the replica $\ell$, $R^-$ is the truncated overlap
with the normalization $N+1$, and the Gibbs averages on the right run over $n+2$ replicas.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The angle form (the base energy being random is handled by
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-outer-derivative} specialized to the product Gaussian law on the
old pairs) is rewritten with the constant diagonal cancelled and the symmetric off-diagonal
pairs counted once (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-diagonal-cancel}). The chain rule for
$t=\cos^2 s=u$, $s=\arccos\sqrt u$, turns the factor $\sin s\cos s$ into $-\tfrac12$
(Lean: \texttt{HasDerivAt.comp\_arccos\_sqrt\_of\_sin\_cos}). The factor $-\tfrac12$ is pushed under
the three integrals; it turns the kernel $\beta^2\varepsilon\varepsilon'(q-R^-)$ into the book's
$\tfrac{\beta^2}2\varepsilon\varepsilon'(R^--q)$ and the covariance combination into the three
families of (1.151) (Lean: \texttt{neg\_half\_replica\_covariance\_offDiag},
\texttt{neg\_sk\_cavity\_covariance}). In the Lean statement the observable is not marginalized: the
three families are written as integrals over $n+2$ replicas of $f(\tau^1,\dots,\tau^n)$ times the
kernel, which is equal to the $\nu_t$ of the statement by
\texttt{integral\_pi\_option\_count\_tilted\_marginal}.
\emph{Not formalized:} the second form (1.152) with $R$ in place of $R^-$ (it follows from
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-overlap-split} since $\varepsilon_\ell^2=1$ and the three
families have total weight $\binom n2-n^2+\binom{n+1}2=0$).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The cavity path derivative with the full overlap (Eq. 1.152)]
\label{thm:sg-tal:cavity-path-derivative-full}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.book_replica_combination_add_const,
        SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_kernel_full_eq,
        SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_integral_sk_cavity_path_square_full}
  \leanok
\talsource{Lemma~1.6.3, Eq.~(1.152)}
The derivative $\nu_t'(f)$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:cavity-path-derivative} is also given by the
same three families with $R_{\ell,\ell'}$ in place of $R^-_{\ell,\ell'}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Since $\varepsilon_\ell^2=1$, $\beta^2\varepsilon\varepsilon'(R-q)=\beta^2\varepsilon\varepsilon'(R^--q)+\beta^2/(N+1)$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-overlap-split}). A constant shift of the kernel does not change the
combination, because the weights $\binom n2$, $-n\cdot n$ and $\binom{n+1}2$ of the three families add up
to zero.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Domination of the cavity derivative]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-derivative-domination}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.abs_cavity_outer_book_derivative_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.155)}
Let $P_0$ be any probability law for the base disorder and $f\ge0$ a function of $n$ replicas.
For every angle $s$, the three-family derivative of
Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:cavity-path-derivative} (in the form with $n+2$ replicas) has absolute
value at most $4n^2\beta^2$ times the path average $\nu_s(f)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-covariance-bound} the kernel is bounded by $2\beta^2$, so by
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-family-count} the bracket is bounded by $4n^2\beta^2$ pointwise;
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:iterated-bound} passes this through the three integrals, and the extra two
replicas are marginalized (\texttt{integral\_pi\_option\_count\_tilted\_marginal}).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Domination of the cavity path (Lemma 1.6.4)]
\label{thm:sg-tal:cavity-domination}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_sk_cavity_path_zero,
        SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_path_square_le_exp_mul}
  \leanok
\talsource{Lemma~1.6.4, Eq.~(1.154)}
For $f\ge0$ a function of $n$ replicas of $N+1$ Ising spins, $q\in[0,1]$ and $0\le t\le1$,
\[
\nu_t(f)\le\exp(4n^2\beta^2)\,\nu(f),
\]
where $\nu(f)$ is the average of $f$ under the $n$-replica Gibbs measure of the full
$(N+1)$-spin SK system (the path at $t=1$, the angle $s=0$).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The path average is continuous on $[0,1]$ (its angle form is differentiable everywhere) and
differentiable on $(0,1)$ with $|\nu_t'(f)|\le4n^2\beta^2\nu_t(f)$ by
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-derivative-domination} and
Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:cavity-path-derivative}. Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:gronwall-backward} gives
$\nu_t(f)\le e^{4n^2\beta^2(1-t)}\nu_1(f)$. At the endpoint the scalar Gaussian drops out and the
split disorder is the $(N+1)$-spin SK disorder (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-coupled-endpoint}).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Concentration of the overlap (Propositions~1.6.5--1.6.7)}
\label{sec:sg-tal:cavity-overlap}

\emph{Content.} Applying C with an observable of $R_{1,2}-q$ and using H\"older's inequality and the
site symmetry of the Gibbs measure gives a contraction of the overlap variance
(Proposition~1.6.6) and, by iterating it, bounds on its higher moments (Proposition~1.6.7).
\emph{Uses:} C.  \emph{Used by:} only to turn $\nu(|R_{1,2}-q|)$ into a rate in $N$ (Part~E needs
no rate).  \emph{Status:} fully proved.

\begin{lemma}[Replica relabelling and the two-replica marginal]
\label{lem:sg-tal:replica-marginal}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_pi_option_option_count_tilted_marginal,
        SpinGlasses.integral_pi_count_tilted_reindex,
        SpinGlasses.integral_pi_count_tilted_pair}
  \leanok
\talsource{no counterpart in the book (used implicitly in Proposition~1.6.5)}
An $R$-replica Gibbs average is unchanged by a relabelling of the replicas, a function of $n$ replicas
has the same average among $n+2$ replicas, and a function of two distinct replicas has the average of two
independent replicas.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
All three are statements about the finite sum $\sum_\tau\prod_r w(\tau_r)\,\Phi(\tau)$ with $\sum w=1$:
reindex the sum, sum out the unused coordinates (hypercube product formula), or sum over the fibres of the
pair of coordinates.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[H\"older's inequality for an iterated average and its use for the cavity bound]
\label{lem:sg-tal:iterated-holder}
  \lean{integral_integral_mul_le_Lp_mul_Lq,
        integral_integral_finset_sum_le,
        SpinGlasses.sk_nested_holder_pair,
        SpinGlasses.sk_nested_holder_sum}
  \leanok
\talsource{H\"older step of the proof of Proposition~1.6.5}
For conjugate exponents $p,r$, nonnegative $A,B$ and a random finite probability measure $\kappa_g$,
$\int\!\int AB\,d\kappa_g\,d\mu\le(\int\!\int A^p)^{1/p}(\int\!\int B^r)^{1/r}$. For the SK average of
$n+2$ replicas, $\nu(|f|\,|R_{ab}-q|)\le\nu(|f|^p)^{1/p}\nu(|R_{1,2}-q|^r)^{1/r}$ for every pair $a\ne b$, and the
weighted sum of the $2n^2$ terms of Eq.~(1.152) is at most $2n^2$ times this bound.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
H\"older's inequality under each $\kappa_g$ and then for the resulting functions of $g$; the replicas
of $f$ and the two replicas of the overlap are marginalized by Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:replica-marginal}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The cavity interpolation bound (Proposition 1.6.5)]
\label{thm:sg-tal:cavity-holder}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.abs_sk_cavity_kernel_full,
        SpinGlasses.abs_two_sum_lt_combination_le,
        SpinGlasses.abs_sk_cavity_angle_combination_le,
        SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_path_square_le_exp_mul_replicas,
        SpinGlasses.abs_integral_nested_gibbs_le,
        SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_nu_sub_nu_zero_le,
        SpinGlasses.integral_nested_gibbs_finset_sum}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.6.5, Eq.~(1.156)}
For a function $f$ of $n$ replicas and conjugate exponents $p,r$,
\[
|\nu(f)-\nu_0(f)|\le2n^2\beta^2\exp\bigl(4(n+2)^2\beta^2\bigr)\,\nu(|f|^p)^{1/p}\,\nu(|R_{1,2}-q|^r)^{1/r}.
\]
\emph{Deviation from the book:} the constant is $\exp(4(n+2)^2\beta^2)$ instead of $\exp(4n^2\beta^2)$, because
the terms of (1.152) are averages over $n+2$ replicas, to which Lemma~1.6.4 applies with $n+2$ replicas.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Differentiate in the angle $s$ and bound the derivative by $\sin s\cos s$ times $2\beta^2\,\nu_s(|f|W)$, where
$W$ is the weighted sum of the overlap deviations (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-family-count} and
Eq.~(1.152)). Lemma~1.6.4 for $n+2$ replicas replaces $\nu_s$ by the coupled endpoint, Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:iterated-holder}
bounds the endpoint average, and $|\varphi(\pi/2)-\varphi(0)|\le K/2$ for $|\varphi'|\le K\sin s\cos s$ finishes.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Elementary power estimates]
\label{lem:sg-tal:add-pow-estimates}
  \lean{add_pow_le_mul_pow_add_mul_pow,
        pow_add_sub_pow_sub_le,
        abs_add_pow_two_mul_le,
        rpow_mul_rpow_le_mul_of_le_rpow_mul,
        Real.exp_half_le_two,
        Real.HolderConjugate.succ_div_self}
  \leanok
\talsource{no counterpart in the book (elementary inequalities used in the proof of Proposition~1.6.7)}
For $a,b\ge0$, $\theta>0$ and $n\ge1$, $(a+b)^n\le(1+\theta)^{n-1}a^n+(1+\theta^{-1})^{n-1}b^n$; in particular
$(|a|+b)^{2k}\le2a^{2k}+(1+4k)^{2k-1}b^{2k}$. For odd $n$ and $x\ge0$, $0\le\frac12((a+x)^n-(a-x)^n)\le
n\,x\,(|a|+x)^{n-1}$. If $Z\le c^pV$ for a conjugate pair $(p,r)$ then $Z^{1/p}V^{1/r}\le cV$. Finally $e^{1/2}\le2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Convexity of $t\mapsto t^n$ with the weights $1/(1+\theta)$ and $\theta/(1+\theta)$, and $(1+\frac1{4k})^{2k-1}\le e^{1/2}$;
the mean value inequality for $t\mapsto t^n$ (Mathlib's \texttt{abs\_pow\_sub\_pow\_le}) and monotonicity of odd
powers; the exponents of $V$ add up to $1/p+1/r=1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Site symmetry and the decoupled endpoint for a function of the overlap (Eqs.~(1.157), (1.158))]
\label{thm:sg-tal:overlap-odd-part}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_replica_site_perm,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_integral_sk_replica,
        SpinGlasses.integral_integral_sk_replica_mono,
        SpinGlasses.integral_integral_sk_replica_affine,
        SpinGlasses.units_cast_mul_eq_one_or,
        SpinGlasses.abs_units_cast_mul_sub_le_two,
        SpinGlasses.sk_overlap_mul_eq_last_site,
        SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_decoupled_odd_part,
        SpinGlasses.abs_units_cast_eq_one,
        SpinGlasses.units_cast_eq_one_or}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.157) and (1.158), for an arbitrary function $\varphi$ of $R_{1,2}-q$}
For every $\varphi:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R$, $\nu((R-q)\varphi(R-q))=\nu((\varepsilon_1\varepsilon_2-q)\varphi(R-q))$
(symmetry between sites), and if $q=\mathbb E\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt q\,z+h)$ then
$\nu_0((\varepsilon_1\varepsilon_2-q)\varphi(R-q))=(1-q^2)\,\nu_0\bigl(\tfrac12[\varphi(D+x)-\varphi(D-x)]\bigr)$,
with $D=R^--q$ and $x=1/(N+1)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The first identity is Eq.~(1.157): the average of $(\sigma^1_i\sigma^2_i-q)\varphi(R-q)$ does not depend on $i$
(relabel the sites) and the $N+1$ of them add up to $(N+1)(R-q)\varphi(R-q)$. For the second, $R-q=D+\varepsilon x$ with
$\varepsilon=\varepsilon_1\varepsilon_2=\pm1$, so $\varphi(D+\varepsilon x)=S+\varepsilon T$ with $S,T$ the even and odd
parts of $\varphi(D+\cdot x)$, and $(\varepsilon-q)(S+\varepsilon T)=\varepsilon S-qS+T-q\varepsilon T$. Lemma~1.6.2 gives
$\nu_0(\varepsilon\,F)=q\,\nu_0(F)$ and $\nu_0(F)=\nu_0^-(F)$ for functions of the first $N$ coordinates, so the $S$
terms cancel and the $T$ terms add up to $(1-q^2)\nu_0(T)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Contraction of the overlap variance (Proposition 1.6.6)]
\label{thm:sg-tal:overlap-contraction}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_overlap_pow_le_two_nu_zero,
        SpinGlasses.sk_overlap_pow_le_two_odd_part,
        SpinGlasses.sk_overlap_sq_le_two_div}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.6.6, Eqs.~(1.157)--(1.159)}
Let $q\in[0,1]$ satisfy $q=\mathbb E\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt q\,z+h)$ and $16\beta^2e^{64\beta^2}\le\frac12$. For the
$(N+1)$-spin SK model, $\nu((R_{1,2}-q)^2)\le 2/(N+1)$. More generally,
$\nu((R-q)^{2k+2})\le2(1-q^2)\,\nu_0\bigl(\tfrac12[(D+x)^{2k+1}-(D-x)^{2k+1}]\bigr)$ for every $k\ge0$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
By Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-odd-part}, $\nu((R-q)^{2k+2})=\nu(f)$ with
$f=(\varepsilon_1\varepsilon_2-q)(R-q)^{2k+1}$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:cavity-holder} with $n=2$, $p=(2k+2)/(2k+1)$ and
$r=2k+2$, and $|f|^p\le2^p(R-q)^{2k+2}$, $|\nu(f)-\nu_0(f)|\le16\beta^2e^{64\beta^2}\nu((R-q)^{2k+2})\le
\frac12\nu((R-q)^{2k+2})$, so $\nu((R-q)^{2k+2})\le2\nu_0(f)$ (this is the absorption of Eq.~(1.159)). The second part of
Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-odd-part} with $\varphi(a)=a^{2k+1}$ evaluates $\nu_0(f)$. For $k=0$ the right-hand
side is $2(1-q^2)x\le2/(N+1)$.
\emph{Deviation:} the smallness condition has the constant $64$ rather than the book's $16$ in the exponent, as in
Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:cavity-holder}, and $\beta_0$ is replaced by this explicit hypothesis.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Higher moments of the overlap (Proposition 1.6.7)]
\label{thm:sg-tal:overlap-moments}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_overlap_moment_arith,
        SpinGlasses.abs_sk_overlap_sub_le_two,
        SpinGlasses.sk_overlap_moment_step,
        SpinGlasses.sk_overlap_moment_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.6.7, Eq.~(1.160)}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-contraction}, for every $k\ge1$ and every $N$,
$\nu((R_{1,2}-q)^{2k})\le(64k/(N+1))^k$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Induction on $k$; $k=1$ is Proposition~1.6.6. Let $x=1/(N+1)$. If $16(k+1)x>1$ the claim is trivial, because
$|R-q|\le2$ and $64(k+1)x>4$. Otherwise Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-contraction} reduces
$\nu((R-q)^{2k+2})$ to $2(1-q^2)\nu_0(T)$ with $T=\frac12[(D+x)^{2k+1}-(D-x)^{2k+1}]$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:add-pow-estimates},
$0\le T\le(2k+1)x(|D|+x)^{2k}\le(2k+1)x(|R-q|+2x)^{2k}=:G$, since $D=R-q-\varepsilon x$. Lemma~1.6.4 for the two
replicas gives $\nu_0(G)\le e^{16\beta^2}\nu(G)\le2\nu(G)$, and the weighted Young inequality bounds
$\nu(G)\le(2k+1)x\,[\,2\nu((R-q)^{2k})+(1+4k)^{2k-1}(2x)^{2k}]$. With $\nu((R-q)^{2k})\le(64kx)^k$ and $16(k+1)x\le1$ the
right-hand side is at most $(64(k+1)x)^{k+1}$.
\emph{Deviation:} $\beta_0$ is replaced by the explicit smallness hypothesis; the book's
$\beta_0$ is not made explicit.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The free-energy increment (Proposition~1.6.8)}
\label{sec:sg-tal:cavity-prop168}

\emph{Statement.} For every $\beta,h$ and $q\in[0,1]$,
$|(N+1)p_{N+1}-Np_N-A(\beta,h,q)|\le K(N^{-1}+\nu(|R_{1,2}-q|))$ with
$A=\log2+\tfrac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q)^2+\mathbb E\log\cosh(\beta z\sqrt q+h)$.
The proof has four steps, each in its own part below:
an \emph{exact} identity (1.176) for the increment; an estimate (1.181) for the first term
(the cavity $\cosh$); an estimate (1.182) for the second term (the pair perturbation); and
the algebra that combines them.  The two estimates are interpolation estimates of the same type as
in Part~C, but for the logarithm of a Gibbs average on $N$ spins.
\emph{Status:} all four steps are proved (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:prop-168}); the deviations from the
book are collected in Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:dev-168}.

\subsubsection{Step 1: the exact increment identity (Eqs.~(1.172)--(1.176))}
No estimate is involved: the $(N+1)$-spin partition function at $\beta_+$ factors, pointwise, as
$2Z_N(\beta)\langle\cosh(\cdot)\rangle$, and taking expectations after the Gaussian rescaling gives
$\mathbb E\log Z_{N+1}-\mathbb E\log Z_N=\log2+\mathbb E\log\langle\cosh\rangle-\mathbb E\log\langle\exp(\text{pair block})\rangle'$.
\emph{Status:} proved.

\begin{theorem}[Pointwise cavity partition identity (Proposition 1.6.8, Eq.~(1.172))]
\label{thm:sg-tal:cavity-free-energy-split}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sum_exp_fin_snoc_cavity,
        SpinGlasses.log_sum_exp_fin_snoc_cavity,
        SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_energy_snoc,
        SpinGlasses.sk_beta_plus_coefficient,
        SpinGlasses.sk_beta_plus_variance,
        ProbabilityTheory.map_pi_gaussianReal_scale_eq_prod_linear_combo,
        ProbabilityTheory.integral_pi_gaussianReal_scale_eq_prod_linear_combo,
        SpinGlasses.map_pi_gaussianReal_sk_beta_plus,
        SpinGlasses.integral_log_partition_sk_beta_plus_eq,
        SpinGlasses.sum_exp_sk_cavity_snoc,
        SpinGlasses.sum_exp_sk_cavity_beta_plus}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.6.8, Eq.~(1.172)}
For $N>0$, put $\beta_+=\beta\sqrt{1+1/N}$.  Pointwise in the disorder of an
$(N+1)$-spin system,
\[
 Z_{N+1}(\beta_+)=2\sum_{\rho\in\{-1,1\}^N}
 \exp\{-H_N(\rho)\}\,
 \cosh\!\left(\frac\beta{\sqrt N}\sum_{i<N}g_{i,N+1}\rho_i+h\right).
\]
Equivalently this is $2Z_N(\beta)$ times the old-system Gibbs average of the displayed
hyperbolic cosine.  The Lean theorem deliberately states the unnormalised finite sum, so it
does not introduce a partition-function wrapper.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
First reindex a configuration by its first $N$ coordinates and its final spin
(\texttt{Fin.snoc}).  The sum over the two final-spin values is
$e^L+e^{-L}=2\cosh L$ (\texttt{sum\_exp\_fin\_snoc\_cavity}).  The pair sum and field split
algebraically by \texttt{sk\_cavity\_energy\_snoc}.  Finally
$\beta_+/\sqrt{N+1}=\beta/\sqrt N$ is \texttt{sk\_beta\_plus\_coefficient}.
The companion scalar identity \texttt{sk\_beta\_plus\_variance} is the variance computation
needed for the Gaussian-law change (1.174) of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-beta-plus-law}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Logarithmic cavity factorization (core of Proposition 1.6.8, Eq.~(1.176))]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-log-factorization}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.log_sum_exp_fin_snoc_cavity,
        SpinGlasses.log_integral_count_tilted}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.6.8, Eq.~(1.176)}
For any finite base energy $A$ and last-spin field $L$, the logarithm of the enlarged
partition function is
\[
 \log 2+\log\sum_\rho e^{A(\rho)}+
 \log\left\langle\cosh L(\rho)\right\rangle_A.
\]
This is the model-independent algebraic component of (1.176); the remaining specialization
inserts the SK old-pair energy and combines it with the two-block Gaussian law.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Apply the final-spin sum identity, then \texttt{log\_integral\_count\_tilted} to the strictly
positive observable $\cosh L$.  Positivity of the finite weighted sum justifies splitting the
logarithm of its factor $2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Gaussian coefficient split (Proposition 1.6.8, Eq.~(1.174))]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-beta-plus-law}
  \lean{ProbabilityTheory.map_pi_gaussianReal_scale_eq_prod_linear_combo,
        SpinGlasses.map_pi_gaussianReal_sk_beta_plus}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.6.8, Eq.~(1.174)}
For independent standard Gaussian edge arrays $g,g'$, the edge field
\[
  \frac\beta{\sqrt{N+1}}g+\frac\beta{\sqrt{N(N+1)}}g'
\]
has the same law as $(\beta/\sqrt N)g$.  The first Lean lemma proves the reusable statement
for every finite coordinate type and coefficients $a,b,c$ with $c^2=a^2+b^2$; the second
instantiates it for \texttt{FinPairs (N+1)}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Use the existing two-block Gaussian Gram-transfer theorem.  The coefficient of coordinate $i$
is represented by the Kronecker vector $c\,\mathbf1_{\{i\}}$; its covariance is diagonal and
agrees with the two independent coefficient vectors precisely when $c^2=a^2+b^2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Log-partition transport (Proposition 1.6.8, Eq.~(1.175))]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-log-partition-law}
  \lean{ProbabilityTheory.integral_pi_gaussianReal_scale_eq_prod_linear_combo,
        SpinGlasses.integral_log_partition_sk_beta_plus_eq,
        SpinGlasses.log_sum_exp_add_eq_log_sum_exp_add_log_integral_exp,
        SpinGlasses.log_partition_sk_pair_perturb,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_log_sum_exp_linear_gaussian,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_log_sum_exp_linear_gaussian_prod,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_log_partition_sk,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_log_partition_sk_pair,
        SpinGlasses.dotProduct_const_pair_spin,
        SpinGlasses.sk_pair_linear_combo_expand}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.6.8, Eq.~(1.175)}
Taking the logarithmic $(N+1)$-spin partition function as the observable in the Gaussian law of
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-beta-plus-law} yields an exact equality between its expectation at coefficient $\beta/\sqrt N$
and the expectation under the two independent edge-disorder blocks.  Pointwise, the latter
logarithmic partition function is the unperturbed log partition function plus the log Gibbs
average of the exponential independent perturbation.  The pointwise factorization has also been
passed through both Gaussian expectations, with the integrability and Fubini obligations explicit.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The reusable integral form transports every measurable observable of the finite Gaussian vector.
For the SK observable, measurability is exactly \texttt{measurable\_log\_sum\_exp}; expanding the
linear combination gives the displayed two-block Hamiltonian.  The second statement is the
generic finite-state log-partition perturbation identity applied to the base SK energy and the
independent pair-disorder perturbation.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact cavity free-energy increment identity (Proposition 1.6.8, Eq.~(1.176))]
\label{thm:sg-tal:cavity-free-energy-increment}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sum_exp_prod_units,
        SpinGlasses.log_sum_exp_prod_units,
        SpinGlasses.log_sum_exp_sk_cavity_snoc,
        SpinGlasses.integral_log_partition_sk_pair_perturb,
        SpinGlasses.integral_log_partition_sk_beta_plus_eq_perturb,
        SpinGlasses.integral_log_partition_sk_cavity_split,
        SpinGlasses.sk_free_energy_increment_eq}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.6.8, Eq.~(1.176)}
For every $N > 0$ and any $\beta, h \in \mathbb R$, the free-energy increment satisfies
the exact identity without Guerra interpolation:
\begin{align*}
(N+1) p_{N+1}(\beta, h) - N p_N(\beta, h)
&= \log 2 + \mathbb E \log \left\langle \cosh\left(\frac\beta{\sqrt N} \sum_{i \le N} g_{i,N+1} \sigma_i + h\right) \right\rangle \\
&\quad - \mathbb E \log \left\langle \exp \frac\beta{\sqrt{N(N+1)}} \sum_{i < j \le N+1} g'_{ij} \sigma_i \sigma_j \right\rangle',
\end{align*}
where $p_N$ is the standard SK free energy, the first expectation is over the $(N+1)$-spin cavity field,
and the second expectation is over the independent pair perturbation under the $(N+1)$-spin Gibbs measure at $\beta$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Combine the integrated cavity split \texttt{integral\_log\_partition\_sk\_cavity\_split}
(Eq.~(1.173)) with the integrated pair perturbation comparison
\texttt{integral\_log\_partition\_sk\_beta\_plus\_eq\_perturb} (Eq.~(1.175)),
using measure-preserving coordinate splits and Fubini transport.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Step 2: the cavity $\cosh$ term (Eqs.~(1.178)--(1.181))}
Interpolating the first term between the cavity field $X$ and the decoupled field $Y$ moves it to
$\tfrac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q)+\mathbb E\log\cosh(\beta z\sqrt q+h)$ at an error controlled by the overlap.
The covariance structure of the $N$-spin fields and a uniform variance bound (Gaussian side) are combined
with the sign-averaging of the two replicas (Gibbs side) to bound the derivative by
$\beta^2\cosh(2h)e^{2\beta^2}\nu$.  This is the same mechanism as Part~C, applied to $\log\langle\cosh\rangle$
on $N$ spins instead of to an observable on $N+1$ spins.
\emph{Uses:} A, B and the algebra of Step~1.  \emph{Status:} proved.

\begin{lemma}[Covariances and Gaussian law of the $N$-spin cavity fields]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-N-fields}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.hasGaussianLaw_linear_form_and_scalar_prod,
        SpinGlasses.hasGaussianLaw_sk_cavity_N_fields,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_cavity_N_edge_field_eq_zero,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_cavity_N_scalar_field_eq_zero,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_cavity_N_edge_field_mul,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_cavity_N_edge_mul_scalar_eq_zero,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_cavity_N_scalar_field_mul,
        SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_N_covariance_difference}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.178)--(1.180)}
For the $N$-spin cavity fields $X_\sigma = \frac{\beta}{\sqrt{N}} \sum_{i \le N} g_i \sigma_i$ and $Y_\sigma = \beta z \sqrt{q}$,
the family forms a jointly Gaussian vector under the product law of the new-edge disorder and $z$, with zero mean,
$\mathbb E X_\sigma X_\tau = \beta^2 R(\sigma, \tau)$, $\mathbb E X_\sigma Y_\tau = 0$, and $\mathbb E Y_\sigma Y_\tau = \beta^2 q$.
The covariance difference satisfies $\mathbb E Y^2 - \mathbb E X^2 = \beta^2(q - R(\sigma, \tau))$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Apply \texttt{hasGaussianLaw\_linear\_form\_and\_scalar\_prod} to the linear forms and Gaussian scalar, then compute moments
by independence.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform variance bound for the cavity field linear combinations]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-linear-combo-var}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sum_sq_units_add_or_sub_le,
        SpinGlasses.sum_sq_sk_cavity_linear_combo_le,
        SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_field_linear_combo_eq,
        Real.cosh_add_add_eq,
        Real.cosh_le_exp_abs,
        integrable_exp_prod_mul_gaussianReal,
        integrable_exp_mul_prod_gaussianReal,
        integrable_cosh_two_sum_mul_gaussianReal,
        integral_cosh_two_sum_mul_gaussianReal,
        integrable_cosh_sum_mul_add_mul_gaussianReal,
        integral_cosh_sum_mul_add_mul_gaussianReal,
        SpinGlasses.integral_cosh_sk_cavity_linear_combo_le,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_abs_sinh_mul_sinh_sk_cavity,
        SpinGlasses.integral_abs_sinh_mul_sinh_sk_cavity_le,
        Real.cosh_mul_cosh,
        Real.abs_sinh_le_cosh,
        Real.abs_sinh_mul_sinh_le_cosh_mul_cosh,
        cosh_sum_mul_add_eq,
        integrable_cosh_sum_mul_gaussianReal,
        integral_cosh_sum_mul_gaussianReal,
        cosh_two_sum_mul_add_eq,
        cosh_sum_mul_add_mul_eq}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proof of Eq.~(1.181)}
For any Ising configurations $\sigma, \tau \in \{-1, 1\}^N$, any angle $s$, and any sign $\pm 1$, the variance of
the linear combination $\cos s (X_\sigma \pm X_\tau) + \sin s (Y_\sigma \pm Y_\tau)$ under the cavity Gaussian disorder
is at most $4 \beta^2$.
Consequently, for every $m\in\mathbb R$, the Gaussian moment of the hyperbolic cosine is bounded
uniformly by
\[
  \mathbb E\cosh\!\left(\cos s(X_\sigma\pm X_\tau)
    +\sin s(Y_\sigma\pm Y_\tau)+m\right)
  \le \cosh(m)\exp(2\beta^2),
\]
and the two-replica cavity factor product satisfies
\[
  \mathbb E [ |\sinh(L_\sigma)| |\sinh(L_\tau)| ] \le \cosh(2h)\exp(2\beta^2).
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The Ising coordinate differences $(\sigma_i \pm \tau_i)^2$ are at most $4$, so their sum is at most $4N$, cancelling
the factor $1/N$. The scalar coordinate satisfies $(1 \pm 1)^2 \le 4$ and $q \le 1$. Squaring and summing with
$\cos^2 s + \sin^2 s = 1$ yields at most $4 \beta^2$.
The reusable product-Gaussian moment formula evaluates the displayed expectation as
$\cosh(m)\exp(V/2)$, where $V$ is that variance bound, and monotonicity of the exponential gives
the result. For the product of hyperbolic sines, we bound $|\sinh a \sinh b| \le (\cosh(a+b) + \cosh(a-b))/2$
and apply the moment bound at $m = 2h$ and $m = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Fixed-base signed-overlap domination for the cavity $\cosh$ path]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-signed-overlap-bound}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_covariance_bracket_eq,
        integral_mul_self_of_sq_eq_one,
        integral_mul_mul_eq_mul_integral,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_integral_prod_count_tilted,
        SpinGlasses.integral_prod_count_tilted_swap,
        SpinGlasses.integral_prod_count_tilted_mul_le,
        SpinGlasses.measurable_integral_prod_count_tilted,
        SpinGlasses.measurable_integral_prod_count_tilted_units_mul,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_integral_prod_count_tilted_det,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_integral_sk_cavity_signed_overlap,
        SpinGlasses.abs_integral_sk_cavity_signed_overlap_le,
        SpinGlasses.sum_exp_mul_pos,
        SpinGlasses.sum_units_int_mul_exp,
        SpinGlasses.sum_units_int_prod_mul_exp,
        SpinGlasses.sum_exp_prod_units_mul_two_replicas,
        SpinGlasses.integral_prod_count_tilted,
        SpinGlasses.integral_prod_count_tilted_eq_double_sum,
        SpinGlasses.mul_integral_count_tilted,
        SpinGlasses.integral_prod_count_tilted_units_mul,
        SpinGlasses.abs_integral_prod_count_tilted_units_mul_le,
        SpinGlasses.measurable_log_integral_cosh_count_tilted}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proof of Eqs.~(1.179)--(1.181)}
Fix an arbitrary base energy $A$ on the $N$-spin hypercube.  For
$L_\sigma(s)=\cos(s)X_\sigma+\sin(s)Y_\sigma+h$, the cavity Gaussian average of the signed
two-replica overlap term satisfies
\[
 \left|\mathbb E_\omega\left\langle
   \varepsilon^1\varepsilon^2\beta^2(R_{1,2}-q)
 \right\rangle_{A+\varepsilon L(s)}^{\otimes 2}\right|
 \le \beta^2\cosh(2h)e^{2\beta^2}
   \left\langle |R_{1,2}-q|\right\rangle_A^{\otimes 2}.
\]
The estimate is pointwise in the old-pair disorder and uniform in the interpolation angle.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
First integrate the two signs and discard the denominator using
\texttt{abs\_integral\_prod\_count\_tilted\_units\_mul\_le}.  The remaining tilted measure
depends only on $A$, so the finite Gibbs sum can be exchanged with the cavity Gaussian integral.
The coordinatewise factor is bounded by the $\sinh$ moment estimate of
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-linear-combo-var}.  The reusable
integrability and measurability lemmas make the triangle and Fubini steps explicit.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Old-disorder-averaged cavity bracket and domination]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cavity-outer-signed-overlap-bound}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.abs_log_integral_cosh_count_tilted_le_sum_abs,
        abs_integral_le_const_mul_integral,
        integral_const_add_of_isProbabilityMeasure,
        SpinGlasses.aestronglyMeasurable_integral_log_integral_cosh_count_tilted,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_integral_log_integral_cosh_count_tilted,
        SpinGlasses.abs_integral_integral_sk_cavity_signed_overlap_le,
        SpinGlasses.integral_integral_sk_cavity_covariance_bracket_eq,
        SpinGlasses.abs_card_inv_mul_dotProduct_sub_le_two}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proof of Eqs.~(1.179)--(1.181)}
Let $A_\eta$ be any measurable family of base energies under a probability law $P_0$.
For $N>0$, $q\in[0,1]$, and every interpolation angle $s$, averaging the estimate of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-signed-overlap-bound}
over $\eta$ gives
\[
 \left|\mathbb E_\eta\mathbb E_\omega\left\langle
   \varepsilon^1\varepsilon^2\beta^2(R_{1,2}-q)
 \right\rangle_{A_\eta+\varepsilon L(s)}^{\otimes 2}\right|
 \le \beta^2\cosh(2h)e^{2\beta^2}
   \mathbb E_\eta\left\langle |R_{1,2}-q|\right\rangle_{A_\eta}^{\otimes 2}.
\]
For each fixed $A$, the complete Gaussian covariance bracket integrates exactly to
$\sin(s)\cos(s)$ times $-\beta^2(1-q)$ plus the signed-overlap average above.
Moreover, for every finite Gibbs measure,
$|\log\langle\cosh L\rangle_A|\le\sum_a|L_a|$; hence coordinatewise integrability of
$L$ gives the outer measurability and integrability required by the conditional derivative
theorem.  The normalized overlap of arbitrary vectors in the finite unit cube differs from
any $q\in[0,1]$ by at most two.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The fixed-base estimate supplies a pointwise integrable majorant.  Measurability of finite
two-replica Gibbs averages and bounded-observable integrability justify the outer triangle
inequality.  The bracket identity follows by integrating
\texttt{sk\_cavity\_covariance\_bracket\_eq}; probability normalization leaves the constant
term unchanged.  Finally, $\cosh x\le e^{|x|}$ gives the logarithmic domination independently
of the Gibbs weights.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Endpoint reduction for the cavity $\cosh$ estimate (a derivative bound implies (1.181))]
\label{thm:sg-tal:cavity-cosh-endpoint}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_cosh_estimate_of_deriv_bound}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.6.8, Eq.~(1.181)}
For any interpolating path $\varphi$ between the cavity $\cosh$ expectation at $0$ and the decoupled
standard Gaussian expectation at $\pi / 2$, if its derivative along the trigonometric path has
deviation from $-\beta^2(1-q)$ bounded by $2 K_1 \nu$ on $[0, \pi/2]$, then
\[
  \left|\mathbb E \log \langle \cosh(u_\sigma + h) \rangle - \frac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q) - \mathbb E \log \cosh(\beta z \sqrt q + h)\right| \le K_1 \nu.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Apply \texttt{Real.abs\_sub\_sub\_half\_le\_of\_exists\_hasDerivAt} with $C = -\beta^2(1-q)$ and $K = 2 K_1 \nu$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Cavity \texttt{cosh} estimate (Eq.~(1.181))]
\label{thm:sg-tal:cavity-cosh-estimate}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.abs_sk_overlap_N_sub_le_two,
        Real.abs_sin_mul_cos_le_half,
        SpinGlasses.abs_integral_integral_sk_cavity_signed_overlap_le_two_sq,
        SpinGlasses.exists_hasDerivAt_sk_cavity_cosh_path,
        SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_cosh_estimate}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eqs.~(1.179)--(1.181)}
Let $N>0$, $\beta,h\in\mathbb R$ and $q\in[0,1]$.  Put $H_A(\rho)$ for the $N$-spin SK energy
with prefactor $\beta/\sqrt N$ and field $h$ in the old couplings $g'$, and let $\nu$ be the
$g'$-average of the two-replica Gibbs average of $|R_{1,2}-q|$.  Then
\[
 \left|\mathbb E\log\left\langle\cosh\Big(\tfrac{\beta}{\sqrt N}\sum_i g_i\rho_i+h\Big)
 \right\rangle_{g'}-\Big(\tfrac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q)+\mathbb E\log\cosh(\beta z\sqrt q+h)\Big)\right|
 \le\beta^2\cosh(2h)e^{2\beta^2}\,\nu .
\]
The path $s\mapsto\mathbb E\log\langle\cosh(\cos s\,X+\sin s\,Y+h)\rangle$ has derivative
$\sin s\cos s\,D$ with $|D+\beta^2(1-q)|\le\beta^2\cosh(2h)e^{2\beta^2}\nu$ at every $s$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Apply the conditional Gaussian derivative of $\log\langle\cosh\rangle$ with the
$N$-spin cavity fields $X_\rho=\beta N^{-1/2}\sum_i g_i\rho_i$ and $Y=\beta z\sqrt q$.
The covariance bracket integrates to $\sin s\cos s(-\beta^2(1-q)+\text{signed overlap})$; the
signed overlap is bounded by $2\beta^2$ (hence the domination constant $3\beta^2$) and, by
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-outer-signed-overlap-bound}, by $\beta^2\cosh(2h)e^{2\beta^2}\nu$.  At $s=0$ the integrand does not depend
on $z$, and at $s=\pi/2$ the Gibbs average of a constant is that constant, so the endpoints are
the two displayed expectations, and the endpoint reduction
(Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:cavity-cosh-endpoint},
\texttt{sk\_cavity\_cosh\_estimate\_of\_deriv\_bound}) concludes.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Step 3: the pair-perturbation term (Eq.~(1.182))}
The second term of (1.176) is the expected logarithm of the Gibbs average of the extra pair block
$\exp(\beta(N(N+1))^{-1/2}\sum g''_{ij}\sigma_i\sigma_j)$ in the $(N+1)$-spin system with old couplings
$g'$ and coefficient $\beta/\sqrt{N+1}$.  Write $a=(a_i)_{i\le N+1}$ for a configuration,
$B_{g'}(a)=\tfrac{\beta}{\sqrt{N+1}}\sum_{i<j}g'_{ij}a_ia_j+h\sum_ia_i$,
$u_a=\tfrac{\beta}{\sqrt{N(N+1)}}\sum_{i<j}g''_{ij}a_ia_j$ and $v=\tfrac{\beta}{\sqrt2}\,q\,z$ (the same scalar for all
$a$).  The proof interpolates $\psi(s)=\mathbb E\log\langle\exp(\cos s\,u+\sin s\,v)\rangle_{B_{g'}}$
from the quantity to be estimated ($s=0$) to $\mathbb E v=0$ ($s=\pi/2$) and has five parts.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Covariances.}  With $S=\sum_ia_ib_i$ one has
  $\mathbb E u_au_b=\tfrac{\beta^2}{2}\tfrac{S^2-(N+1)}{N(N+1)}$ and $\mathbb E v^2=\tfrac{\beta^2q^2}{2}$.
  Put $Z(a,b)=\tfrac{S^2-(N+1)}{N(N+1)}$.  Since $Z(a,a)=1$, the covariance bracket of Eq.~(1.178),
  diagonal minus off-diagonal, equals $-\tfrac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q^2)+\tfrac{\beta^2}{2}(Z(a,b)-q^2)$.
\item \emph{The overlap.}  Let $R=N^{-1}\sum_{i\le N}a_ib_i$ be the overlap of the first $N$ spins and
  $\varepsilon=a_{N+1}b_{N+1}$.  Then exactly $Z=R^2-(R-\varepsilon)^2/(N+1)$, hence
  $|Z-q^2|\le2|R-q|+4/(N+1)$.  So the derivative of $\psi$ is within
  $\beta^2\,\nu_s(|R-q|)+2\beta^2/(N+1)$ of $-\tfrac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q^2)$, where $\nu_s$ is the
  two-replica average along the path.
\item \emph{Comparison along the path.}  Lemma~1.4.2 for the observable $|R-q|$ of two replicas gives
  $|\nu_s'|\le20\beta^2\sin s\cos s\,\nu_s$ (the four covariance terms of the replica formula carry the
  weights $n^2+2n^2+n+n(n+1)=2n(2n+1)=20$ for $n=2$ and $|U|\le\beta^2$), so Gr\"onwall gives
  $\nu_s\le e^{10\beta^2}\nu_0$.
\item \emph{The coupled endpoint.}  At $s=0$ the two Gaussian blocks have coefficients $\beta/\sqrt{N+1}$
  and $\beta/\sqrt{N(N+1)}$ with squares adding to $\beta^2/N$, so by (1.174) $\nu_0$ is the two-replica
  average of the $(N+1)$-spin system with the single coefficient $\beta/\sqrt N=\beta_+/\sqrt{N+1}$.
\item \emph{The last cavity step.}  Summing out the last spin of both replicas replaces the
  $(N+1)$-spin average of $|R-q|$ by the $N$-spin average of $|R-q|\cosh L_1\cosh L_2/\langle\cosh L\rangle^2
  \le|R-q|\cosh L_1\cosh L_2$, with $L_\ell=\tfrac{\beta}{\sqrt N}\sum_ig_i\rho^\ell_i+h$ (since
  $\langle\cosh L\rangle\ge1$).  The Gaussian average of $\cosh L_1\cosh L_2=\tfrac12[\cosh(L_1+L_2)+\cosh(L_1-L_2)]$
  is at most $\cosh(2h)e^{2\beta^2}$ uniformly in the configurations, so $\nu_0\le\cosh(2h)e^{2\beta^2}\nu$.
\end{enumerate}
Altogether the derivative of $\psi$ is within $2\beta^2/N+\beta^2\cosh(2h)e^{12\beta^2}\nu$ of
$-\tfrac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q^2)$, and the endpoint lemma below gives (1.182) with
$K_2=\beta^2$, $K_3=\beta^2\cosh(2h)e^{12\beta^2}$.
\emph{Uses:} A, B and Step~1.  \emph{Status:} proved.

\begin{theorem}[Endpoint reduction for the pair perturbation estimate (a derivative bound implies (1.182))]
\label{thm:sg-tal:pair-perturb-estimate}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_pair_perturb_estimate_of_deriv_bound}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.6.8, Eq.~(1.182)}
For any interpolating path $\psi$ between the pair perturbation expectation at $0$ and $0$ at $\pi / 2$,
if its derivative along the trigonometric path has deviation from $-\frac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q^2)$ bounded by
$2 (K_2 / N + K_3 \nu)$ on $[0, \pi/2]$, then
\[
  \left|\mathbb E \log \langle \exp(\text{perturb}) \rangle' - \frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q^2)\right| \le \frac{K_2}{N} + K_3 \nu.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Apply \texttt{Real.abs\_sub\_sub\_half\_le\_of\_exists\_hasDerivAt} with $C = -\frac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q^2)$ and $K = 2(K_2/N + K_3 \nu)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Gaussian law and covariances of the pair-perturbation fields]
\label{lem:sg-tal:pair-perturb-fields}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.hasGaussianLaw_sk_pair_perturb_fields,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_pair_perturb_edge_field_eq_zero,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_pair_perturb_scalar_field_eq_zero,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_pair_perturb_edge_mul_scalar_eq_zero,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_pair_perturb_edge_field_mul,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_pair_perturb_scalar_field_mul,
        SpinGlasses.sk_pair_perturb_covariance_difference,
        SpinGlasses.sk_pair_perturb_bracket_eq}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proof of Eq.~(1.182): the formula for $\mathbb E u_{\tau^1}u_{\tau^2}$ via (1.63), and $U(\tau,\tau)$}
For $N>0$, the fields $u_a=\tfrac{\beta}{\sqrt{N(N+1)}}\sum_{i<j\le N+1}g''_{ij}a_ia_j$ and
$v_a=\tfrac{\beta}{\sqrt2}\,q\,z$ ($a\in\{-1,1\}^{N+1}$) form a centered jointly Gaussian family under the
product law of $g''$ and $z$, with $\mathbb E u_av_b=0$,
\[
 \mathbb E u_au_b=\frac{\beta^2}{2}\,\frac{S^2-(N+1)}{N(N+1)},\qquad
 \mathbb E v_av_b=\frac{\beta^2q^2}{2},\qquad S=\sum_{i\le N+1}a_ib_i .
\]
With $U(a,b)=\mathbb E v_av_b-\mathbb E u_au_b$ and $Z(a,b)=\frac{S^2-(N+1)}{N(N+1)}$ one has
$U(a,b)=\frac{\beta^2}{2}(q^2-Z(a,b))$, $U(a,a)=-\frac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q^2)$, and the covariance bracket
$U(a,a)-U(a,b)=-\frac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q^2)+\frac{\beta^2}{2}\bigl(Z(a,b)-q^2\bigr)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Joint Gaussianity and the moments follow from the generic coefficient-array lemmas
(Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-N-fields} is the analogue for the $N$-spin fields): $u$ is a family of
linear forms in $g''$ and $v$ a multiple of the independent $z$.  The covariance of the pair block is
$\frac{\beta^2}{N(N+1)}\sum_{i<j}a_ia_jb_ib_j$, and $\sum_{i<j}x_ix_j=\frac12[(\sum x_i)^2-\sum x_i^2]$
for $x_i=a_ib_i$, $x_i^2=1$.  The bracket identity is the algebra with $Z(a,a)=1$.
\emph{Deviation:} the book's $U$ is $\frac12(\mathbb E u u-\mathbb E vv)$ (the factor $\frac12$ comes
from differentiating in $t$ rather than in the angle); the formalized $U$ is $\mathbb E vv-\mathbb E uu$
and every derivative carries the factor $\sin s\cos s$.  The book writes the overlap through
$R'=\frac1N\sum_{i\le N+1}\sigma_i^1\sigma_i^2$, which is not used; $Z$ is expressed through $S$
directly.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Overlap algebra for the pair perturbation (the bound $|R'^2-q^2|\le3|R-q|+2/N$)]
\label{lem:sg-tal:pair-perturb-overlap-algebra}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_pair_perturb_overlap_decomp,
        SpinGlasses.abs_sk_pair_perturb_overlap_sub_le,
        SpinGlasses.abs_sk_pair_perturb_Z_sub_le,
        SpinGlasses.abs_sk_pair_perturb_Z_le_one}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proof of Eq.~(1.182): ``$|R'^2_{1,2}-q^2|\le3|R'_{1,2}-q|\le3|R_{1,2}-q|+2/N$''}
Let $R=N^{-1}\sum_{i\le N}a_ib_i$, $\varepsilon=a_{N+1}b_{N+1}$ and $q\in[0,1]$.  Then $S=NR+\varepsilon$ and
\[
 Z(a,b)=R^2-\frac{(R-\varepsilon)^2}{N+1},\qquad
 |Z(a,b)-q^2|\le2|R-q|+\frac4{N+1},\qquad |Z(a,b)|\le1 .
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Expand $(NR+\varepsilon)^2$ and use $\varepsilon^2=1$:
$Z=\frac{N^2R^2+2NR\varepsilon+1-(N+1)}{N(N+1)}=\frac{NR^2+2R\varepsilon-1}{N+1}$, which is the displayed
identity.  Then $|R^2-q^2|=|R-q||R+q|\le2|R-q|$ and $|R-\varepsilon|\le2$.  The bound $|Z|\le1$ follows
from $|S|\le N+1$.
\emph{Deviation:} the book bounds $|R'^2-q^2|$ by the cruder $3|R-q|+2/N$ with its own normalization
of $R'$; the exact decomposition above is used instead, which gives the better constants $2$ and $4/(N+1)$
(any constants would do).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Derivative of the pair-perturbation path (Eqs.~(1.178), (1.182))]
\label{thm:sg-tal:pair-perturb-path}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_hasDerivAt_sk_pair_perturb_path}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proof of Eq.~(1.182): $\varphi'(t)$ computed from (1.178) with $f=\exp$}
Let $B(\eta)$ be a measurable family of energies on $\{-1,1\}^{N+1}$ with integrable coordinates (for the
$(N+1)$-spin SK model, $\eta=g'$).  Then
$\psi(s)=\mathbb E_\eta\mathbb E_\omega\log\sum_a\exp(\cos s\,u_a+\sin s\,v+B(\eta)_a)$ is differentiable
with derivative $\sin s\cos s\,D(s)$, where
\[
 \Bigl|D(s)+\tfrac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q^2)\Bigr|\le\beta^2\,\mathbb E_\eta\mathbb E_\omega
 \bigl\langle|R_{1,2}-q|\bigr\rangle_{\cos s\,u+\sin s\,v+B(\eta)}+\frac{2\beta^2}{N+1}.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Apply the conditional Gaussian derivative of the log partition function
(Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:conditional-derivative}) with the fields of
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:pair-perturb-fields}.  Its derivative integrand is, by the bracket identity,
$\sin s\cos s\bigl(-\tfrac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q^2)+\tfrac{\beta^2}{2}\langle Z-q^2\rangle\bigr)$, which is bounded
by the constant $2\beta^2$ because $|Z-q^2|\le6$, giving the domination.  The affine map
$\langle\cdot\rangle\mapsto c_1\langle\cdot\rangle+c_2$ passes through the nested average
(Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:nested-two-replica}); Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:pair-perturb-overlap-algebra}
and monotonicity bound $\langle Z-q^2\rangle$ by $2\langle|R-q|\rangle+4/(N+1)$.
\emph{Deviation:} the book applies (1.178) to the whole system with weights
$w_\tau=\exp(-H_{N+1,\beta}(\tau))$; the old couplings are frozen and integrated afterwards.
The theorem holds for every base-energy family with integrable coordinates, not only the SK one.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Comparison of the overlap average along the path (the bound $\psi(t)\le K\psi(1)$)]
\label{thm:sg-tal:pair-perturb-gronwall}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_pair_perturb_overlap_path_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proof of Eq.~(1.182): $\psi(t)=3\mathbb E\langle|R_{1,2}-q|\rangle_t$, $|\psi'|\le K\psi$, $\psi(t)\le K\psi(1)$}
For $s\in[0,\pi/2]$ and the path energies of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:pair-perturb-path},
\[
 \mathbb E_\eta\mathbb E_\omega\bigl\langle|R_{1,2}-q|\bigr\rangle_s\le
 e^{10\beta^2}\;\mathbb E_\eta\mathbb E_\omega\bigl\langle|R_{1,2}-q|\bigr\rangle_{u+B(\eta)}
\]
(the right side is the coupled endpoint $s=0$).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:conditional-replica-derivative} to $\Phi(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)=|R(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)-q|\in[0,2]$
with $n=2$.  The kernel satisfies $|U|\le\beta^2$ (from $|Z|\le1$ and $q^2\le1$), so the replica combination is bounded
by $\bigl(n^2+2n^2+n+n(n+1)\bigr)\beta^2=20\beta^2$ times $\Phi$, and
\[
 \varphi'(s)\le20\beta^2\sin s\cos s\;\varphi(s),\qquad\varphi(s)=\mathbb E_\eta\mathbb E_\omega\langle\Phi\rangle_s ,
\]
after marginalizing the two extra replicas.  Gr\"onwall in the angle
(Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:gronwall-angle}) gives $\varphi(s)\le e^{10\beta^2\sin^2s}\varphi(0)$.
\emph{Deviation:} the book states only that $|U|\le K$ and obtains some constant $K$; here the
constant $20\beta^2$ is computed from the replica combinatorics, so the factor is $e^{10\beta^2}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The coupled endpoint: the Gaussian rotation (1.174) for an overlap average]
\label{lem:sg-tal:pair-perturb-endpoint}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_sk_pair_perturb_endpoint_transport}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proof of Eq.~(1.182): ``$\psi(1)=3\mathbb E\langle|R_{1,2}-q|\rangle_1=3\mathbb E\langle|R_{1,2}-q|\rangle_+$'' by (1.174)}
At $s=0$ the two-replica average of $|R-q|$ in the $(N+1)$-spin system with old couplings of
coefficient $\beta/\sqrt{N+1}$ and independent pair block of coefficient $\beta/\sqrt{N(N+1)}$ equals the
same average in the $(N+1)$-spin SK system at the single coefficient $\beta/\sqrt N$ (that is, at
$\beta_+$).  The scalar $z$ plays no role.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The average is a bounded measurable function of the coupling array, so Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-beta-plus-law}
(the Gaussian rotation, with $c^2=a^2+b^2$ by \texttt{sk\_beta\_plus\_variance}) transports it.
Integration over $z$ is trivial because the integrand does not depend on it at $s=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The last cavity step: summing out the last spin of two replicas]
\label{lem:sg-tal:pair-perturb-cavity}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_prod_count_tilted_equiv,
        SpinGlasses.sum_units_int_exp_mul,
        SpinGlasses.integral_prod_count_tilted_units,
        SpinGlasses.integral_prod_count_tilted_fin_snoc_cavity_units,
        SpinGlasses.integral_prod_count_tilted_fin_snoc_cavity_units_le,
        SpinGlasses.cosh_mul_cosh_sk_cavity_eq,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_cosh_mul_cosh_sk_cavity,
        SpinGlasses.integral_cosh_mul_cosh_sk_cavity_le,
        SpinGlasses.integral_sk_pair_perturb_cavity_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{End of the proof of Eq.~(1.182): the cavity method for $-H_{N+1,\beta_+}$, $\mathrm{Av}\exp\sum_{\ell\le2}\sigma_{N+1}^\ell(\cdots)$, and $\operatorname{ch}x\ge1$}
For $N>0$ and $q\in[0,1]$, the $(N+1)$-spin two-replica average of $|R-q|$ ($R$ the overlap of the first
$N$ spins) at coefficient $\beta/\sqrt N$, averaged over the couplings, is at most
\[
 \cosh(2h)\,e^{2\beta^2}\;\nu,\qquad \nu=\mathbb E_{g'}\bigl\langle|R_{1,2}-q|\bigr\rangle_{\text{$N$-spin SK at }\beta},
\]
and more generally for an observable $f\ge0$ of the first $N$ coordinates and an arbitrary base energy
$A$ and field $L$ on $N$ spins,
$\langle f\rangle_{A+\varepsilon L}^{\otimes2}\le\langle f\cosh L_1\cosh L_2\rangle_A^{\otimes2}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Reindex $(N+1)$-spin configurations by their first $N$ coordinates and the last spin
(\texttt{Fin.snoc}; the two-replica reindexing is \texttt{integral\_prod\_count\_tilted\_equiv}).
Summing the two last spins independently gives $e^{\pm L}$ factors, i.e.\ $2\cosh L$ for each replica
(\texttt{sum\_units\_int\_exp\_mul}), so the two-replica average equals
$\langle f\cosh L_1\cosh L_2\rangle_A/\langle\cosh L\rangle_A^2$; the denominator is at least $1$.
Splitting the disorder into old couplings and the last-spin edges is a measure-preserving
identification (\texttt{measurePreserving\_sk\_cavity\_disorder}).  For the edges, $\cosh L_1\cosh L_2=
\tfrac12[\cosh(L_1+L_2)+\cosh(L_1-L_2)]$ and \texttt{integral\_cosh\_sum\_mul\_gaussianReal} give
$\mathbb E\cosh L_1\cosh L_2\le\tfrac12[\cosh(2h)e^{2\beta^2}+e^{2\beta^2}]\le\cosh(2h)e^{2\beta^2}$,
because the total variance of $L_1\pm L_2$ is at most $4\beta^2$.  Since the Gibbs weights of the old system
do not depend on the edges, the Gaussian average is exchanged with the finite Gibbs sum.
\emph{Deviation:} the book writes the $\operatorname{Av}$ over the two last spins and takes
expectations in $g_{i,N+1}$ and $h_{N+1}$ together; here the sums over signs are done on the finite
configuration space, the exchange with the Gaussian integral is an exchange of a finite sum (no Fubini
