theorem for the Gibbs measure), and $h$ is deterministic.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The pair-perturbation estimate (Eq.~(1.182)) with explicit constants]
\label{thm:sg-tal:pair-perturb-estimate-proved}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_pair_perturb_estimate}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.6.8, Eq.~(1.182) and the bound of its last term by $K\nu(|R_{1,2}-q|)$}
For $N>0$, $\beta,h\in\mathbb R$ and $q\in[0,1]$,
\[
 \left|\mathbb E\log\left\langle\exp\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{N(N+1)}}\sum_{i<j\le N+1}g''_{ij}\sigma_i\sigma_j\right\rangle'
 -\frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q^2)\right|\le\frac{\beta^2}{N}+\beta^2\cosh(2h)\,e^{12\beta^2}\,\nu(|R_{1,2}-q|),
\]
where $\langle\cdot\rangle'$ is the Gibbs average of the $(N+1)$-spin system with old couplings $g'$ and
coefficient $\beta/\sqrt{N+1}$, and $\nu$ is the $N$-spin two-replica average at $\beta$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Let $\psi(s)=\mathbb E\log\sum_a\exp(\cos s\,u_a+\sin s\,v+B_{g'}(a))-\mathbb E\log\sum_a\exp B_{g'}(a)$.
At $s=\pi/2$ the sum equals $e^{v}\sum_a e^{B(a)}$ and $\mathbb Ev=0$, so $\psi(\pi/2)=0$.  At $s=0$, by
the integrated Gibbs factorization (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-log-partition-law}, Eq.~(1.175)) $\psi(0)$ is the
displayed expectation.  By Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:pair-perturb-path}, then
Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:pair-perturb-gronwall}, Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:pair-perturb-endpoint} and
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:pair-perturb-cavity}, the derivative of $\psi$ is $\sin s\cos s\,D$ with
\[
 \Bigl|D+\tfrac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q^2)\Bigr|\le\beta^2e^{10\beta^2}\cosh(2h)e^{2\beta^2}\nu+\frac{2\beta^2}{N+1}
 \le2\Bigl(\frac{\beta^2}{N}+\beta^2\cosh(2h)e^{12\beta^2}\nu\Bigr).
\]
The endpoint reduction (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:pair-perturb-estimate}, with $K_2=\beta^2$ and
$K_3=\beta^2\cosh(2h)e^{12\beta^2}$) concludes.
\emph{Deviation:} an earlier version of the formalized statement (the hypothesis of the conditional
theorems of Step~4) carried the $h$-free constants $K_2=K_3=3\beta^2e^{4\beta^2}$; they are replaced by the
constants above, which are what the proof gives, because the Gaussian average of
$\cosh(x+h)\cosh(y+h)$ produces the factor $\cosh(2h)$ and the Gr\"onwall step produces $e^{10\beta^2}$.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Step 4: assembly of Eq.~(1.170)}
The three theorems below show how the increment bound follows from the exact identity and the two
estimates, at increasing concreteness: the real-number algebra, the same statement for the SK system with
abstract constants, and the statement with explicit constants $K_1,K_2,K_3,K$ and $\nu$.
These three take the two estimates (1.181) and (1.182) as hypotheses; the final theorem
(Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:prop-168}) discharges both with Theorems~\ref{thm:sg-tal:cavity-cosh-estimate}
and~\ref{thm:sg-tal:pair-perturb-estimate-proved} and is Proposition~1.6.8.

\begin{theorem}[Conditional reduction of the cavity free-energy increment (the algebra of (1.170))]
\label{thm:sg-tal:cavity-free-energy-assembly}
  \lean{Real.abs_sub_sub_half_le_of_exists_hasDerivAt,
        SpinGlasses.sk_free_energy_step_algebra,
        SpinGlasses.abs_sk_free_energy_step_sub_le,
        SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_free_energy_step_le_of_estimates}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.6.8, Eq.~(1.170)}
The exact increment deviation from $A(\beta, h, q) = \log 2 + \frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q)^2 + \mathbb E \log \cosh(\beta z \sqrt q + h)$
decomposes algebraically as
\[
  ((N+1) p_{N+1} - N p_N) - A(\beta, h, q) =
  \left(\mathbb E \log \langle \cosh L \rangle - \frac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q) - \mathbb E \log \cosh\right)
  - \left(\mathbb E \log \langle \exp(\text{perturb}) \rangle' - \frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q^2)\right).
\]
Consequently, any quantitative bounds on the cavity $\cosh$ and pair-perturbation errors
yield the Proposition~1.6.8 bound $|(N+1)p_{N+1} - N p_N - A(\beta, h, q)| \le K(N^{-1} + \nu(|R_{1,2}-q|))$.
This is only a reduction: it takes the two estimates as input.  They are proved in
Theorems~\ref{thm:sg-tal:cavity-cosh-estimate} (Eq.~(1.181)) and~\ref{thm:sg-tal:pair-perturb-estimate-proved}
(Eq.~(1.182)).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Expanding $(1-q)^2 = 1 - 2q + q^2$ and combining with $\frac{\beta^2}{2}(1-q) - \frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-q^2)$ gives
the exact constant $A(\beta, h, q)$ by \texttt{sk\_free\_energy\_step\_algebra}.
The triangle inequality \texttt{abs\_sk\_free\_energy\_step\_sub\_le} and \texttt{sk\_cavity\_free\_energy\_step\_le\_of\_estimates}
complete the reduction.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Conditional SK cavity free-energy step reduction (abstract constants)]
\label{thm:sg-tal:cavity-free-energy-step}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.cavity_free_energy_increment_le_of_estimates,
        SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_free_energy_increment_le_of_estimates}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.6.8, Eq.~(1.170)}
For all $N > 0$ and $\beta, h, q \in \mathbb R$, combining the exact free-energy increment identity
(Eq.~(1.176)) with the cavity $\cosh$ estimate (Eq.~(1.181)) and the pair perturbation estimate (Eq.~(1.182))
yields the quantitative rate, provided the two displayed interpolation estimates are supplied:
\[
  |(N+1) p_{N+1}(\beta, h) - N p_N(\beta, h) - A(\beta, h, q)| \le K \left(\frac{1}{N} + \nu\right).
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Combine \texttt{sk\_free\_energy\_increment\_eq} with the assembly theorem
\texttt{sk\_cavity\_free\_energy\_step\_le\_of\_estimates}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Cavity free-energy increment bound with explicit constants, given the two estimates (1.181), (1.182)]
\label{thm:sg-tal:cavity-free-energy-increment-bound}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_constants_le,
        SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_free_energy_increment_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.6.8, Eq.~(1.170)}
For all $N > 0$, $\beta, h, q \in \mathbb R$ with $0 \le q \le 1$, the expected free-energy increment satisfies
\[
  |(N+1) p_{N+1}(\beta, h) - N p_N(\beta, h) - A(\beta, h, q)| \le K \left(\frac{1}{N} + \nu(|R_{1,2} - q|)\right)
\]
with explicit constant $K = 2 \beta^2 \exp(12\beta^2) \cosh(2h) + 1$, where $\nu$ is the $N$-spin two-replica
average at inverse temperature $\beta$, provided the two interpolation estimates (1.181) and (1.182) hold
with the constants $K_1=\beta^2\cosh(2h)e^{2\beta^2}$, $K_2=\beta^2$, $K_3=\beta^2\cosh(2h)e^{12\beta^2}$.  The two
estimates are Theorems~\ref{thm:sg-tal:cavity-cosh-estimate} and~\ref{thm:sg-tal:pair-perturb-estimate-proved};
applying this theorem to them gives Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:prop-168}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Follows from the exact increment decomposition (\texttt{sk\_free\_energy\_increment\_eq}), the arithmetic bounds
$K_2 \le K$ and $K_1 + K_3 \le K$ (\texttt{sk\_cavity\_constants\_le}, which uses $\cosh\ge1$, $e^{x}\ge1$ and
$e^{2\beta^2}\le e^{12\beta^2}$), and \texttt{sk\_cavity\_free\_energy\_increment\_le\_of\_estimates}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Talagrand's Proposition~1.6.8: the cavity free-energy increment]
\label{thm:sg-tal:prop-168}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_cavity_free_energy_increment}
  \leanok
\talsource{Proposition~1.6.8, Eq.~(1.170)}
For every $N>0$, all $\beta,h\in\mathbb R$ and every $q\in[0,1]$ (no smallness of $\beta$ and no fixed-point
equation for $q$),
\[
 \bigl|(N+1)p_{N+1}(\beta,h)-Np_N(\beta,h)-A(\beta,h,q)\bigr|\le K\Bigl(\frac1N+\nu\bigl(|R_{1,2}-q|\bigr)\Bigr),
\]
where $p_N=N^{-1}\mathbb E\log Z_N$, $A(\beta,h,q)=\log2+\frac{\beta^2}4(1-q)^2+\mathbb E\log\cosh(\beta z\sqrt q+h)$,
$K=2\beta^2e^{12\beta^2}\cosh(2h)+1$, and $\nu$ is the two-replica Gibbs and disorder average of
$|R_{1,2}-q|$ in the $N$-spin SK model at inverse temperature $\beta$ and field $h$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Since $(N+1)p_{N+1}-Np_N=\mathbb E\log Z_{N+1}-\mathbb E\log Z_N$, the exact identity (1.176)
(Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:cavity-free-energy-increment}) writes the increment as
$\log2+\mathbb E\log\langle\cosh L\rangle-\mathbb E\log\langle\exp u\rangle'$.  The cavity $\cosh$ term is within
$K_1\nu$ of $\frac{\beta^2}2(1-q)+\mathbb E\log\cosh(\beta z\sqrt q+h)$ by
Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:cavity-cosh-estimate}, and the pair term is within $K_2/N+K_3\nu$ of
$\frac{\beta^2}4(1-q^2)$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:pair-perturb-estimate-proved}.  Since
$\frac{\beta^2}2(1-q)-\frac{\beta^2}4(1-q^2)=\frac{\beta^2}4(1-q)^2$, the triangle inequality and
$K_2\le K$, $K_1+K_3\le K$ give the claim
(Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:cavity-free-energy-increment-bound}).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Deviations from the book in the proof of Proposition~1.6.8]
\label{rem:sg-tal:dev-168}
The statement is the book's, with $K$ explicit.  The proof follows the book (the exact identity (1.176),
two first-order interpolations, a Gr\"onwall step and a last cavity step) but differs as follows.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Constants.}  The book only asserts that $K$ depends on $\beta$ and $h$.  Here
  $K_1=\beta^2\cosh(2h)e^{2\beta^2}$ in (1.181), $K_2=\beta^2$ and $K_3=\beta^2\cosh(2h)e^{12\beta^2}$ in (1.182).
  The factor $e^{10\beta^2}$ comes from the Gr\"onwall step with the replica combinatorics computed
  explicitly ($2n(2n+1)=20$ for $n=2$, $|U|\le\beta^2$), and $\cosh(2h)e^{2\beta^2}$ from the last cavity
  step.  An earlier version of the formalized hypothesis for (1.182) had the $h$-free constants
  $3\beta^2e^{4\beta^2}$; the proof cannot give that, and the statement was corrected.
\item \emph{External field.}  The book writes the last-spin field as $h_{N+1}$ and takes the expectation
  in it together with $g_i$ and $z$.  The formalization keeps $h$ a deterministic real number; the
  dependence of $K$ on $h$ is through $\cosh(2h)$.
\item \emph{Conditioning on the old couplings.}  The book applies the Gaussian derivative formulas (1.178)
  and Lemma~1.4.2 to the whole system, with the old energy inside the weights $w_\tau$.  Here the old
  couplings $g'$ are frozen, the formulas are applied to the independent new Gaussians with the
  random shift $B(g')$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:conditional-replica-derivative} and the log-partition
  version), and the derivative is integrated over $g'$ using a constant domination.  The proof of
  Lemma~1.4.2 is not repeated.
\item \emph{The parameter.}  Every interpolation is run in the angle $s$ with $\cos s\,X+\sin s\,Y$, so
  derivatives carry the factor $\sin s\cos s$; the book's $t=\cos^2 s$, the factor $\frac12$ in its $U$ and
  its sign convention differ from the formalized $U=\mathbb E YY-\mathbb E XX$.  Gr\"onwall is applied in
  the angle (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:gronwall-angle}).
\item \emph{Overlap algebra.}  The book bounds $|R'^2-q^2|$ by $3|R-q|+2/N$ with $R'=\frac1N\sum_{i\le N+1}$.
  The formalization uses the exact decomposition $Z=R^2-(R-\varepsilon)^2/(N+1)$ and the
  constants $2$ and $4/(N+1)$.
\item \emph{Log of a Gibbs average.}  The path is on $\mathbb E\log\sum_a\exp(\cdot)$ minus the
  $s$-independent $\mathbb E\log\sum_a\exp B$; since the scalar field $v$ is the same for all
  configurations, it contributes $\mathbb Ev=0$ at $s=\pi/2$.  For the first term the log of the
  Gibbs average of $\cosh$ is turned into a signed log-partition function (index type
  $\{-1,1\}^N\times\{-1,1\}$) before differentiating.
\item \emph{Finite sums instead of Fubini.}  Averages over the last spins and the exchange of Gaussian
  integrals with Gibbs sums are carried out on the finite configuration space; the last cavity step uses the
  measure-preserving splitting of the pair disorder into old pairs and the $N$ new edges, and
  \texttt{Fin.snoc} for configurations.  Unlike the book, $N$-spin and $(N+1)$-spin systems are kept
  apart by explicit normalizations ($\beta/\sqrt N$ versus $\beta/\sqrt{N+1}$).
\item \emph{Not formalized.}  The summed form (1.171) of the proposition, the improved version with
  $\nu((R_{1,2}-q)^2)$, and the rate $K/\sqrt N$ obtained by combining the proposition with Part~D are
  not formalized as statements; Part~D supplies $\nu((R-q)^2)\le2/(N+1)$ for the $(N+1)$-spin system
  (apply it with $N-1$ to bound $\nu$ of Part~E) under its smallness hypothesis on $\beta$.
\end{itemize}
\end{remark}

\section{The Gaussian fluctuation principle (Talagrand Section~1.7)}
\label{sec:sg-tal:fluct}

\paragraph{The idea.}
Section~1.5 explains that, under the Gibbs measure, the random variable
$\boldsymbol\sigma\mapsto N^{-1/2}\sum_{i\le N}y_i\sigma_i$ with $y_i$ i.i.d.\ standard Gaussians
should be approximately Gaussian, and the cavity method of Section~1.6 needs this for the
\emph{centered} variable $\dot S=N^{-1/2}\sum_{i\le N}y_i\dot\sigma_i$, $\dot\sigma_i=\sigma_i-\langle\sigma_i\rangle$,
whose variance under the Gibbs measure is $N^{-1}\sum_i\dot\sigma_i^2\approx 1-q$.
Theorem~1.7.11 makes this precise for every smooth observable $U$: the Gibbs average
$\langle U(\dot S)\rangle$ differs from the Gaussian average $\mathbb E_\xi U(\xi\sqrt{1-q})$ by an amount whose
$2k$-th moment is $O(N^{-k})$.
The proof is a single interpolation, used with $2k$ replicas.
Raising $\langle V(\dot S)\rangle$ to the power $2k$ turns it into the Gibbs average of the product
$\prod_{\ell\le 2k}V(\dot S^\ell)$ over $2k$ independent replicas, where $V=U-\mathbb E_\xi U(\xi\sqrt{1-q})$ has
Gaussian mean zero.
One interpolates, with a parameter $t\in[0,1]$, between $2k$ independent Gaussians of variance $1-q$
($t=0$) and the $2k$ projections $\dot S^\ell$ ($t=1$), and applies Taylor's formula to
$\varphi(t)=\mathbb E\langle\prod_\ell V(\sqrt t\,\dot S^\ell+\sqrt{1-t}\,\xi^\ell\sqrt{1-q})\rangle$.
(The formalization uses the angle $s$, $t=\sin^2s$, and replaces Taylor's formula by a fencing argument in $s$; see Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:dev-17}.)
Two things have to be shown:
\begin{itemize}
\item the first $2k-1$ derivatives of $\varphi$ vanish at $t=0$.  The $r$-th derivative is a sum over lists of $r$
  pairs of replicas of products of the entries $T_{\ell\ell'}$ of the covariance defect times a Gaussian average; it
  vanishes unless every replica occurs in the list at least twice, because a replica occurring \emph{zero} times
  contributes the factor $\mathbb E V(\xi\sqrt{1-q})=0$, and a replica occurring \emph{once} makes the Gibbs average of the
  $T$'s vanish ($\langle\dot\sigma_i\rangle=0$).  A list of $r$ pairs has $2r$ slots, so $2r\ge 4k$;
\item the $2k$-th derivative is $O(N^{-k})$, because each entry $T_{\ell\ell'}$ is a centered overlap and has $2k$-th
  moment $O(N^{-k})$ (Eq.~(1.88), Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-moments-all}).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{How the section is organized.}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[A.] \emph{Gaussian interpolation of projections} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:fluct-gauss}): everything about the path
  that does not involve the Gibbs measure.  Fully proved.
\item[B.] \emph{Gibbs-side parity} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:fluct-gibbs}): the Gibbs average of the path functional vanishes at the
  start of the path.  Fully proved.
\item[C.] \emph{Taylor's formula in the angle} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:fluct-taylor}): a chain $Q_r'=\sin s\cos s\,Q_{r+1}$ with
  $Q_r(0)=0$ for $r<n$ and $Q_n$ bounded has $|Q_0(\pi/2)|\le M/(2^nn!)$.  Fully proved.
\item[D.] \emph{The Taylor step for a Gibbs measure} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:fluct-gibbs-taylor}): A, B and C combine to bound
  $\mathbb E_y\langle V(\dot S)\rangle^m$ by the Gibbs average of the products of Gram entries.  Fully proved.
\item[E.] \emph{Moments of the Gram entries} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:fluct-gram}; Eq.~(1.201)): $\int T_{\ell\ell'}^{2k}\le4^{2k}\int(R_{1,2}-q)^{2k}$.  Fully proved.
\item[F.] \emph{Theorem~1.7.11} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:fluct-main}): D and E combine, for a fixed Gibbs measure, into a model-independent bound by the overlap moment $J_k$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:gibbs-fluct-core}); the overlap moments of Section~1.4 (Eq.~(1.88)) then give the theorem for the SK model.  Fully proved.
\item[G.] \emph{Fixed-point shift} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:fixed-point-shift}; Lemma~1.7.5): $|q - q_-|\le 1/N$.  Fully proved.
\item[H.] \emph{Corollary~1.7.13} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:fluct-exp}; Eqs.~(1.206)--(1.207)): Theorem~1.7.11 for $U(x)=e^{\varepsilon\beta x}$ and $U(x)=xe^{\varepsilon\beta x}$, i.e.\ for the Gibbs average of the \emph{uncentered} exponential.  Fully proved, with constants uniform in $|\beta|\le\beta_0<1/2$.
\item[I.] \emph{Lemma~1.7.14 and Corollary~1.7.15} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:fluct-cavity-field}; Eqs.~(1.209)--(1.210)): the ratio $\langle\sinh(X+h)\rangle/\langle\cosh(X+h)\rangle$ is close to $\tanh$ of the thermal mean field, and the reaction term.  Fully proved.
\item[J.] \emph{Lemma~1.7.4, Eq.~(1.185)} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:fluct-last-spin}): $\langle\sigma_{N+1}\rangle$ is close to $\tanh$ of the cavity field built from the old Gibbs measure at $\beta_-$.  Fully proved.
\item[K.] \emph{Lemma~1.7.4, Eq.~(1.186)} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:fluct-old-spin}): adding a spin moves an old magnetization by $O(N^{-1/2})$ in $L^2$.  Fully proved.
\item[L.] \emph{Lemma~1.7.6} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:fluct-gaussian-field}): the last spin is a $\tanh$ of $\beta\sqrt q\,z+h$ for a standard Gaussian $z$ that depends on all couplings but is independent of the couplings of the first $N$ spins.  Fully proved, with $K=K(\beta_0,h)$.
\item[M.] \emph{Theorem~1.7.1} (Section~\ref{sec:sg-tal:fluct-iid}): for $n$ fixed the local magnetizations $\langle\sigma_i\rangle$, $i<n$, are within $K/N$ in $L^2$ of $\tanh(\beta z_i\sqrt q+h)$ for i.i.d.\ standard Gaussians $z_i$ that are functions of the couplings.  Fully proved, for $\beta\ge0$ and $N>n$.
\end{enumerate}
Parts~A--E are independent of the spin-glass model: Part~A is about Gaussian vectors, Parts~B--E about an arbitrary finite
Gibbs measure carrying $N$ bounded observables (Part~E needs $\xi_i^2=1$).  Only the last step of Part~F uses the SK model, through the overlap moments of Section~1.4.  Part~G is the fixed-point stability under the cavity parameter shift $\beta_-=\beta\sqrt{1-1/N}$.  Parts~H and~I specialize Part~F to the exponentials that the cavity method produces; they use the SK model through Part~F and through the overlap moments of Section~1.4 directly (the intermediate, disorder-by-disorder bound of Part~F is reused, not just its statement).

\begin{remark}[Conventions and deviations from the book in Section~1.7]
\label{rem:sg-tal:dev-17}
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{The hypothesis on $U$.}  The book assumes (1.196): $\mathbb E|U^{(\ell)}(z)|^k<\infty$ for every Gaussian $z$.
  The formalization assumes that $U$ is \emph{exponentially tame} (Definition~\ref{def:sg-tal:exptame}): smooth with every derivative bounded by
  $Ce^{c|x|}$.  This is a strengthening of the hypothesis.  It is what makes the Gaussian averages along the path
  \emph{uniformly} bounded, in $N$ and in the Gibbs configuration, without H\"older's inequality (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:projpath-bound}),
  and it covers every application of the section: $U(x)=e^{\varepsilon\beta x}$ and $U(x)=xe^{\varepsilon\beta x}$
  in Corollary~1.7.13.
\item \emph{Angle versus $t$; fencing instead of Taylor's theorem.}  The Gaussian path is differentiated in the angle $s$,
  $Z_s=\cos s\,(v\xi)+\sin s\,(u\cdot y)$, with derivative factor $\sin s\cos s$, exactly as in Sections~1.4 and~1.6.  The book's
  parameter is $t=\sin^2 s$, for which $\sqrt t\,\dot S+\sqrt{1-t}\,\xi\sqrt{1-q}$ is $Z_s$ with $u_{\ell}=N^{-1/2}\dot\sigma^{\ell}$ and
  $v=\sqrt{1-q}$.  The derivative in $t$ is $\frac12\sum T_{\ell\ell'}\partial_\ell\partial_{\ell'}$, i.e.\ the factor $2^{-r}$ of
  Eq.~(1.203).  The formalization never changes variable: the iterated derivatives $Q_r$ in $s$ satisfy $Q_r'=\sin s\cos s\,Q_{r+1}$, and a
  fencing argument for the boundary function $\frac{M}{j!}(\sin^2s/2)^j$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:taylor-vanishing}) gives the same bound $M/(2^nn!)$ as Taylor's formula in $t$,
  without one-sided derivatives at $t=0$ and without any continuity or domination argument for the $r$-th derivatives of the disorder average.
  A consequence: the Taylor step is carried out for each fixed disorder (Part~D), and only afterwards integrated over the disorder (Part~F).
\item \emph{Hypotheses on $\beta$ and $q$.}  As in Sections~1.4 and~1.5 the fixed point $q$ is a hypothesis
  ($q=\mathbb E\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt q\,z+h)$), and $\beta<1/2$ is stated as $4\beta^2<1$.
\item \emph{Common $y$.}  All $2k$ replicas are projected on the \emph{same} Gaussian vector $y$; this is why the path functional
  involves the Gram entries $u_\ell\cdot u_{\ell'}$ and not independent vectors.
\item \emph{Model independence.}  Parts~A--E and the core of Part~F are stated for arbitrary vectors $u_\ell\in\mathbb R^N$ and for an arbitrary finite
  Gibbs measure (with a vector field $u$ of Gibbs mean zero in Parts~B--D, and with observables $\xi_i$ in Parts~E--F), since only the SK overlap moments of Part~F use the model.  For the SK model $\xi_i(\boldsymbol\sigma)=\sigma_i$.
\item \emph{Crude constants.}  The constant $K$ is not tracked: the product of $2k$ Gram entries is bounded by the sum of their $2k$-th powers
  (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:amgm-pairprod}) and the four pieces of $T_{\ell\ell'}$ by convexity, which loses factors depending on $k$ only.
\end{itemize}
\end{remark}

\subsection{Gaussian interpolation of projections}
\label{sec:sg-tal:fluct-gauss}

\emph{Content.}  Fix $m$ replicas.  For deterministic vectors $u_\ell\in\mathbb R^N$ ($\ell\le m$) and a scalar $v$, the path
$Z_s=\cos s\,(v\xi)+\sin s\,(u\cdot y)$ interpolates between $m$ independent Gaussians of variance $v^2$ ($s=0$) and the $m$ projections
$u_\ell\cdot y$ of one Gaussian vector ($s=\pi/2$).  We compute all derivatives of $\mathbb E\prod_\ell V(Z_{s,\ell})$ in $s$,
show that they are bounded uniformly in $N$, and evaluate them at $s=0$.
\emph{Uses:} the derivative formula of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:gaussian-interp}, the Gaussian moment formulas of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:first-moment}.
\emph{Used by:} B, D and F.  \emph{Status:} fully proved.

\begin{definition}[Exponentially tame functions]
\label{def:sg-tal:exptame}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.ExpTame}
  \leanok
\talsource{replaces the integrability hypothesis (1.196) of Theorem~1.7.11}
A function $V:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R$ is \emph{exponentially tame} if it is infinitely differentiable and for every $j\in\mathbb N$ there are
constants $C_j,c_j\ge0$ with $|V^{(j)}(x)|\le C_je^{c_j|x|}$ for all $x\in\mathbb R$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Derivatives of tame functions are tame]
\label{lem:sg-tal:exptame-deriv}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.ExpTame.deriv_tame,
        SpinGlasses.ExpTame.iterate_deriv_tame}
  \leanok
\talsource{no counterpart in the book}
If $V$ is exponentially tame then so are $V'$ and every iterated derivative $V^{(n)}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The bound for the $j$-th derivative of $V^{(n)}$ is the bound for the $(j+n)$-th derivative of $V$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Products of derivatives, the covariance defect and the projection path]
\label{def:sg-tal:projpath}
\talsource{notation of the proof of Theorem~1.7.11: $F^{(\mathbf s)}$ in Eq.~(1.202), the entries $T_{\ell,\ell'}$, and the path $\mathbf X_t$}
Let $m,N\in\mathbb N$, $V:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R$ smooth, $u=(u_\ell)_{\ell\le m}$ with $u_\ell\in\mathbb R^N$, and $v\in\mathbb R$.
For a multi-index $a\in\mathbb N^m$ and $x\in\mathbb R^m$ put
\[
F^{(a)}(x)=\prod_{\ell\le m}V^{(a_\ell)}(x_\ell)\quad(\text{written out in Lean as the product of iterated derivatives, with no separate definition}),\qquad
T_{\ell k}=u_\ell\cdot u_k-v^2\delta_{\ell k}\quad(\text{written out in Lean}).
\]
Let $(\xi,y)$ have law $\gamma_m\otimes\gamma_N$, $\gamma_n=\mathcal N(0,I_n)$ (\text{written out in Lean as }\texttt{Measure.pi}\text{ and }\texttt{Measure.prod}), and set
\[
Z_s(\xi,y)_\ell=\cos s\,(v\xi_\ell)+\sin s\,(u_\ell\cdot y)\quad(\text{written out in Lean}).
\]
Then $Z_0=(v\xi_\ell)_\ell$ has independent $\mathcal N(0,v^2)$ coordinates, $Z_{\pi/2}=(u_\ell\cdot y)_\ell$ has covariance $(u_\ell\cdot u_k)$, and
$\mathbb E Z_{s,\ell}Z_{s,k}=\cos^2 s\,v^2\delta_{\ell k}+\sin^2 s\,u_\ell\cdot u_k$.
In the proof of Theorem~1.7.11, $u_{\ell}=N^{-1/2}\dot{\boldsymbol\sigma}^{\ell}$ and $v^2=1-q$, so that $T_{\ell\ell}=N^{-1}\sum_i(\dot\sigma^\ell_i)^2-(1-q)$ and
$T_{\ell\ell'}=N^{-1}\sum_i\dot\sigma^\ell_i\dot\sigma^{\ell'}_i$ for $\ell\ne\ell'$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Derivatives of $F^{(a)}$]
\label{lem:sg-tal:derivprod}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.ExpTame.contDiff_derivProd,
        SpinGlasses.ExpTame.hasFDerivAt_derivProd,
        SpinGlasses.ExpTame.abs_derivProd_le,
        SpinGlasses.ExpTame.exists_bound_upTo,
        SpinGlasses.abs_derivProd_le_of_bound}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.202)}
Let $V$ be exponentially tame and $a\in\mathbb N^m$.  Then $F^{(a)}$ is $C^\infty$, its partial derivative in the direction $e_k$ is
$\partial_kF^{(a)}=F^{(a+e_k)}$ (Eq.~(1.202): derivatives of the product act on one factor each), and there are $C,c\ge0$ with
$|F^{(a)}(x)|\le C\exp\bigl(c\sum_{\ell}|x_\ell|\bigr)$ for all $x\in\mathbb R^m$.
\emph{Quantitative form.}  If $|V^{(j)}(x)|\le C_je^{c|x|}$ for all $j\le J$ and all $x$, with $C_j,c\ge0$, and $a_\ell\le J$ for all $\ell$, then $|F^{(a)}(x)|\le\bigl(\prod_\ell C_{a_\ell}\bigr)\exp\bigl(mc\sum_\ell|x_\ell|\bigr)$.  For an exponentially tame $V$ the finitely many derivatives up to order $J$ always admit such $C_j$ and a common $c$ (take $c$ the sum of the rates).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Smoothness and the derivative follow from the product rule for $\prod_\ell V^{(a_\ell)}(x_\ell)$, only the $k$-th factor depends on $x_k$.
For the bound multiply the bounds $C_{a_\ell}e^{c|x_\ell|}$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:exptame-deriv} (with the common rate $c$) and use $|x_\ell|\le\sum_j|x_j|$ to get the exponent $mc\sum_j|x_j|$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First derivative along the projection path]
\label{thm:sg-tal:projpath-deriv}
  \uses{lem:sg-tal:gaussian-interp, lem:sg-tal:derivprod}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.ExpTame.hasDerivAt_integral_derivProd_projPath}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.40) applied to $t\mapsto\mathbb E_0F(\mathbf X_t)$ in the proof of Theorem~1.7.11}
Let $V$ be exponentially tame, $a\in\mathbb N^m$, and use the notation of Definition~\ref{def:sg-tal:projpath}.  The function
$s\mapsto\mathbb E\,F^{(a)}(Z_s)$ is differentiable on $\mathbb R$ with
\[
\frac{d}{ds}\mathbb E\,F^{(a)}(Z_s)=\sin s\cos s\sum_{\ell,k\le m}T_{\ell k}\;\mathbb E\,F^{(a+e_\ell+e_k)}(Z_s).
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The family $X_\ell=v\xi_\ell$, $Y_\ell=u_\ell\cdot y$ is jointly Gaussian (a family of Gaussian linear forms in the independent vectors
$\xi$ and $y$, the same fact as in Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:cavity-fields-gaussian}), centered, with $\mathbb E X_\ell X_k=v^2\delta_{\ell k}$,
$\mathbb E Y_\ell Y_k=u_\ell\cdot u_k$ and $\mathbb E X_\ell Y_k=0$, so that $\mathbb E Y_\ell Y_k-\mathbb E X_\ell X_k=T_{\ell k}$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:gaussian-interp} (in its differentiated form, Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:path-derivative}) gives the formula for a $C^2$ function whose first and
second partial derivatives are dominated by an integrable function.  By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:derivprod},
$\partial_\ell\partial_kF^{(a)}=F^{(a+e_\ell+e_k)}$, and each of $F^{(a)}$, $F^{(a+e_\ell)}$, $F^{(a+e_\ell+e_k)}$ is bounded by $Ce^{c\sum_\ell|x_\ell|}$;
along the path $\sum_\ell|Z_{s,\ell}|\le\sum_\ell(|X_\ell|+|Y_\ell|)$ because $|\cos s|,|\sin s|\le1$, and $\exp(c\sum_\ell(|X_\ell|+|Y_\ell|))$ is integrable because the coordinates are Gaussian.
\emph{Formalization note.}  The domination is carried out coordinatewise through the Gaussian moment generating function (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:projpath-bound}).
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Lists of pairs and the path functional]
\label{def:sg-tal:projsum}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.pairCount,
        SpinGlasses.projSum}
  \leanok
\talsource{the list $\ell_1,\ell_1',\dots,\ell_r,\ell_r'$ and the multi-index $\mathbf s$ above Eq.~(1.203)}
For $r\in\mathbb N$ a \emph{list of $r$ pairs} is a function $p:\{1,\dots,r\}\to\{1,\dots,m\}^2$, $p_j=(\ell_j,\ell_j')$.
Its \emph{multiplicity} is $\mathrm{pairCount}(p)_\ell=\#\{(j,e):p_j^{(e)}=\ell\}$ (the number of times $\ell$ occurs among the $2r$ entries of the list),
and $\mathrm{pairProd}(p)=\prod_{j\le r}T_{\ell_j\ell_j'}$.  The \emph{path functional} is
\[
\mathrm{projSum}_r(s)=\sum_{p}\mathrm{pairProd}(p)\;\mathbb E\,F^{(\mathrm{pairCount}(p))}(Z_s),
\]
the sum over all $m^{2r}$ lists of $r$ pairs.  (In the book $\varphi^{(r)}(t)=2^{-r}\sum\mathbb E\langle T_{\ell_1\ell_1'}\cdots T_{\ell_r\ell_r'}F^{(\mathbf s)}(\mathbf X_t)\rangle$, Eq.~(1.203), is $2^{-r}$ times the Gibbs and disorder average of $\mathrm{projSum}_r$.)
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Combinatorics of lists of pairs]
\label{lem:sg-tal:paircount}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.pairCount_cons,
        SpinGlasses.pairProd_cons,
        SpinGlasses.sum_pairCount,
        SpinGlasses.exists_pairCount_le_one_of_lt,
        SpinGlasses.card_le_of_forall_two_le_pairCount}
  \leanok
\talsource{the counting argument ending the proof of Theorem~1.7.11: ``since the length of the list is $2r$ we must have $2r\ge4k$''}
(a) Prepending a pair $(\ell,k)$ to a list $p$ adds $e_\ell+e_k$ to the multiplicity and multiplies $\mathrm{pairProd}$ by $T_{\ell k}$.
(b) The multiplicities of a list of $r$ pairs add up to $2r$.
(c) If $r<m$, some replica has multiplicity $0$ or $1$.
(d) If every replica has multiplicity at least $2$, then $m\le r$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
(a) is the definition, split off the first entry.  (b): each pair contributes two to the total.  (c): otherwise $2m\le\sum_\ell\mathrm{pairCount}(p)_\ell=2r<2m$.  (d): $2m\le2r$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[All derivatives of the path functional]
\label{thm:sg-tal:projsum-deriv}
  \uses{thm:sg-tal:projpath-deriv, lem:sg-tal:paircount}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.ExpTame.hasDerivAt_projSum}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.203), by induction on $r$}
For exponentially tame $V$, every $r\in\mathbb N$ and every $s\in\mathbb R$,
\[
\frac{d}{ds}\,\mathrm{projSum}_r(s)=\sin s\cos s\;\mathrm{projSum}_{r+1}(s).
\]
Consequently the $r$-th derivative of $s\mapsto\mathbb E\prod_\ell V(Z_{s,\ell})=\mathrm{projSum}_0(s)$, taken $r$ times with respect to the variable
$\tfrac12(1-\cos 2s)=\sin^2s$ on $(0,\pi/2)$, is $2^{-r}\mathrm{projSum}_r(s)$ (Eq.~(1.203)); that change of variable is not yet formalized.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Differentiate each term $\mathrm{pairProd}(p)\,\mathbb E F^{(\mathrm{pairCount}(p))}(Z_s)$ with Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:projpath-deriv}: the factor $\mathrm{pairProd}(p)$ does not depend on
$s$.  The result is $\sin s\cos s\sum_p\sum_{\ell,k}\mathrm{pairProd}(p)T_{\ell k}\mathbb E F^{(\mathrm{pairCount}(p)+e_\ell+e_k)}(Z_s)$.  Index the pairs $(p,(\ell,k))$ by the lists $(\ell,k)::p$ of length
$r+1$ (the equivalence \texttt{Fin.consEquiv}) and apply Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:paircount}(a).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Gaussian moments along the path]
\label{lem:sg-tal:projpath-mgf}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.projPath_apply_exp,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_exp_mul_projPath,
        SpinGlasses.integral_exp_mul_projPath,
        SpinGlasses.exp_sum_le_avg_exp,
        SpinGlasses.exp_abs_le_add,
        SpinGlasses.ExpTame.integrable_derivProd_projPath}
  \leanok
\talsource{no counterpart in the book (the book uses H\"older's inequality and (1.196))}
For every $b\in\mathbb R$ and every replica $\ell$, $e^{bZ_{s,\ell}}$ is integrable and
\[
\mathbb E\,e^{bZ_{s,\ell}}=\exp\Bigl(\frac{b^2}{2}\bigl(\cos^2s\,v^2+\sin^2s\,u_\ell\cdot u_\ell\bigr)\Bigr).
\]
Moreover $\exp(\sum_\ell a_\ell)\le\frac1m\sum_\ell\exp(ma_\ell)$ for $m\ge1$, and $e^{|x|}\le e^x+e^{-x}$.  Consequently $F^{(a)}(Z_s)$ is integrable for every
exponentially tame $V$, every $a\in\mathbb N^m$ and every $s$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
$bZ_{s,\ell}=b\cos s\,v\xi_\ell+\sum_i b\sin s\,u_{\ell i}y_i$ is a linear form in independent standard Gaussians, so the product moment generating function of
Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:first-moment} applies.  The inequality $\exp(\sum a_\ell)\le\frac1m\sum\exp(ma_\ell)$ is Jensen's inequality for the exponential with
the weights $1/m$.  For integrability, Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:derivprod} bounds $|F^{(a)}(Z_s)|$ by $C\exp(c\sum_\ell|Z_{s,\ell}|)$; apply the two inequalities and
the moment generating function with $b=\pm mc$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A bound on the path functional, uniform in $N$ and in the observable]
\label{lem:sg-tal:projpath-bound}
  \uses{lem:sg-tal:projpath-mgf}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.abs_integral_derivProd_projPath_le,
        SpinGlasses.abs_dotProduct_le_of_self_le,
        SpinGlasses.abs_projGram_le,
        SpinGlasses.pairCount_le,
        SpinGlasses.exists_abs_projSum_le_of_bound}
  \leanok
\talsource{the bound $|\varphi^{(r)}(t)|\le KN^{-r/2}$ in the proof of Theorem~1.7.11, here without the Gibbs average}
Let $m\ge1$, $r\ge0$, $B\ge0$, and let $C_j\ge0$ ($j\le2r$) and $c\ge0$ be given.  There is $C\ge0$, depending only on $m,r,B,(C_j)_{j\le2r},c$ and \emph{not on $V$}, such that for every smooth $V$ with $|V^{(j)}(x)|\le C_je^{c|x|}$ ($j\le2r$), every $N$, all vectors $u_\ell\in\mathbb R^N$ with $u_\ell\cdot u_\ell\le B$, every $v$ with $v^2\le B$ and every $s\in\mathbb R$,
\[
\bigl|\mathrm{projSum}_r(s)\bigr|\le C\sum_{p}\bigl|\mathrm{pairProd}(p)\bigr|,
\]
the sum over lists of $r$ pairs.  The single-multiindex ingredient is: if $|F^{(a)}(x)|\le C_0\exp(c'\sum_\ell|x_\ell|)$ then $|\mathbb E\,F^{(a)}(Z_s)|\le2C_0\exp(\frac{(mc')^2}{2}\max(B,0))$.  Moreover $|u_\ell\cdot u_k|\le B$ and $|T_{\ell k}|\le2B$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
By Jensen's inequality, $|F^{(a)}(x)|\le C_0\frac1m\sum_\ell(e^{b x_\ell}+e^{-bx_\ell})$ with $b=mc'$.  By
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:projpath-mgf} each of the $2m$ expectations is $\exp(\frac{b^2}2(\cos^2s\,v^2+\sin^2s\,u_\ell\cdot u_\ell))\le\exp(b^2B/2)$, hence
$|\mathbb E F^{(a)}(Z_s)|\le 2C_0\exp(b^2B/2)$, which depends on neither $N$ nor $s$.
The bound $|u\cdot u'|\le B$ is $|u\cdot u'|\le\frac12(u\cdot u+u'\cdot u')$, and $|T_{\ell\ell}|\le2B$ since $0\le u_\ell\cdot u_\ell\le B$ and $0\le v^2\le B$.
For the last claim take absolute values term by term and apply the first bound to each of the finitely many multiplicities $a=\mathrm{pairCount}(p)$; each multiplicity is at most $2r$ (the total multiplicity of a list of $r$ pairs is $2r$), so only the derivatives of order $\le2r$ enter, with $C_0=\prod_\ell C_{a_\ell}$ and $c'=mc$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:derivprod}).  The resulting constant is the sum over the $(m^2)^r$ lists of $2\prod_\ell C_{\mathrm{pairCount}(p)_\ell}\exp(\frac{(m^2c)^2}2\max(B,0))$, which does not involve $V$.
\emph{Design note.}  The book only needs a constant for one fixed $U$.  We keep the dependence on $V$ explicit through the bounds $C_j,c$ because Section~1.7 is applied to the families $U(x)=e^{\varepsilon\beta x}$, $xe^{\varepsilon\beta x}$ with $|\beta|\le\beta_0$ (Corollary~\ref{thm:sg-tal:cor-1713}), and the book's claim that the constants stay bounded as $\beta$ varies is exactly this uniformity.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Continuity and measurability of the path]
\label{lem:sg-tal:projpath-meas}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.continuous_projPath,
        SpinGlasses.continuous_projPath_uncurry,
        SpinGlasses.ExpTame.measurable_projSum}
  \leanok
\talsource{no counterpart in the book (measurability is implicit in taking $\mathbb E\langle\cdot\rangle$ of $\mathrm{projSum}$)}
For fixed $v,s$, the map $(u,\xi,y)\mapsto Z_s(\xi,y)$ and, for fixed $u,v$, the map $(\xi,y)\mapsto Z_s$ are continuous, and for exponentially tame $V$ the
function $u\mapsto\mathrm{projSum}_r(s)$ is measurable on $(\mathbb R^N)^m$.  Hence $\mathbb E\langle\mathrm{projSum}_r\rangle$ makes sense when $u$ is a measurable function of the Gibbs configurations and the disorder.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The coordinates of $Z_s$ are polynomials in the entries of $u$, $\xi$, $y$.  The map $u\mapsto\mathrm{projSum}_r(s)$ is a finite sum of products of the continuous entries $T_{\ell k}$ and of
the parameter integral $u\mapsto\mathbb E F^{(a)}(Z_s)$ of a jointly continuous function, which is measurable.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The start of the path]
\label{lem:sg-tal:projpath-zero}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.projPath_zero,
        SpinGlasses.integral_derivProd_projPath_zero,
        SpinGlasses.ExpTame.integrable_derivProd_scale_gaussian,
        MeasureTheory.integral_pi_prod_eq_zero_of_coord_zero,
        SpinGlasses.ExpTame.integral_derivProd_projPath_zero_eq_zero}
  \leanok
\talsource{``$\mathbb E F^{(\mathbf s)}((\xi^\ell\sqrt{1-q})_\ell)=\prod_\ell\mathbb E V^{(s_\ell)}(\xi\sqrt{1-q})=0$ unless $\forall\ell,s_\ell\ge1$'', Eqs.~(1.199) and (1.204)}
At $s=0$ the path is $Z_0=(v\xi_\ell)_\ell$ and $\mathbb E F^{(a)}(Z_0)=\int\prod_\ell V^{(a_\ell)}(v\xi_\ell)\,d\gamma_m(\xi)$, which does not depend on $u$.
If $V$ is exponentially tame, $m\ge1$, $a_j=0$ for some replica $j$, and $\int V(vt)\,d\gamma_1(t)=0$, then $\mathbb E F^{(a)}(Z_0)=0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
At $s=0$ the $y$-component drops out and the integral over $\xi$ remains (a product of probability measures).  Since the integrand is the product over $\ell$ of one-variable functions of
$\xi_\ell$, it is integrable (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:projpath-mgf} at $s=0$), and when one factor, here $\xi_j\mapsto V(v\xi_j)$, has integral zero, Fubini gives that the integral of the
product is zero (\texttt{integral\_pi\_prod\_eq\_zero\_of\_coord\_zero}).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Gibbs-side parity}
\label{sec:sg-tal:fluct-gibbs}

\emph{Content.}  Let $\mu$ be a Gibbs measure on a finite set $\alpha$ ($\mu(a)\propto e^{f(a)}$) and $\xi_1,\dots,\xi_N:\alpha\to\mathbb R$ observables, with Gibbs means
$\langle\xi_i\rangle=\int\xi_i\,d\mu$.  For $m$ replicas $\boldsymbol\sigma=(\sigma^1,\dots,\sigma^m)$ distributed according to $\mu^{\otimes m}$, take the \emph{centered scaled} data
$u_{\ell i}=N^{-1/2}(\xi_i(\sigma^\ell)-\langle\xi_i\rangle)$, so that $T_{\ell k}=N^{-1}\sum_i\dot\xi_i(\sigma^\ell)\dot\xi_i(\sigma^k)-v^2\delta_{\ell k}$.
We show that products of the $T_{\ell k}$ in which some replica occurs exactly once have Gibbs average zero, and conclude that $\langle\mathrm{projSum}_r(0)\rangle=0$ for $r<m$.
The argument uses only that the replica data are $u(\sigma^\ell)$ for a vector field $u:\alpha\to\mathbb R^N$ with Gibbs mean zero, so the statements are made for an abstract such $u$; the centered scaled data above is the instance $u_i=N^{-1/2}(\xi_i-\langle\xi_i\rangle)$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:centered-mean}).
For the SK model $\xi_i(\boldsymbol\sigma)=\sigma_i$, $\dot\xi_i=\dot\sigma_i$.
\emph{Uses:} A.  \emph{Used by:} D.  \emph{Status:} fully proved.

\begin{lemma}[The centered observables have Gibbs mean zero]
\label{lem:sg-tal:centered-mean}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_centered_eq_zero,
        SpinGlasses.sum_weight_mul_eq_zero}
  \leanok
\talsource{$\langle\dot\sigma_i\rangle=0$, used in the proof of Theorem~1.7.11}
Let $f$ be an energy on a nonempty finite set $\alpha$ with Gibbs weights $w(a)=e^{f(a)}/\sum_be^{f(b)}$ and let $u:\alpha\to\mathbb R^N$.  (a) If $\int u_j\,d\mu=0$ then $\sum_aw(a)u_j(a)=0$.
(b) For observables $\xi_j$ and $u_j=N^{-1/2}\bigl(\xi_j-\langle\xi_j\rangle\bigr)$, where $\langle\xi_j\rangle=\int\xi_j\,d\mu$, one has $\int u_j\,d\mu=0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
(a) A Gibbs integral over a finite set is the weighted sum.  (b) $\mu$ is a probability measure, so $\int u_j\,d\mu=N^{-1/2}(\langle\xi_j\rangle-\langle\xi_j\rangle)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A replica entering only linearly integrates to zero]
\label{lem:sg-tal:linear-vanishing}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.insertNth_eq_update,
        SpinGlasses.sum_prod_weight_mul_linear_eq_zero}
  \leanok
\talsource{``averaging $T_{\ell_1\ell_1'}\cdots T_{\ell_r\ell_r'}$ in $\boldsymbol\sigma^\ell$ would already be $0$''}
Let $w$ be a weight function on a finite set $\alpha$, $z_j:\alpha\to\mathbb R$ with $\sum_aw(a)z_j(a)=0$ for all $j\le N$, and let $H,c_j$ be functions of $\sigma\in\alpha^{n+1}$ that do not
depend on the coordinate $\ell$.  Then
\[
\sum_{\sigma\in\alpha^{n+1}}\Bigl(\prod_{r}w(\sigma_r)\Bigr)\,H(\sigma)\sum_{j\le N}z_j(\sigma_\ell)\,c_j(\sigma)=0 .
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Split the sum over $\sigma$ into the coordinate $\sigma_\ell$ and the remaining coordinates (\texttt{Fin.insertNthEquiv}).  The weight factorizes, $H$ and $c_j$ do not depend on $\sigma_\ell$, and the sum over $\sigma_\ell$
of $w(\sigma_\ell)z_j(\sigma_\ell)$ is $0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A replica occurring once]
\label{lem:sg-tal:occurs-once}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_of_pairCount_eq_one}
  \leanok
\talsource{implicit in the proof of Theorem~1.7.11 (the case ``$\ell$ occurs exactly once'' in the list)}
If replica $\ell$ has multiplicity exactly $1$ in a list $p$ of pairs, then there are a position $i_0$ and a replica $\ell'\ne\ell$ with $p_{i_0}\in\{(\ell,\ell'),(\ell',\ell)\}$, and
no other position contains $\ell$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The multiplicity is the sum over positions of the number of entries of $p_i$ equal to $\ell$; a total of one means that exactly one position contributes, with exactly one entry.  The other entry
of that pair differs from $\ell$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Parity vanishing]
\label{thm:sg-tal:parity}
  \uses{lem:sg-tal:centered-mean, lem:sg-tal:linear-vanishing, lem:sg-tal:occurs-once}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_pairProd_centered_eq_zero}
  \leanok
\talsource{proof of Theorem~1.7.11: ``when (1.205) holds no number $\ell\le2k$ can occur exactly once in the list''}
Let $\mu=G_f$ be a Gibbs measure on a finite set $\alpha$, $u:\alpha\to\mathbb R^N$ with $\int u_j\,d\mu=0$ for all $j$ (for instance $u_j=N^{-1/2}(\xi_j-\langle\xi_j\rangle)$), $v\in\mathbb R$, $m,r\in\mathbb N$, and $p$ a list of $r$ pairs of replicas in $\{1,\dots,m\}$ in which some replica $\ell$ occurs exactly once.  With
$u_\ell(\boldsymbol\sigma)=u(\sigma^\ell)$,
\[
\int\mathrm{pairProd}\bigl(T(u(\boldsymbol\sigma),v)\bigr)(p)\;d\mu^{\otimes m}(\boldsymbol\sigma)=0 .
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:occurs-once} the replica $\ell$ appears in exactly one factor $T_{\ell\ell'}$, $\ell'\ne\ell$, and there the term $-v^2\delta_{\ell\ell'}$ is absent, so the factor is the sum
$\sum_iz_i(\sigma^\ell)c_i(\boldsymbol\sigma)$ with $z_i=u_i$ and $c_i=u_i(\sigma^{\ell'})$.  All other factors do not depend on $\sigma^\ell$.
Writing the integral over $\mu^{\otimes m}$ as a finite sum with the product weights, Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:linear-vanishing} with Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:centered-mean} gives zero.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The Gibbs average of the path functional vanishes at the start of the path]
\label{thm:sg-tal:projsum-vanish}
  \uses{thm:sg-tal:parity, lem:sg-tal:projpath-zero, lem:sg-tal:paircount}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_projSum_centered_zero_eq_zero}
  \leanok
\talsource{proof of Theorem~1.7.11: ``$\varphi^{(r)}(0)=0$ unless $r\ge2k$'', Eqs.~(1.204)--(1.205)}
Let $V$ be exponentially tame, $\mu=G_f$ a Gibbs measure on a finite set $\alpha$, $u:\alpha\to\mathbb R^N$ with Gibbs mean zero, $m\ge1$, $v\in\mathbb R$ with $\int V(vt)\,d\gamma_1(t)=0$ (for $v=\sqrt{1-q}$ this is
$\mathbb E_\xi V(\xi\sqrt{1-q})=0$, Eq.~(1.199)), and $r<m$.  Then
\[
\int\mathrm{projSum}_r\bigl(u(\boldsymbol\sigma),v;\,s=0\bigr)\;d\mu^{\otimes m}(\boldsymbol\sigma)=0 .
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:projpath-zero}, $\mathrm{projSum}_r(0)=\sum_p\mathrm{pairProd}(p)\,I_p$ with $I_p=\int F^{(\mathrm{pairCount}(p))}(v\xi)\,d\gamma_m(\xi)$ independent of $u$ (hence of $\boldsymbol\sigma$).
Fix a list $p$ of $r<m$ pairs.  By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:paircount}(c) some replica $\ell$ has multiplicity $0$ or $1$.  If it is $0$, then $I_p=0$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:projpath-zero}.  If it is $1$, the Gibbs average of
$\mathrm{pairProd}(p)$ vanishes by Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:parity}.  In both cases the Gibbs average of the $p$-th term is zero; sum over $p$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Taylor's formula in the angle: fencing a trigonometric chain}
\label{sec:sg-tal:fluct-taylor}

\emph{Content.}  The elementary last step of the proof of Theorem~1.7.11, in a form adapted to the angle parametrization: if a chain
of functions satisfies $Q_r'=\sin s\cos s\,Q_{r+1}$, the first $n$ members vanish at $0$ and $Q_n$ is bounded, then $Q_0(\pi/2)$ is small.
This is Taylor's formula in the variable $t=\sin^2 s$, but it is proved directly in $s$, so that neither the change of variable nor one-sided
derivatives at the endpoint are needed.
\emph{Uses:} nothing.  \emph{Used by:} D.  \emph{Status:} fully proved.

\begin{lemma}[Fencing a trigonometric chain]
\label{lem:sg-tal:taylor-vanishing}
  \lean{Real.abs_chain_le_of_hasDerivAt,
        Real.abs_chain_zero_le_of_hasDerivAt}
  \leanok
\talsource{``it suffices to prove that $\varphi^{(r)}(0)=0$ for $r<2k$ and that $|\varphi^{(2k)}(t)|\le KN^{-k}$'' (Taylor's formula for $\varphi$ on $[0,1]$)}
Let $Q_r:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R$ ($r\in\mathbb N$) be differentiable with $Q_r'(s)=\sin s\cos s\,Q_{r+1}(s)$ for all $s$, let $n\in\mathbb N$ and $M\in\mathbb R$ with $Q_r(0)=0$ for all $r<n$ and
$|Q_n(s)|\le M$ for $s\in[0,\pi/2]$.  Then for $0\le j\le n$ and $s\in[0,\pi/2]$,
\[
|Q_{n-j}(s)|\le\frac{M}{j!}\Bigl(\frac{\sin^2s}{2}\Bigr)^{j},\qquad\text{in particular}\qquad |Q_0(\pi/2)|\le\frac{M}{2^n\,n!}.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Induction on $j$; $j=0$ is the hypothesis.  For the step put $B(s)=\frac{M}{(j+1)!}(\sin^2 s/2)^{j+1}$, so that $B(0)=0$ and $B'(s)=\sin s\cos s\cdot\frac{M}{j!}(\sin^2s/2)^j$.  The function $f=Q_{n-j-1}$ satisfies $f(0)=0$
and, on $[0,\pi/2)$ where $\sin s\cos s\ge0$, $|f'(s)|=\sin s\cos s\,|Q_{n-j}(s)|\le B'(s)$ by the induction hypothesis.  Mathlib's fencing theorem for functions with a derivative bounded by the
derivative of a boundary function (\texttt{image\_norm\_le\_of\_norm\_deriv\_right\_le\_deriv\_boundary}) gives $|f(s)|\le B(s)$ on $[0,\pi/2]$.
\emph{Why not Taylor's theorem.}  With $t=\sin^2s$ one has $\frac d{dt}=\frac1{2\sin s\cos s}\frac d{ds}$, so $\frac{d}{dt}Q_r=\frac12Q_{r+1}$ on $(0,1)$ and the lemma says $|Q_0(1)|\le M/(2^nn!)$, which
is Taylor's formula for a function with $Q_0^{(r)}(0)=0$ for $r<n$ and $|Q_0^{(n)}|\le M2^{-n}$.  Taylor's theorem would need $Q_0$ to be $C^n$ on a neighbourhood of $t=0$, i.e.\ one-sided derivatives at the endpoint of the interpolation, which the angle form avoids.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The Taylor step for a Gibbs measure}
\label{sec:sg-tal:fluct-gibbs-taylor}

\emph{Content.}  We apply Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:taylor-vanishing} to the Gibbs average $Q_r(s)=\int\mathrm{projSum}_r(s)\,d\mu^{\otimes m}$ of the path functionals of Part~A.
\emph{Uses:} A, B, C.  \emph{Used by:} F.  \emph{Status:} fully proved.  The statements are model independent: $\mu=G_f$ is an arbitrary Gibbs measure on a finite set $\alpha$ and $u:\alpha\to\mathbb R^N$ an arbitrary vector field with Gibbs mean zero and $u\cdot u\le4$; for observables $|\xi_i|\le1$ the field $u_i=N^{-1/2}(\xi_i-\langle\xi_i\rangle)$ qualifies (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:gibbs-chain}(c)).

\begin{definition}[The Gibbs-averaged path functional]
\label{def:sg-tal:gibbs-projsum}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.gibbsProjSum}
  \leanok
\talsource{notation $u_\ell=N^{-1/2}\dot{\boldsymbol\sigma}^\ell$ in the proof of Theorem~1.7.11}
For $\boldsymbol\sigma=(\sigma^1,\dots,\sigma^m)\in\alpha^m$ put $u_\ell(\boldsymbol\sigma)=u(\sigma^\ell)$ and
$Q_r(s)=\int\mathrm{projSum}_r\bigl(u(\boldsymbol\sigma),v;s\bigr)\,d\mu^{\otimes m}(\boldsymbol\sigma)$ (\texttt{gibbsProjSum}).  In the application $u_i(a)=N^{-1/2}(\xi_i(a)-\langle\xi_i\rangle)$, the centered scaled data of Part~B.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[The chain of Gibbs-averaged path functionals]
\label{lem:sg-tal:gibbs-chain}
  \uses{thm:sg-tal:projsum-deriv, thm:sg-tal:projsum-vanish}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_gibbsProjSum,
        SpinGlasses.gibbsProjSum_zero_eq_zero,
        SpinGlasses.dotProduct_centered_le,
        SpinGlasses.abs_gibbsProjSum_le_of_bound}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.203) averaged over $\langle\cdot\rangle$, and the bound $|\varphi^{(r)}(t)|\le KN^{-r/2}$}
Let $V$ be exponentially tame.  (a) $Q_r'(s)=\sin s\cos s\,Q_{r+1}(s)$ for all $r$ and $s$.  (b) If $m\ge1$, $u$ has Gibbs mean zero, $\int V(vt)\,d\gamma_1(t)=0$ and $r<m$, then $Q_r(0)=0$.
(c) If $|\xi_i|\le1$ and $u_i=N^{-1/2}(\xi_i-\langle\xi_i\rangle)$ then $u(a)\cdot u(a)\le4$ for all $a$; and if $u\cdot u\le4$, $v^2\le4$ and $C$ is a constant for which the bound of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:projpath-bound} holds with $B=4$,
\[
|Q_r(s)|\le C\sum_{p}\int\bigl|\mathrm{pairProd}\bigl(T(u(\boldsymbol\sigma),v)\bigr)(p)\bigr|\,d\mu^{\otimes m}(\boldsymbol\sigma)\qquad\text{for all }s,
\]
the sum over lists of $r$ pairs.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The Gibbs average over $m$ replicas is a finite sum with weights $\prod_\ell e^{f(\sigma^\ell)}/Z$ (\texttt{integral\_pi\_count\_tilted}) that do not depend on $s$.  (a) is Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:projsum-deriv} for each term; (b) is Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:projsum-vanish}.
(c): $|\xi_i-\langle\xi_i\rangle|\le2$ gives $u\cdot u\le N^{-1}\cdot N\cdot4$ (and $0$ for $N=0$); the bound follows from Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:projpath-bound} applied to each $\boldsymbol\sigma$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The endpoint $s=\pi/2$ is the $m$-th power of the Gibbs average]
\label{lem:sg-tal:gibbs-endpoint}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.projSum_zero_index,
        SpinGlasses.integral_integral_swap_pi_count_tilted,
        SpinGlasses.gibbsProjSum_zero_index_pi_div_two}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.200): $\varphi(1)=\mathbb E\langle V(\dot S)\rangle^{2k}$}
Let $V$ be exponentially tame, $m\ge1$.  Then $Q_0(\pi/2)=\mathbb E_y\bigl(\int V(u(a)\cdot y)\,d\mu(a)\bigr)^m$, where $y\sim\gamma_N$; for $u=N^{-1/2}(\xi-\langle\xi\rangle)$ this is $\mathbb E_y\bigl(\int V(N^{-1/2}\sum_iy_i(\xi_i(a)-\langle\xi_i\rangle))\,d\mu(a)\bigr)^m$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
$\mathrm{projSum}_0$ has a single term, the empty list, so $\mathrm{projSum}_0(s)=\mathbb E\prod_\ell V(Z_{s,\ell})$.  At $s=\pi/2$, $Z_{s,\ell}=u_\ell\cdot y$ does not involve $\xi$.  Since the Gibbs average is a finite sum and each term is integrable
(Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:projpath-mgf}), it can be exchanged with the integral over $y$.  For fixed $y$ the replicas are independent, so the integral of the product $\prod_\ell V(u(\sigma^\ell)\cdot y)$ over $\mu^{\otimes m}$ is the product of the $m$ equal integrals
(\texttt{integral\_fintype\_prod\_eq\_prod}).
\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[The Taylor step of Theorem~1.7.11]
\label{thm:sg-tal:gibbs-taylor}
  \uses{lem:sg-tal:taylor-vanishing, lem:sg-tal:gibbs-chain, lem:sg-tal:gibbs-endpoint}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_gibbs_fluctuation_abs_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{proof of Theorem~1.7.11: $\varphi(1)$ is bounded by Taylor's formula from $\varphi^{(r)}(0)=0$ ($r<2k$) and $|\varphi^{(2k)}|\le KN^{-k}$}
Let $m\ge1$ and let $(C_j)_{j\le2m}$, $c\ge0$ be given.  There is $C\ge0$, depending only on $m,(C_j),c$ (not on $N$, on $\alpha$, on the energy $f$ or on the observables), such that for every exponentially tame $V$ with $|V^{(j)}(x)|\le C_je^{c|x|}$ ($j\le2m$), every $v^2\le4$ with $\int V(vt)\,d\gamma_1(t)=0$, every Gibbs measure $\mu=G_f$ on a finite set $\alpha$ and every $u:\alpha\to\mathbb R^N$ with $u\cdot u\le4$ and Gibbs mean zero,
\[
\Bigl|\mathbb E_y\Bigl(\int V\bigl(u(a)\cdot y\bigr)d\mu(a)\Bigr)^m\Bigr|\le C\sum_{p}\int\bigl|\mathrm{pairProd}(T(u(\boldsymbol\sigma),v))(p)\bigr|\,d\mu^{\otimes m}(\boldsymbol\sigma),
\]
the sum over all $m^{2m}$ lists $p$ of $m$ pairs of replicas.  For observables $|\xi_i|\le1$ one takes $u=N^{-1/2}(\xi-\langle\xi\rangle)$, by Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:centered-mean} and Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:gibbs-chain}(c).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:taylor-vanishing} with $n=m$ to the chain $Q_r$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:gibbs-chain}: (a) is the chain relation, (b) gives $Q_r(0)=0$ for $r<m$, and (c) gives $|Q_m|\le M$ with
$M=C_0\sum_p\int|\mathrm{pairProd}|$, uniformly in $s$.  Hence $|Q_0(\pi/2)|\le M/(2^mm!)$, and $Q_0(\pi/2)$ is the left side by Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:gibbs-endpoint}; take $C=C_0/(2^mm!)$.  Here $C_0$ is the constant of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:projpath-bound} with $r=m$ and $B=4$, which is why the dependence on $V$ is only through the bounds $C_j$, $c$ (for a single tame $V$ such bounds exist: \texttt{ExpTame.exists\_bound\_upTo}).
\emph{Comparison with the book.}  The book takes $m=2k$, obtains $|\varphi^{(2k)}|\le KN^{-k}$ by H\"older's inequality and gets the same factor $1/(2k)!$ from Taylor's formula.
Here the sum over lists is kept and estimated in Part~E.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Moments of the Gram entries (Eq.~(1.201))}
\label{sec:sg-tal:fluct-gram}

\emph{Content.}  Each entry $T_{\ell\ell'}=N^{-1}\sum_i\dot\xi_i(\sigma^\ell)\dot\xi_i(\sigma^{\ell'})-(1-q)\delta_{\ell\ell'}$ is the \emph{doubly centered} version of the centered overlap kernel $K=R-q$: $K(\sigma^\ell,\sigma^{\ell'})$ minus its two partial averages plus its total average.
The proof has two independent parts.  Part~1 is a general fact about any kernel on any Gibbs measure on a finite set: double centering costs at most a factor $4^{2k}$ in the $2k$-th moment, by Jensen's inequality (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:double-centering}).  Part~2 is the algebraic identity that identifies $T_{\ell\ell'}$ with the doubly centered $R-q$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:gram-moments}); the partial and total averages are never given names of their own.
\emph{Uses:} nothing from the other parts of this section (only the definition of $T$).  \emph{Used by:} F.  \emph{Status:} fully proved, for any Gibbs measure on a finite set carrying observables with $\xi_i^2=1$.

\begin{definition}[The overlap moment]
\label{def:sg-tal:gibbs-overlap}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.overlapMoment}
  \leanok
\talsource{notation of the proof of (1.201)}
For $\xi_i^2=1$ put $R(a,b)=N^{-1}\sum_i\xi_i(a)\xi_i(b)$ and $J_k=\int(R(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)-q)^{2k}\,d\mu^{\otimes2}$ (\texttt{overlapMoment}).
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Jensen for even powers, pair marginals and the overlap moment]
\label{lem:sg-tal:gibbs-jensen}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.pow_integral_count_tilted_le,
        SpinGlasses.integral_pi_pair_count_tilted,
        SpinGlasses.overlapMoment_eq,
        SpinGlasses.integral_overlap_right}
  \leanok
\talsource{``using Jensen's inequality (1.23) to average in $\boldsymbol\sigma^2$ outside the power $2r$''}
For every probability measure $\mu=G_f$ on a finite set, $(\int h\,d\mu)^{2k}\le\int h^{2k}d\mu$.  For two distinct replicas $\ell\ne\ell'$ of $\mu^{\otimes m}$ and any $H$, $\int H(\sigma^\ell,\sigma^{\ell'})\,d\mu^{\otimes m}=\iint H(a,b)\,d\mu(a)\,d\mu(b)$.  For $K=R-q$ one has $J_k=\iint K(a,b)^{2k}\,d\mu\,d\mu$ and $\int R(a,b)\,d\mu(b)=N^{-1}\sum_i\xi_i(a)\langle\xi_i\rangle$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Jensen's inequality for the convex function $x\mapsto x^{2k}$ and the probability measure $\mu$.  The two-replica marginal is Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:replica-marginal}; $\mu^{\otimes2}$ is the product measure, which gives the iterated integral.  The last formula is linearity of the integral.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Double centering costs a factor $4^{2k}$]
\label{lem:sg-tal:double-centering}
  \uses{lem:sg-tal:gibbs-jensen}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integral_pi_doubleCentered_pow_le,
        add_pow_four_two_mul_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{the convexity step in the proof of (1.201)}
Let $\mu=G_f$ be a Gibbs measure on a finite set $\alpha$, $K:\alpha^2\to\mathbb R$ any kernel, $k\ge1$, and $\ell,\ell'$ replicas of $\mu^{\otimes m}$.  Put
\[
T(\boldsymbol\sigma)=x_1-\int K(\sigma^\ell,b)\,d\mu(b)-\int K(\sigma^{\ell'},b)\,d\mu(b)+\iint K\,d\mu\,d\mu,\qquad x_1=\begin{cases}K(\sigma^\ell,\sigma^{\ell'})&\ell\ne\ell',\\0&\ell=\ell'.\end{cases}
\]
Then $\int T^{2k}\,d\mu^{\otimes m}\le4^{2k}\iint K(a,b)^{2k}\,d\mu(a)\,d\mu(b)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
By convexity $(x_1-x_2-x_3+x_4)^{2k}\le4^{2k-1}(x_1^{2k}+x_2^{2k}+x_3^{2k}+x_4^{2k})$.  Put $J=\iint K^{2k}$.  By Jensen's inequality, $x_2^{2k}=(\int K(\sigma^\ell,b)\,d\mu(b))^{2k}\le\int K(\sigma^\ell,b)^{2k}d\mu(b)$, whose integral over $\mu^{\otimes m}$ is $J$ because the marginal of one replica is $\mu$; the same for $x_3$.  The constant $x_4$ satisfies $x_4^{2k}\le J$ by Jensen applied twice.  Finally $\int x_1^{2k}=J$ for $\ell\ne\ell'$ by the pair marginal formula, and $x_1=0$ for $\ell=\ell'$.  Sum: $4^{2k-1}\cdot4J=4^{2k}J$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Moments of the Gram entries, Eq.~(1.201)]
\label{thm:sg-tal:gram-moments}
  \uses{lem:sg-tal:double-centering, lem:sg-tal:gibbs-jensen, def:sg-tal:gibbs-overlap}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.overlap_self,
        SpinGlasses.gram_centered_eq,
        SpinGlasses.integral_integral_overlap,
        integral_integral_finset_sum_mul,
        SpinGlasses.integral_projGram_pow_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{Eq.~(1.201): $\mathbb E\langle T_{\ell,\ell'}^{2r}\rangle\le K/N^r$, and the identity displayed after it}
Let $\mu=G_f$ be a Gibbs measure on a finite set with observables satisfying $\xi_i^2=1$, $u=N^{-1/2}(\xi-\langle\xi\rangle)$, $N\ge1$, $q\in\mathbb R$, $v^2=1-q$, $k\ge1$, and $m\ge1$ replicas.  For all replicas $\ell,\ell'$, with $T_{\ell\ell'}=u(\sigma^\ell)\cdot u(\sigma^{\ell'})-v^2\delta_{\ell\ell'}$ and $K=R-q$,
\[
T_{\ell\ell'}=x_1-\int K(\sigma^\ell,b)\,d\mu(b)-\int K(\sigma^{\ell'},b)\,d\mu(b)+\iint K\,d\mu\,d\mu,\qquad
\int T_{\ell\ell'}^{2k}\,d\mu^{\otimes m}\le4^{2k}J_k,
\]
where $x_1=K(\sigma^\ell,\sigma^{\ell'})$ for $\ell\ne\ell'$ and $x_1=0$ for $\ell=\ell'$.  In the book's notation $\int K(a,b)\,d\mu(b)=A(a)-q$ with $A(a)=N^{-1}\sum_i\xi_i(a)\langle\xi_i\rangle$, and $\iint K=B-q$ with $B=N^{-1}\sum_i\langle\xi_i\rangle^2$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Expanding $(\xi_i(\sigma^\ell)-\langle\xi_i\rangle)(\xi_i(\sigma^{\ell'})-\langle\xi_i\rangle)$ gives $N^{-1}\sum_i\dot\xi_i(\sigma^\ell)\dot\xi_i(\sigma^{\ell'})=R(\sigma^\ell,\sigma^{\ell'})-A(\sigma^\ell)-A(\sigma^{\ell'})+B$.  For $\ell=\ell'$ use $R(a,a)=1$ (this is where $\xi_i^2=1$ enters, \texttt{overlap\_self}) and $v^2=1-q$,
which gives the book's identity $T_{\ell\ell}=-2(A-q)+(B-q)$; for $\ell\ne\ell'$ the constants $q$ cancel.  This is the identity.  The moment bound is Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:double-centering} for the kernel $K=R-q$, together with $\iint K^{2k}=J_k$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:gibbs-jensen}).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Theorem~1.7.11}
\label{sec:sg-tal:fluct-main}

\emph{Content.}  First the model-independent core (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:gibbs-fluct-core}): for a fixed Gibbs measure the fluctuation is bounded by a multiple of the overlap moment $J_k$.  Then the SK model, which enters only through the bound $\mathbb E_gJ_k(g)\le K'/N^k$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-moments-all}.  \emph{Uses:} A--E and Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-moments-all}.
\emph{Status:} fully proved.

\begin{lemma}[Arithmetic--geometric mean for products of Gram entries]
\label{lem:sg-tal:amgm-pairprod}
  \lean{prod_abs_le_sum_pow,
        SpinGlasses.sum_integral_abs_pairProd_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{``each factor $T_{\ell,\ell'}$ contributes as $N^{-1/2}$''}
For $a_1,\dots,a_n\in\mathbb R$ ($n\ge1$), $\prod_j|a_j|\le\sum_j|a_j|^n$.  Consequently, under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:gram-moments} with $m=2k$,
\[
\sum_{p}\int\bigl|\mathrm{pairProd}(T)(p)\bigr|\,d\mu^{\otimes2k}\le(2k)^{4k}\cdot2k\cdot4^{2k}\,J_k .
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Let $j_0$ maximize $|a_j|$; then $\prod_j|a_j|\le|a_{j_0}|^n\le\sum_j|a_j|^n$ (this is cruder than the arithmetic--geometric mean inequality $\prod|a_j|\le\frac1n\sum|a_j|^n$ and loses the constant $n$ only).  A list of $2k$ pairs gives a product of $2k$ entries; integrate and use $|T|^{2k}=T^{2k}$ with Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:gram-moments}
on each of the $2k$ factors.  There are $((2k)^2)^{2k}$ lists.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The model-independent core of Theorem~1.7.11]
\label{thm:sg-tal:gibbs-fluct-core}
  \uses{thm:sg-tal:gibbs-taylor, thm:sg-tal:gram-moments, lem:sg-tal:amgm-pairprod}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sub_integral_count_tilted_eq,
        SpinGlasses.exists_gibbs_fluctuation_pow_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{proof of Theorem~1.7.11, Eqs.~(1.200)--(1.205), before averaging over the disorder}
Let $k\ge1$ and let $(C_j)_{j\le4k}$, $c\ge0$ be given.  There is $C\ge0$, depending only on $k,(C_j),c$, such that the following holds.  Let $V$ be exponentially tame with $|V^{(j)}(x)|\le C_je^{c|x|}$ ($j\le4k$), let $q\ge0$ and $v^2=1-q$ with $\int V(vt)\,d\gamma_1(t)=0$, let $\mu=G_f$ be a Gibbs measure on a finite set $\alpha$ with observables $\xi_i$ satisfying $\xi_i^2=1$, $N\ge1$, and $u=N^{-1/2}(\xi-\langle\xi\rangle)$.  Then
\[
\mathbb E_y\Bigl(\int V\bigl(u(a)\cdot y\bigr)\,d\mu(a)\Bigr)^{2k}\le C\,J_k .
\]
Moreover $\int g\,d\mu-c_0=\int(g-c_0)\,d\mu$ for a probability measure $\mu$ (used to pass from $U$ to $V=U-c_0$).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:gibbs-taylor} with $m=2k$ applies: $v^2=1-q\le1\le4$, $|\xi_i|\le1$ because $\xi_i^2=1$, and $u$ has Gibbs mean zero and $u\cdot u\le4$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:centered-mean}, Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:gibbs-chain}(c)).  It bounds the left side, which is $\le$ its absolute value, by $C_1\sum_p\int|\mathrm{pairProd}|\,d\mu^{\otimes2k}$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:amgm-pairprod} bounds the sum by $(2k)^{4k}\,2k\,4^{2k}J_k$.  Take $C=C_1(2k)^{4k}\,2k\,4^{2k}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The SK Gibbs energy and the spin observables]
\label{def:sg-tal:sk-gibbs-energy}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.skGibbsEnergy,
        SpinGlasses.measurable_skGibbsEnergy,
        SpinGlasses.abs_units_cast_le_one,
        SpinGlasses.units_cast_sq}
  \leanok
\talsource{the Hamiltonian (1.1) with the disorder $g$ held fixed}
For $g\in\mathbb R^{\mathrm{FinPairs}\,N}$ let $f_g(a)=\frac{\beta}{\sqrt N}\sum_{i<j}g_{ij}a_ia_j+h\sum_ia_i$ be the SK energy on $\{-1,1\}^N$ and $\xi_i(a)=a_i$.  Then $|\xi_i|\le1$ and $\xi_i^2=1$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[General Gaussian fluctuation principle (Theorem~1.7.11)]
\label{thm:sg-tal:general-fluct}
  \uses{def:sg-tal:exptame, thm:sg-tal:gibbs-taylor, thm:sg-tal:gram-moments, lem:sg-tal:amgm-pairprod, thm:sg-tal:overlap-moments-all, def:sg-tal:sk-gibbs-energy}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.ExpTame.sub_const,
        SpinGlasses.ExpTame.integrable_comp_mul_gaussianReal,
        SpinGlasses.ExpTame.integral_sub_const_mul_gaussianReal,
        SpinGlasses.sk_general_gaussian_fluctuation}
  \leanok
\talsource{Theorem~1.7.11, Eq.~(1.197)}
Let $\beta,h\in\mathbb R$ with $4\beta^2<1$, let $q=\mathbb E\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt q\,z+h)$, and let $U:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R$ be exponentially tame.  Let $y$ be a standard Gaussian vector independent of the disorder $g$, and
$\dot\sigma_i=\sigma_i-\langle\sigma_i\rangle$ in the $N$-spin SK model at $(\beta,h)$.  For every $k\ge1$ there is $K>0$, depending on $k,U,\beta,h,q$ only, such that for all $N\ge1$
\[
\mathbb E\Bigl(\Bigl\langle U\Bigl(\frac1{\sqrt N}\sum_{i\le N}y_i\dot\sigma_i\Bigr)\Bigr\rangle-\mathbb E_\xi U(\xi\sqrt{1-q})\Bigr)^{2k}\le\frac K{N^k},
\]
where $\mathbb E$ is the expectation over the disorder and over $y$, and $\mathbb E_\xi$ over a standard Gaussian $\xi$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Put $c=\mathbb E_\xi U(\xi\sqrt{1-q})$, $V=U-c$ and $v=\sqrt{1-q}$; $q\in[0,1]$ because it is the integral of $\tanh^2\in[0,1]$ (\texttt{fixedPoint\_mem\_Icc}), so $v^2=1-q\le4$.  The function $V$ is exponentially tame and $\int V(vt)\,d\gamma_1=0$ (Eq.~(1.199)), because $U(v\,\cdot\,)$ is integrable against a Gaussian.
Fix the disorder $g$ and let $\mu_g$ be the Gibbs measure of $f_g$.  Since $\mu_g$ is a probability measure, $\langle U(\dot S)\rangle-c=\int V(\dot S)\,d\mu_g$, so by Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:gibbs-fluct-core} (this is the book's steps (1.200)--(1.205), Taylor's formula and (1.201)), with $C$ the constant for the explicit bounds of $V$ given by \texttt{ExpTame.exists\_bound\_upTo},
\[
0\le\mathbb E_y\bigl(\langle U(\dot S)\rangle-c\bigr)^{2k}\le C\,J_k(g).
\]
The left side is nonnegative as an even power.  Integrating over $g$ and using
$\mathbb E_gJ_k(g)\le K'/N^k$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:overlap-moments-all}; $J_k(g)$ is integrable in $g$ as a Gibbs average of a bounded function) gives the claim with $K=CK'+1$.
\emph{Deviations from the book.}  (i) The interpolation is carried out in the angle $s$, with $t=\sin^2s$ never introduced: the chain relation $Q_r'=\sin s\cos s\,Q_{r+1}$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:projsum-deriv}) and the fencing lemma replace the formula $\varphi^{(r)}=2^{-r}\sum\mathbb E\langle T\cdots T\,F^{(\mathbf s)}\rangle$ and Taylor's theorem.  (ii) The Gaussian factor is bounded uniformly (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:projpath-bound}) instead of by H\"older's inequality, which is why $U$ is assumed exponentially tame rather than satisfying (1.196).
(iii) The bound is deterministic in the Gibbs measure and model independent (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:gibbs-fluct-core}): for every disorder $g$, $\mathbb E_y(\cdot)^{2k}\le C\,J_k(g)$, and only the last step integrates over $g$.  (iv) The theorem is stated with the centered vector $(N^{-1/2}(\sigma_i-\langle\sigma_i\rangle))_i$ dotted with $y$.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKGaussianFluctuation.lean}, \texttt{GibbsFluctuationMoment.lean} (the core), \texttt{GibbsGramMoments.lean} and \texttt{GibbsProjectionBound.lean}; the three \texttt{ExpTame} lemmas used here (shift by a constant, integrability, Gaussian mean zero) live in \texttt{GaussianProjectionPath.lean} with the definition of \texttt{ExpTame}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Fixed-point shift under parameter perturbation (Lemma~1.7.5)}
\label{sec:sg-tal:fixed-point-shift}

\begin{lemma}[Fixed-point shift under parameter perturbation (Lemma~1.7.5)]
\label{lem:sg-tal:fixed-point-shift}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_fixedPointMap_param,
        SpinGlasses.abs_deriv_fixedPointMap_param_le,
        SpinGlasses.abs_fixedPointMap_sub_le,
        SpinGlasses.sk_fixed_point_shift_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{Lemma~1.7.5, Eq.~(1.187)}
Let $\beta\ge 0$ with $4\beta^2 < 1$, $h\in\mathbb R$, and $N\ge 1$. Let $q$ be the fixed point at $\beta$,
$q = \mathbb E\tanh^2(\beta\sqrt q\,z + h)$, and $q_-$ the fixed point at $\beta_- = \beta\sqrt{1 - 1/N}$.
Then
\[
|q - q_-| \le \frac{1}{N}.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Follows from the mean value theorem applied to the parameter map $w \mapsto F(w) = \int \tanh^2(w z + h)\,d\gamma(z)$,
where the derivative is bounded by $2w$, giving $|F(w_1) - F(w_2)| \le 2|w_1^2 - w_2^2|$.
Since $q = F(\beta\sqrt q)$ and $q_- = F(\beta_-\sqrt{q_-})$,
\[
|q - q_-| \le 2|\beta^2 q - \beta_-^2 q_-| \le 2(\beta^2 - \beta_-^2)q + 2\beta_-^2|q - q_-| \le \frac{2\beta^2}{N} + 2\beta^2|q - q_-|.
\]
Since $4\beta^2 < 1$, $1 - 2\beta^2 > 1/2 \ge 2\beta^2$, which yields $|q - q_-| \le \frac{2\beta^2}{1 - 2\beta^2}\frac{1}{N} \le \frac{1}{N}$.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKFixedPoint.lean}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Corollary~1.7.13: the exponential observables}
\label{sec:sg-tal:fluct-exp}

\emph{Content.}  Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:general-fluct} for the two functions that the cavity method actually needs,
$U(x)=e^{\varepsilon\beta x}$ and $U(x)=x\,e^{\varepsilon\beta x}$, in the form in which they are used in Section~1.7.
\emph{Uses:} Parts~A--F.  \emph{Status:} fully proved, with a constant $K$ that is uniform in $|\beta|\le\beta_0<1/2$ as in the book (Remark~\ref{rem:sg-tal:dev-1713}).

\paragraph{Where the statement comes from.}
The cavity method (Lemma~1.7.4) needs the \emph{whole} Gibbs average of $\exp(\varepsilon\beta N^{-1/2}\sum_iy_i\sigma_i)$, not of its centered version.
Writing $\sigma_i=\dot\sigma_i+\langle\sigma_i\rangle$ factors this exponential into the part $\exp(\varepsilon\beta\dot S)$, $\dot S=N^{-1/2}\sum_iy_i\dot\sigma_i$,
to which Theorem~1.7.11 applies, and the \emph{deterministic-given-the-Gibbs-measure} factor
$B=\exp(\varepsilon\beta N^{-1/2}\sum_iy_i\langle\sigma_i\rangle)$.  The theorem says that $\langle e^{\varepsilon\beta\dot S}\rangle$ is within $O(N^{-1/2})$ in $L^{2k}$ of the Gaussian value $e^{\beta^2(1-q)/2}$,
and the factor $B$ is harmless because its moments are bounded (it is the exponential of a Gaussian of variance $\le\beta^2$).  This is the whole mathematical content of the corollary; the remaining lemmas of this subsection
are the bookkeeping that makes it a formal statement.

\begin{lemma}[The two observables are exponentially tame]
\label{lem:sg-tal:exptame-instances}
  \uses{def:sg-tal:exptame}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.hasDerivAt_exp_mul,
        SpinGlasses.iterate_deriv_exp,
        SpinGlasses.expTame_exp,
        SpinGlasses.iterate_deriv_id_mul_exp,
        SpinGlasses.expTame_id_mul_exp,
        SpinGlasses.abs_iterate_deriv_exp_le,
        SpinGlasses.abs_iterate_deriv_id_mul_exp_le_abs,
        SpinGlasses.abs_iterate_deriv_id_mul_exp_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{``with $U(x)=\exp\varepsilon\beta x$'' and ``using now $U(x)=x\exp\varepsilon\beta x$'' in the proof of Corollary~1.7.13}
For every $c\in\mathbb R$ the functions $x\mapsto e^{cx}$ and $x\mapsto xe^{cx}$ are exponentially tame (Definition~\ref{def:sg-tal:exptame}).  Moreover the bounds are uniform in the parameter: if $|c|\le B$ then for all $j$ and $x$
\[
\Bigl|\tfrac{d^j}{dx^j}e^{cx}\Bigr|\le B^je^{B|x|},\qquad
\Bigl|\tfrac{d^j}{dx^j}\bigl(xe^{cx}\bigr)\Bigr|\le2\bigl(jB^{j-1}+B^j\bigr)e^{(B+1)|x|}.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
By induction on $j$, $\frac{d^j}{dx^j}e^{cx}=c^je^{cx}$ and $\frac{d^j}{dx^j}\bigl(xe^{cx}\bigr)=(jc^{j-1}+c^jx)e^{cx}$.  Since $cx\le|c||x|$ the first is bounded by $|c|^je^{|c||x|}$.
For the second use $|x|\le e^{|x|}$ and $1\le e^{|x|}$, which give $|jc^{j-1}+c^jx|\le 2(j|c|^{j-1}+|c|^j)e^{|x|}$, hence a bound $Ce^{(|c|+1)|x|}$.  The uniform bounds follow because the right sides are increasing in $|c|$.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/GaussianProjectionPath.lean}, next to the definition of \texttt{ExpTame}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Gaussian averages of the two observables]
\label{lem:sg-tal:exp-gauss-averages}
  \lean{integral_exp_mul_gaussianReal_zero_one,
        integral_id_mul_exp_mul_gaussianReal_zero_one,
        SpinGlasses.integral_exp_scaled_gaussianReal_zero_one,
        SpinGlasses.integral_id_mul_exp_scaled_gaussianReal_zero_one}
  \leanok
\talsource{``(using Gaussian integration by parts and (A.6)) $\mathrm E_\xi U(\xi\sqrt{1-q})=\varepsilon\beta(1-q)\exp(\beta^2(1-q)/2)$''}
For a standard Gaussian $\xi$ and $c,v\in\mathbb R$:
\[
\mathbb E\,e^{c\,v\xi}=e^{c^2v^2/2},\qquad \mathbb E\,(v\xi)\,e^{c\,v\xi}=c\,v^2\,e^{c^2v^2/2}.
\]
In particular, for $\varepsilon^2=1$ and $v^2=1-q$: $\mathbb E\,e^{\varepsilon\beta v\xi}=e^{\beta^2(1-q)/2}$ and $\mathbb E\,(v\xi)e^{\varepsilon\beta v\xi}=\varepsilon\beta(1-q)e^{\beta^2(1-q)/2}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The first identity is the moment generating function of $\mathcal N(0,1)$ (Appendix (A.6)).  For the second, Gaussian integration by parts $\mathbb E[\xi F(\xi)]=\mathbb E[F'(\xi)]$ (Stein's lemma, \texttt{integral\_id\_mul\_gaussianReal\_eq}) with $F(x)=e^{cvx}$,
$F'(x)=cv\,e^{cvx}$, followed by the first identity.  The integrability hypotheses of Stein's lemma are the domination $|x|e^{cvx}\le e^{(1+|cv|)|x|}$ and the integrability of $e^{w|x|}$ against a Gaussian.
Formalized in \texttt{ForMathlib/Probability/Moments/GaussianPiExp.lean} (the unscaled identities) and \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKGaussianCorollaries.lean} (the rescaling by $v$).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Splitting off the thermal mean]
\label{lem:sg-tal:ising-split}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.dotProduct_ising_split,
        SpinGlasses.exp_dotProduct_ising_split,
        SpinGlasses.id_mul_exp_dotProduct_ising_split,
        SpinGlasses.integral_exp_dotProduct_ising_eq,
        SpinGlasses.integral_id_mul_exp_dotProduct_ising_eq,
        SpinGlasses.pow_exp_dotProduct_ising}
  \leanok
\talsource{the factorization $\exp\frac{\varepsilon\beta}{\sqrt N}\sum y_i\sigma_i=\exp\frac{\varepsilon\beta}{\sqrt N}\sum y_i\dot\sigma_i\cdot\exp\frac{\varepsilon\beta}{\sqrt N}\sum y_i\langle\sigma_i\rangle$, used implicitly in the proof of Corollary~1.7.13}
Let $\mu$ be any measure on $\{-1,1\}^N$, $m\in\mathbb R^N$ (we use $m_i=\langle\sigma_i\rangle$), $y\in\mathbb R^N$, and write $\dot S_a=N^{-1/2}\sum_iy_i(a_i-m_i)$ and $B=\exp(\varepsilon\beta N^{-1/2}\sum_iy_im_i)$.  Then
\[
\int e^{\varepsilon\beta N^{-1/2}\sum_iy_ia_i}\,d\mu(a)=B\int e^{\varepsilon\beta\dot S_a}\,d\mu(a),\qquad
\int\dot S_a\,e^{\varepsilon\beta N^{-1/2}\sum_iy_ia_i}\,d\mu(a)=B\int\dot S_a\,e^{\varepsilon\beta\dot S_a}\,d\mu(a),
\]
and $B^n=\exp\bigl(n\varepsilon\beta N^{-1/2}\sum_iy_im_i\bigr)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Pointwise in $a$, $a_i=(a_i-m_i)+m_i$ so the exponent splits as a sum and the exponential of a sum is the product of exponentials; $B$ does not depend on $a$ and comes out of the integral.
The last identity is $(e^x)^n=e^{nx}$ and linearity of the dot product.
Formalized in \texttt{SpinGlasses/SKGaussianCorollaries.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Moments of the thermal factor $B$]
\label{lem:sg-tal:thermal-factor-moment}
  \lean{integral_exp_sum_ising_moment_le,
        integrable_exp_dotProduct_gaussianReal,
        integrable_exp_const_mul_dotProduct_gaussianReal,
        SpinGlasses.abs_integral_units_cast_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{``(A.6) entails that $\mathrm E B^{4k}\le K^k$'' in the proof of Corollary~1.7.13}
Let $y$ be a standard Gaussian vector in $\mathbb R^N$ and $m\in\mathbb R^N$ with $|m_i|\le1$.  Then $\exp(c\,N^{-1/2}\sum_im_iy_i)$ is integrable for every $c$ and
\[
\mathbb E\exp\Bigl(c\,\frac1{\sqrt N}\sum_{i\le N}m_iy_i\Bigr)\le e^{c^2/2}.
\]
Moreover the thermal means $m_i=\langle\sigma_i\rangle$ of any probability measure on $\{-1,1\}^N$ satisfy $|m_i|\le1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The sum $\sum_ic_iy_i$ with $c_i=cN^{-1/2}m_i$ is centered Gaussian of variance $\sum_ic_i^2=c^2N^{-1}\sum_im_i^2\le c^2$, so its exponential has expectation $e^{\frac12\sum c_i^2}\le e^{c^2/2}$ (the product formula (A.6) for independent coordinates, \texttt{integral\_exp\_sum\_mul\_gaussianReal}).  Finally $|\int a_i\,d\mu|\le\int|a_i|\,d\mu=1$.
With $c=4k\varepsilon\beta$ this gives $\mathbb E B^{4k}\le e^{8k^2\beta^2}$, the book's $K^k$ with an explicit constant.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Powers of a Gibbs average are integrable in the Gaussian variables]
\label{lem:sg-tal:gibbs-pow-integrable}
  \uses{def:sg-tal:exptame, lem:sg-tal:projpath-mgf}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.integrable_prod_derivProd_zero,
        SpinGlasses.integrable_integral_gibbs_pow}
  \leanok
\talsource{no counterpart in the book, which treats the integrability of the quantities in Corollary~1.7.13 as obvious}
Let $V$ be exponentially tame, $f$ an energy on $\{-1,1\}^N$ with Gibbs measure $\mu_f$, $m\in\mathbb R^N$ and $n\ge1$.  Then $y\mapsto\bigl(\int V(N^{-1/2}\sum_iy_i(a_i-m_i))\,d\mu_f(a)\bigr)^n$ is integrable against the standard Gaussian measure on $\mathbb R^N$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The $n$-th power of a Gibbs average is the Gibbs average over $n$ independent replicas of the product $\prod_{\ell\le n}V(u(\sigma^\ell)\cdot y)$, a finite sum (over $\{-1,1\}^{Nn}$) of constants times such products.  Each product is integrable in $y$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:projpath-mgf} (integrability of $F^{(a)}(Z_s)$) evaluated at the angle $s=\pi/2$, where $\cos s=0$ and the path $Z_s$ reduces to the projections $u_\ell\cdot y$.  A finite sum of integrable functions is integrable.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A weighted arithmetic--geometric mean inequality]
\label{lem:sg-tal:amgm-weighted}
  \lean{mul_le_half_mul_add_inv_mul}
  \leanok
\talsource{replaces the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality $\mathrm E(AB)^{2k}\le(\mathrm EA^{4k}\,\mathrm EB^{4k})^{1/2}$ of the proof of Corollary~1.7.13}
For $t>0$ and $a,b\in\mathbb R$: $ab\le\frac12(t\,a^2+t^{-1}b^2)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
$ab=\frac12(ta^2+t^{-1}b^2)-\frac1{2t}(ta-b)^2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A dominated iterated integral]
\label{lem:sg-tal:double-dominated}
  \lean{double_integral_le_of_dominated}
  \leanok
\talsource{no counterpart; the measure-theoretic bookkeeping behind ``integrating over $g$ and over $y$''}
Let $F\ge0$ on $X\times Y$, and suppose $F(x,y)\le\mathrm{Up}(x,y)$ where for each $x$ the function $\mathrm{Up}(x,\cdot)$ is $\nu$-integrable with $\int\mathrm{Up}(x,y)\,d\nu(y)\le\mathrm{Bnd}(x)$, and $\mathrm{Bnd}$ is $\mu$-integrable with $\int\mathrm{Bnd}\,d\mu\le C$.  Then $\int\!\!\int F\,d\nu\,d\mu\le C$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Monotonicity of the inner integral (for $F\ge0$ dominated by an integrable function), then monotonicity of the outer integral (the inner integral is $\ge0$ and dominated by the integrable $\mathrm{Bnd}$).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The master dominating kernel for Corollary~1.7.13]
\label{lem:sg-tal:cor-kernel}
  \uses{thm:sg-tal:general-fluct, thm:sg-tal:gibbs-taylor, lem:sg-tal:amgm-pairprod, lem:sg-tal:amgm-weighted, lem:sg-tal:ising-split, lem:sg-tal:thermal-factor-moment, lem:sg-tal:gibbs-pow-integrable, thm:sg-tal:overlap-moments-all}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.sk_gaussian_fluctuation_upper_bound,
        SpinGlasses.abs_integral_comp_mul_gaussianReal_le,
        SpinGlasses.abs_iterate_deriv_sub_const_le}
  \leanok
\talsource{the proof of Corollary~1.7.13: ``$\mathrm EA^{4k}\le K/N^{2k}$, $\mathrm EB^{4k}\le K^k$, hence $\mathrm E(AB)^{2k}\le K/N^k$''}
Let $\beta_0$ satisfy $4\beta_0^2<1$, $k\ge1$, and let $C_j\ge0$ ($j\le8k$) and $c\ge0$ be given.  There are constants $C_2,M,K'>0$, depending only on $k,\beta_0,(C_j),c$, with the following property.  Let $\beta$ satisfy $\beta^2\le\beta_0^2$, $h\in\mathbb R$, $q$ the fixed point at $\beta$, $\varepsilon^2=1$, and let $U$ be exponentially tame with $|U^{(j)}(x)|\le C_je^{c|x|}$ for $j\le8k$.  Put, for the SK Gibbs measure $\langle\cdot\rangle=\langle\cdot\rangle_g$,
\[
A=\bigl\langle U(\dot S)\bigr\rangle-\mathbb E_\xi U(\xi\sqrt{1-q}),\qquad B=\exp\Bigl(\varepsilon\beta N^{-1/2}\sum_iy_i\langle\sigma_i\rangle\Bigr).
\]
Then for every $N\ge1$ there exist functions $\mathrm{Up}(g,y)$ and $\mathrm{Bnd}(g)$ with
\begin{enumerate}
\item $(AB)^{2k}\le\mathrm{Up}(g,y)$ pointwise;
\item $\mathrm{Up}(g,\cdot)$ is integrable in $y$ and $\mathbb E_y\mathrm{Up}(g,\cdot)\le\mathrm{Bnd}(g)$;
\item $\mathrm{Bnd}$ is integrable in $g$ and $\mathbb E_g\mathrm{Bnd}\le\frac12(C_2K'+M)/N^k$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Take $t=N^k$ in Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:amgm-weighted} applied to $a=A^{2k}$, $b=B^{2k}$:
\[
(AB)^{2k}\le\mathrm{Up}:=\tfrac12\bigl(N^kA^{4k}+N^{-k}B^{4k}\bigr).
\]
\emph{The $A$ term.}  Put $v^2=1-q\in[0,1]$, $c_0=\mathbb E_\xi U(v\xi)$ and $V=U-c_0$, so that $A=\langle V(\dot S)\rangle$ and $\int V(vt)\,d\gamma=0$.  Since $|U|\le C_0e^{c|x|}$, $|c_0|\le2C_0e^{c^2v^2/2}\le K_0:=2C_0e^{c^2/2}$ (split $e^{c|vt|}\le e^{cvt}+e^{-cvt}$ and use the Gaussian MGF), hence $V$ obeys $|V^{(j)}(x)|\le(C_j+K_0)e^{c|x|}$ for $j\le8k$ (subtracting a constant changes only the order-zero derivative).  The uniform form of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:gibbs-taylor} with $m=4k$ therefore gives one constant $C$, independent of $U$, $\beta$, $h$ and $q$, with $\mathbb E_yA^{4k}\le C\sum_p\int|\mathrm{pairProd}|\,d\mu_g^{\otimes4k}$; as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg-tal:general-fluct} (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:amgm-pairprod} and Eq.~(1.201)) this is at most $C_2J_{2k}(g)$ with $J_{2k}(g)=\langle(R_{1,2}-q)^{2k}\rangle_g$.
\emph{The $B$ term.}  $\mathbb E_yB^{4k}\le e^{8k^2\beta^2}\le M:=e^{8k^2\beta_0^2}$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:thermal-factor-moment}, because $B^{4k}=\exp(4k\varepsilon\beta N^{-1/2}\sum y_i\langle\sigma_i\rangle)$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg-tal:ising-split}).
